1
Popular Science Writing: Problems
and Potential




In studying science communication, especially popular science writ-
ing, I have found that two cultures exist on either side of a divide.
I speak not of the two-cultures gap between science and literature that
C. P. Snow lamented in 1959, but of a gap within the ranks of those who
write about scientific issues for nonspecialist readers.
   On one side are the science boosters, advocates who see science com-
munication as public relations, with success measured in terms of how
well the public’s priorities and concerns align with those of scientists.
This side is represented by scientists, such as Alan Sokal, Norman Levitt,
and Paul R. Gross; historians of science, such as John C. Burnham; and
science writers, such as Richard Dawkins, Lars Lindberg Christensen,
and Timothy Ferris. Ferris offers a fine example of how these science
boosters view the purpose of science communication. First, he says
that ‘Science, though young, has already transformed our world, sav-
ing over a billion people from starvation and fatal disease, striking
shackles of ignorance and superstition from millions more, and fueling
a democratic revolution that has brought political liberty to a third of
humankind. And that’s only the beginning’ (‘Foreword’ in Blum et al.,
p. v). Then, having listed its accomplishments, he describes the public’s
alleged views of science:


  Yet few understand science, and many fear its awesome power. To
  the uncomprehending, the pronouncements of scientists can sound
  as opaque as the muttered spells of magicians…Technophobes warn
  that science must be stopped before it goes ‘too far.’ Religious funda-
  mentalists enjoin the righteous to study only one (holy) book, con-
  sulting what Galileo called the book of nature only insofar as it serves

                                    3
4 Communicating Popular Science


  to confirm their beliefs. Fashionable academics teach that science
  is but a collection of socially conditioned opinions, as changeable as
  haute couture. (p. v)

In other words, science is wonderful; the public are idiots for not recog-
nizing this; and anyone who questions any aspect of science is acting
from fear, fundamentalism, or (my personal favorite) the keen fashion
sense that apparently causes academics to follow hot intellectual trends
rather than thinking for themselves. Against this deplorable state, one
in which even popular culture is the enemy of science, Ferris offers the
work of science writers. Although they are ‘few in number, relatively
unheralded, and often underestimated’, they offer their ‘cure for fear
and loathing of science’ by telling ‘the most momentous, important,
and startlingly original stories’ available to any writer (p. vi).
  I might be tempted to dismiss Ferris’s worshipful view of science as
anomalous were it not for two things. First, the above quotation is
from Ferris’s foreword to A Field Guide for Science Writers: The Official
Guide of the National Association of Science Writers (second edition).
Although not all of the fifty-two pieces in the book take this view
of science, the foreword sets a tone that is echoed in many other
sections. Second, Ferris’s views are repeated in many places besides
A Field Guide, including in scholarly works. For example, in a recent
special issue of Science focused on science literacy, science education
specialist Paul Webb describes the exigence for increased science edu-
cation as ‘concern around the apparent inability of science education
to counter current negative perceptions of science in both developing and
industrial countries’ (p. 448, emphasis added). An older, but widely
cited, text, How Superstition Won and Science Lost, delivers the same
message that the goal of science communication is to promote sci-
ence, here characterized as ‘a way to truth, civilization, morality, and
other constructive values’ (Burnham, p. 261). These examples rep-
resent a belief that science communication exists to extol science’s
virtues to the ignorant and hostile masses arrayed against it.
  In contrast with the science boosters are the science critics, who, in
a role parallel to that of other professional critics (art critics and his-
torians, literary critics, and so on), combine appreciation for science
with the kind of critical analysis that characterizes good scholarship,
as well as good critique. On this side of the divide I found scientists,
such as Michael Gibbons and Robert Winston; historians of science,
such as Bernadette Bensaude-Vincent, Daniel Patrick Thurs, and
John M. Ziman; sociologists of science, such as Alan Irwin and Brian
                             Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 5


Wynne; philosophers of science, such as Don Ihde; and rhetoricians and
communication scholars, such as Peter Broks, Davida Charney, Jeanne
Fahnestock, Dorothy Nelkin, and Michael Zerbe (to name just a few).
In the following example from Winston—Emeritus Professor of Fertility
Studies and Professor of Science and Society—we see a call for critical
engagement implicit in his concern about ethics and applications and
in his plea for increased public involvement with science:

  … if we are to avoid harm from the increasingly powerful tools we
  have, we need to have much better methods of control. This control
  cannot be exercised solely by governments: history shows…that
  governments do not always use scientific knowledge wisely. Nor can
  control simply be left to my community—the scientists…[Instead]
  people from all sections of the community have a responsibility to
  learn and understand more about science in order that, in demo-
  cratic societies at least, they will have a more powerful say in how
  science is used. (pp. 10–11)

Winston’s aim is shared—that is, democratic—control, and he urges his
fellow scientists to improve their communication skills in order to foster
a healthy skepticism about science and its uses. ‘We should try to com-
municate our work as effectively as possible’, Winston writes, ‘because
ultimately it is done on behalf of society and because its adverse conse-
quences may affect members of the society in which we all live’ (p. 518).
   Winston is writing to scientists about their own communication prac-
tices, but the critical role he recommends also applies to popular science
texts by anyone. Although popular science writing, broadly defined, is
science-related writing that is aimed at nonspecialist audiences, science
boosters tend to see popular science writing as a form of public rela-
tions, while critics believe it should promote democratic engagement.
Proponents of the latter approach note that people in a technoscientific
society are asked constantly to make decisions affecting their day-to-day
lives, and to make informed judgments and weigh in on decisions about
technoscientific issues more broadly.
   On the individual level, for example, people wonder about ques-
tions like whether synthetic motor oil really makes a car get better gas
mileage; about which fruits carry less of a pesticide residue and can be
bought commercially, and which carry more and should be organic
whenever possible; about what kind of birth control has the least risky
long-term side effects; about what kinds of exercise maintain the best
cardiovascular fitness; and so on. As citizens, people are asked questions
6 Communicating Popular Science


such as whether we should continue to use nuclear power, and, if
so, where new power plants should be built and where waste should
go; what kinds of policies we should implement to combat climate
change; whether NASA should fund human space travel; whether we
should permit or ban offshore drilling for oil in certain areas; and so on.
  These examples demonstrate that both boosters and critics are cor-
rect in saying that science permeates our lives, and that nonspecialists
are routinely called upon to make decisions about science and science-
related issues. However, these examples also demonstrate the gulf
between the boosters and critics regarding the basis on which people
should decide. In the boosters’ minds, experts should not just inform,
but determine, policy in areas such as nuclear power, whereas critics
argue that the locus of decision-making is properly with a critically
engaged citizenry. These two points of view can be summed up in terms
of two models:

• boosters advocate for the deficit model, also known as the PAST
  (Public Appreciation of Science and Technology) model;
• critics argue for a model that fosters democratic engagement with sci-
  ence; adopting a term from historian Peter Broks, I call this the CUSP
  (Critical Understanding of Science in Public) model.


Social Contracts

The tension between PAST and CUSP approaches to popular science
writing reflects a broader tension about the nature of the relationship
between the scientific and public spheres. This relationship is often
discussed in terms of a tacit ‘social contract’ between the scientific and
social spheres, with different people supporting different versions of
it—the traditional contract that has been in place since the post-World
War II era, or a new social contract that takes into account the changing
social climate in which science takes place.
  The traditional social contract was articulated in a 1945 report by
presidential advisor and agency head Vannevar Bush. The report,
Science: The Endless Frontier, describes scientific knowledge as a reservoir
from which knowledge flows to society, a metaphor that encapsulates
the following beliefs about science:

• that all science, regardless of its aims, is inherently (if indirectly) ben-
  eficial to society because science produces an ever-growing store of
  knowledge, and from that store come applications that benefit society;
                             Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 7


• that separation of science from society is not only possible, but
  necessary for science to do its job well; as Byerly and Pielke observe,
  Bush’s metaphor depicts ‘a linear model of the relation between sci-
  ence and society in which social benefits occur “downstream” from
  the reservoir of knowledge’ (Byerly and Pielke, p. 1531).
• that society should provide science with resources and otherwise
  allow it autonomy, and in return science will provide society with
  knowledge and with consumer goods.

This social contract, formulated in the post-war climate of ‘extraordi-
nary faith in science as the basis of technological progress’ (Nelkin,
Selling Science, p. 117) has, in the more skeptical climate of recent
decades, ‘proved vulnerable to questions about the public value of the
science and technology it produced’ (Doubleday, p. 20). Science boost-
erism is one response to this increased skepticism, especially from those
who hold onto the Bush model view of science as separate from and
‘above’ society.
   The other response is to call for a new social contract, one based on
an understanding of science as a cultural institution (or, rather, set of
institutions) that must be accountable to the larger society of which it
is a part. This is a ‘socially contextualized science’ (Trench, ‘Towards,’
p. 126) in which the internal validity of the science is no less important
than in the old model, but is subsumed within the larger context in
which the scientific work takes place and the scientific knowledge is
used. This emerging social contract is based on the principle of socially
robust knowledge, the production of which is ‘both transparent and
participative’ (Gibbons, p. C81), and on a belief in public engagement
with scientific issues, that is a belief in CUSP.


Why Popular Science Writing Matters

Popular science writing matters in these and other areas of public inter-
est because of the discursive work it does. Kamler and Thomson define
a discourse as ‘a particular formation of stories and practices, which
constructs both knowledge and power relations’ (p. 11); applying this
to science popularization, it is clear that they do both.
   In terms of information, popular science writing, by definition,
tells readers about scientific findings. For example, Trevor Corson’s
‘Stalking the American Lobster’ informs readers about the life cycles
and reproductive habits of Maine lobsters. Popular science writing also
informs readers about the nature and working of sciences themselves.
8   Communicating Popular Science


Corson’s article, in addition to sharing lobster-related facts, also talks
about the advantages and drawbacks of field-based versus laboratory-
based research methods, and about how a scientist’s choice of tools
will influence what he or she learns (points I return to in Chapters 5
and 8). Additionally, popular science writing can help readers—scientists,
nonscientists, and scientists working outside their areas of expertise—
understand how a given area of research affects and is affected by other
social institutions. In Corson’s case, the expertise of the Maine lobster-
men turned out to be essential to developing a more accurate under-
standing of changes in the lobster populations. Here, the text functions
discursively by providing readers with a story about the nature of
knowledge production in a particular area of research.
   A discourse also, in Kamler and Thomson’s words, influences ‘how we
talk about an object of knowledge, and it influences how ideas are put
into practice’ (p. 11). Popular science writing does so by contributing to
the store of meanings that people draw upon in talking about science
and science-related issues. Although we have many ways of experienc-
ing the world—cognitively, emotionally, physically—our shared under-
standings of the world are created through language. Texts are part of
this creation, as they represent the world—socially, as well as materially—
and so provide us with schema for understanding situations and phe-
nomena we encounter in our lives. For popular science texts, this means
they provide stories about how science works and should work, of what
science’s relationship with society is or should be, of whose opinions
about science are valid and whose aren’t, and so on. These then become
available as ways of talking about and understanding science-related
issues; when those understandings translate into action, they become
solidified in social practices.
   Given the discursive power of popular science texts, communication
scholars and rhetoricians agree that popular science writing can and
should contribute to civic engagement. This belief is summed up by
rhetorician Alan Gross’s hope that science popularization will aid ‘the
appropriate integration of science into the general culture’ (‘Science and
Culture’, p. 170). Rhetorician Greg Myers furthers this vision, suggest-
ing that popular science writing can help us understand how science is
always ‘a part of public culture, drawing on and contributing to ideas
about nature, the place of humans in nature, the direction of history,
the nature of government and economy’ (‘Discourse Studies’, p. 271).
Al Gore’s Nobel-winning documentary, An Inconvenient Truth, illustrates
how this kind of communication can bring together the social, politi-
cal, and scientific, all of which blur together when we look at scientific
                             Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 9


issues not in and of themselves but in the context of the public spheres
that make up civil society. As Hauser explains, public life involves
interactions with others, and in those interactions many factors ‘influ-
ence and are influenced by’ each other, with these reciprocal influences
including other people, ‘the climate for communication…the resources
of language available to us…the situation in which we communicate
and the impulse for communication it contains, and…the myriad of
conditioning factors that mark our human existence’ (‘Aristotle on
Epideictic’, p. 14). As one provider of such linguistic resources, popular
science writing can have a profound, albeit indirect, effect on conversa-
tions about science in society.
   Unfortunately, hopes for critical popular science writing are more
honored in the breach than in the observance. Although popular sci-
ence writing spans a spectrum from science boosterism to insightful
critique of scientific issues, the emphasis is still at the booster end of
the continuum. Sociologist Dorothy Nelkin explains that ‘Science often
appears in the press today as an arcane and incomprehensible subject…
And scientists still appear to be remote but superior wizards, above
ordinary people, culturally isolated from the society’ (Selling, p. 15).
Although this shows most clearly in mass media, she says ‘the mys-
tique of science as a superior culture is also conveyed in the promotion
of science literacy, in the coverage of scientific theories, and even in
stories about scientific fraud’ (Selling, p. 15). Nelkin wrote this gloomy
overview in 1987, but communications research (e.g., Bauer and Bucchi,
‘Introduction’; Huntington; Cheng et al.; Bucchi; Russell, N. J.) suggests
that not much has changed, except perhaps to have grown worse.
Bauer describes how the phenomenon of science communication as
‘science public relations’ has actually ‘gained a critical mass’ as Nelkin
first pointed out the problem (‘Selling’, p. 11). Other research shows ‘a
general trend to represent science as consensual, linear, and uncontro-
versial’ (Bauer and Bucchi, ‘Introduction’, p. 3), and documents ‘the
shifting power balance between public relations and journalism’ (Bauer
and Bucchi, ‘Introduction’, p. 6), with the publicity approach gaining
ground over the last two decades.


How Rhetoric of Science Can Help Improve Popular
Science Writing

Rhetoric of science can help improve popular science writing by pro-
viding an analytic and heuristic framework for understanding popular
science texts’ PAST and CUSP functions. This work has begun in existing
10 Communicating Popular Science


studies of popular science writing that talk about what science populari-
zation does and could do; however, these works don’t specifically explore
how popular science texts can better promote CUSP. Even scholars who
pin their hope on science communication express concern about how
to overcome the gap between its potential and what it actually does.
‘Science communication’, Holliman et al. say, is ‘at a crossroads…but
frustratingly in need of a map’ (‘Investigating’, p. xviii). To help resolve
the discrepancy between goal and reality—that is to provide a map—I
turn to rhetoric, understood as ‘the study of misunderstanding and its
remedies’ (Richards and Constable, p. 3) to understand not only what
popular science writing does and why, but also how it can do something
more. My focus on offering strategies for improvement places this book
within what Condit et al. call the ‘meliorative’ tradition of rhetoric of
science; in their terms, the goal is to be both descriptive and critical, but
critical in a way that goes beyond identifying problems to ‘offer alterna-
tive options’ (p. 390) and ‘to specify rhetorical approaches that might be
useful…for public communication about science’ (p. 391).
   From within the broad scope of rhetorical theory, I chose to use a
genre-based approach. Because ‘a genre embodies an aspect of cultural
rationality’ (Miller, quoted in Bawarshi and Reiff, p. 72), understand-
ing a genre means drawing connections between rhetorical strategies
and the social norms and goals they reflect and further. At the same
time, genres are not static. Like other sets of social norms, discourses
and the genres that encode them are flexible, changing over time.
Therefore, genres have a role in both stabilizing and changing the
social systems and value sets of which they are both reflections and rec-
reations. Rhetorical genre theory therefore also functions heuristically,
rhetoricians Anis Bawarshi and Mary Jo Reiff say ‘by revealing the rela-
tionship between text structures and social purposes in ways that enable
[writers] to produce texts more effectively and critically’ (p. 32).
   To understand this view of genre and discourse it is helpful to think
of a genre as analogous to a physical place. When people enter a genre
place they ‘take on the mood, attitude, and actional possibilities of that
place—they go to that place to do the kinds of things you do there,
think the kinds of thoughts you think there, be the kind of person you
become there’ (Bazerman, ‘Genre and Identity’, p. 13). For example,
people go to a bar to relax, hang out with friends, maybe complain
about their jobs, maybe laugh at stupid jokes. They don’t go to bars to
read rhetorical theory or to work on scholarly monographs. At the same
time, by using a space, people have the potential to change it. If people
conform strictly to the place’s existing norms, there’s no change. If they
                            Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 11


do things a bit differently, though, this changes the understanding—
tacit or otherwise—of what one can or should do and say in that place.
As a case in point, I was once invited to join a knitting and crocheting
group that met in a bar. The participants were adhering to some of the
social norms of going to a bar—they were there to drink and to socialize—
but were also altering those social norms by introducing a new set of
activities not usually associated with bars.
   Rhetorical genre theory, one of several current approaches to genre
study, elucidates the rhetorical purposes of genres—the kinds of topics
one discusses and stances one can take in a given genre—by looking at
how discourses are ‘enacted as genres’ (Fairclough, p. 208). For example,
consider the central text in mental health treatment, the Diagnostic and
Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM). In A Psychiatrist Using
DSM-III: The Influence of a Charter Document in Psychiatry, McCarthy
looks at how the text ‘shapes reality’ for a psychiatrist by shaping her
understanding of and communication about mental illness (p. 359).
McCarthy explains that the DSM-III shapes the psychiatrist’s diagnostic
work, including what she asks and how she interprets the information
she receives, and that the text therefore ‘defines as authoritative certain
ways of seeing and deflects attention from other ways. It thus stabilizes
a particular reality and sets the terms for future discussions’ (p. 359).
McCarthy’s work illustrates how viewing a text in terms of genre lets
us ask what it does, what cultural role it plays, what ideologies it
embodies. Indeed, genre scholars agree that ‘communicative purpose’
is the most salient feature of any genre (Bawarshi and Reiff, p. 44) and
that a genre-based analysis is useful for focusing on how texts relate to
social situations.


Models of Science Popularization

Social purposes for popular science writing fall on a continuum
from science boosterism to science criticism, from PAST to CUSP. To
understand the range of this continuum, I look at three different
models of science communication (PAST and CUSP and an in-between
model) and at how each frames the relationships between scientific
and public spheres. These models, although presented separately, often
overlap and blur together in texts, and any given popular science text
may actually contain instances of all three models; thus, the goal in
describing each is not to create a taxonomy for labeling texts, but rather
to introduce some of the characteristics that exist at various points on
the continuum.
12 Communicating Popular Science


Deficit: PAST
The PAST1 model is characterized as a one-way flow of information from
the scientific sphere to the public, with that public viewed as a ‘blank
slate of ignorance on which scientists write knowledge’ (Myers, p. 266).
Underpinning the deficit view of the public is the idea of a sharp divide
between two cultures: ‘that of the scientist, who holds the monopoly
of true, valid statements, and that of the rest…the public’ (Bensaude-
Vincent, p. 106). In addition to being insulting to everyone but scientist
experts, the deficit model is problematic for a variety of reasons.
   First, it tends to take an uncomplicated view of science as a black box,
a sort of fact-making factory into which go resources, pieces of the natu-
ral world, and out of which comes truth. As I discuss in Chapter 2, this
uncomplicated view of science promotes a ‘positivist view of the inexo-
rably rational and progressive nature of scientific knowledge’ (Taylor,
p. 115). Such a view, while useful when talking about well-established
scientific facts, does not take into account how science functions as a
social institution, does not account for the uncertainty of new findings,
and does not consider the fact that even established science exists and
is put to use in complex social, cultural, political, and personal contexts.
   Second, it takes an uncomplicated view reading roles by assuming
that a given person either is or is not a scientist, ignoring the fact that
scientists, when reading outside of their own specialties, are no more
expert than any other reader.2
   Third, it assumes that the deficit exists only on one side of the false
divide, that of nonscientist readers. In this conception, science has
much to offer the public, but nothing to learn from or about it, and no
need to reflect on its own processes.
   Fourth, it is based on ‘a pervasive but outdated and overly simplistic
model of communication as a simple linear process’ (Hansen, p. 107)
in which scientists produce knowledge, the knowledge is packaged in
language, and the packages are opened by the public and the knowledge
in them absorbed in its original form (Figure 1.1).




                                                    Civil
                        Science
                                                   society



Figure 1.1   Science sends knowledge to civil society
                             Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 13


   There is no room here for an involved public or for interactions
between different segments of society. In short, the deficit model
reflects and promotes science boosterism. Its goal is to improve a defec-
tive public, and success in that mission is defined in terms of how well
the public absorbs science’s messages and agrees with the scientific
point of view.
   Fifth, it doesn’t work. Science journalism professor Robert A. Logan
reports that even after ‘a generation of sophisticated efforts to work
with the news media to boost awareness, interest, and education about
science’, there were no increases in any of the measured goals (Logan,
p. 150). Logan suggests that the model itself may be to blame, as com-
munications in this model could have been perceived as condescending
rather than informative or helpful.
   Sixth, and last, it is not necessary. Contrary to claims from some scien-
tists and science writers that Americans have lost faith in science and no
longer want to support it, research shows that, in reality, there is no lack
of public interest, respect, and trust when it comes to science generally.
   Figures from the National Science Board’s (NSB) 2012 Science and
Engineering Indicators demonstrate this generally high regard. First, they
show high interest in science, with ‘41% reporting they were “very
interested” and 50% reporting they were “moderately interested” in new
scientific discoveries’ (p. 7–4). In other words, 91 per cent of respond-
ents say that are at least moderately interested in science. In addition,
‘Americans in all demographic groups consistently endorse the past
achievements and future promise of S&T [Science and Technology]’
(p. 7–4), a confidence that extends to scientists themselves, as a ‘major-
ity of Americans accord scientists “very great” prestige’ (p. 7–5), more
than they do to any other group except firefighters.
   The positive regard for science and scientists extends to funding
preferences, as ‘Support for government funding of scientific research
remains strong’ (p. 7–4), with 82 per cent of respondents ‘express[ing]
support for government funding of basic research’ (p. 7–4), and also
to a belief that scientists should exercise leadership on science-related
issues. Confidence in scientists as leaders extends even to conten-
tious issues such as climate change, stem cells, nuclear power, and
genetically-modified organisms; on these issues ‘Americans regard sci-
ence and engineering leaders as both knowledgeable and impartial—
relative to other leaders—and believe they should be influential in
decisions about these topics’ (p. 7–4). The NSF found that when people
do speak out against something, it is not ‘science’ as a generalized phe-
nomenon, but a particular line of research or a particular application,
14 Communicating Popular Science


something the PAST model is ill-equipped to handle given its confi-
dence in science as an engine of progress.

Dialogue: Public Engagement with Science and Technology
The second model—known as the dialogue model or the Public
Engagement with Science and Technology (PEST) model—addresses
some of the shortcomings of the deficit model by conceiving of science
popularization in terms of a conversation. Although I don’t use the dia-
logue model in this book, I talk about it because it is sometimes offered
as an alternative to the deficit model, and I feel that I should explain
what it is and why I prefer the CUSP model.
   A 2006 article in the Chronicle of Higher Education by Alan I. Leshner,
then Chief Executive Officer of the American Academy for the
Advancement of Science, describes the deficit-to-dialogue shift from
the perspective of a science organization: ‘Instead of simply increasing
public understanding of science, scientists need to have a real dialogue
with members of the public, listening to their concerns, their priorities,
and the questions they would like us to help answer’ (p. B20). Similarly,
Logan describes what he calls the interactive science tradition, which
‘conceives mass communication as more of an informal conversation—
a shared and multidirectional experience. The emphasis is less on
informing persons than on improving communication among citizens,
scientists, politicians, government and corporate officials, and journal-
ists’ (Logan, p. 153).
   Although the PEST model is an improvement on the deficit model of
science communication, it also has problems.
   First, the PEST model still separates science and society, even as it tries
to bring them together in conversation with each other (Figure 1.2).
   To say science and society should talk is sort of like suggesting I have a
dialogue with my spleen—or like trying to treat a troubled spleen with-
out accounting for its function in the body as a whole, or the effects
of other body systems on the spleen. Although such abstractions can
be useful, for example when a medical student is first learning about




                                                    Civil
                        Science
                                                   society



Figure 1.2   Science and civil society exchange ideas
                            Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 15


the spleen, that usefulness is extremely limited. Likewise, a model that
considers science and society as different domains fails to account for
how science is as much a part of society as the spleen is part of the
human body.
   Second, the PEST model still keeps the center of gravity in science.
Discussions about science and society often assume that the problem
lies on the ‘society’ side of the divide; science is seen as a fixed entity
toward which the public should move. The PEST model, while improv-
ing on the deficit model by casting members of the public as active
agents who are empowered in their interactions with science, still
emphasizes public change. It does not take into account the ways that
science, or the interplay between spheres, might also change.
   By taking science as a mostly uncomplicated institution, its pro-
ponents often echo the deficit model view of science as a black box
by suggesting that public concerns about science are caused, not by
problems with science, but by public misperceptions of science. For
example, Leshner suggests that efforts toward public engagement
‘can help assuage public fears about new areas of research’ such as the
Human Genome Project, ignoring the possibility that those fears are
based on legitimate concerns. Here we see what science communication
scholar Nicholas Russell refers to as ‘a democratic deficit at the heart of
the PEST agenda’ (p. 90), and what Broks says can happen when ‘the
new language of dialogue masks the old objective of public relations’
(‘Understanding’, p. 126).

Democracy: CUSP
The CUSP model resolves the PEST model’s lingering deficit model
characteristics by suggesting a kind of science communication that
considers all the elements of science-in-society, including their interac-
tions, to be worth scrutinizing. Historian Peter Broks, originator of the
CUSP model, describes it as ‘a third generation model that proposes that
popular science be thought of in terms of conceptual spaces that are
opened up and closed down’ (‘Dr Peter Broks’). This model recognizes
that science communication is multidimensional and contextual, and
it focuses on the meaning-making work that popular science texts do,
rather than on how accurately they communicate facts. The term ‘also
captures the sense of in between, not one or the other but both, at the
edge, an interface, and interaction, a starting point, a meeting point’
(Broks, ‘Understanding’, p. 134).
   The CUSP model offers four advantages over the PAST and PEST
models.
16 Communicating Popular Science




                                     Civil society




                                       Science




Figure 1.3   Science is an interactive part of civil society


   First, it has a ‘relational focus’ (Irwin and Wynne, ‘Introduction’,
p. 7). Unlike approaches with a science-centered focus, CUSP’s rela-
tional focus highlights ways that specific scientific and social spheres
interact with and affect each other (Figure 1.3). It acknowledges that
science is not separate from other social institutions and foregrounds
the relevant connections.
   Second, it sees expertise as a multiple, rather than a unitary, con-
struct. Both scientifically- and nonscientifically-generated forms of
knowledge have value, and ‘communication about science takes place
between diverse groups on the basis that all can contribute, and that
all have a stake in the outcome of the deliberations and discussions’
(Trench, ‘Towards’, p. 132).
   Third, it focuses on both ‘twin duties’ of science communication:
‘To inform and educate the public about science on the one hand, but
also to probe and criticize it on the other’ (Russell, N. J., p. xiii). In the
deficit and PEST models, the critical role is often elided. In contrast,
CUSP suggests a view of technoscientific literacy similar to that pro-
posed by philosopher and physicist Gerard Fourez who says ‘People
are “scientifically and technically literate” when their knowledge gives
them…a certain capacity to communicate…and some practical ways of
coping with specific situations and negotiating over outcomes’ (p. 906,
emphasis original). Critique is not seen as a substitute for information,
but a complement, as knowledge matters but so does the ability to act
on that knowledge.
   Fourth, CUSP fits the reality of the public’s views of science, which is
a combination of ‘general public enthusiasm for the conduct and prod-
ucts of science’ and ‘public criticism of specific forms and applications
                             Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential 17


of scientific research that are perceived to undermine the quality of
human (and often non-human) life’ (Fuller, p. 289).
  By aligning itself with public perception rather than trying to amend
that perception in some didactic way, popular science writing can
praise science when praise is called for, challenge it when challenges
are needed, and explain it in terms that situate it in its social, cultural,
and material context. Ultimately, all of this matters because science is
a god term—perhaps the god term—of our time, and god terms must
be subject to critical scrutiny if their rhetorical power is to be kept in
proportion to the benefits they actually offer.
2
Theoretical and Analytical
Framework




The previous chapter talked about the importance of promoting civic
engagement with science-related issues and described different perspec-
tives on what popular science writing should do. These perspectives
are rooted in different understandings of what science is, how it works,
and what its relationships with society should be like. This chapter
expands on those differences, explaining their social and philosophical
underpinnings, moving from the general to the specific. It begins by
talking about philosophy of science;1 creating socially robust knowledge
requires an understanding of science that accounts for its connections
to and roles in society, an understanding provided by a social construc-
tionist view of science that Bauer et al. have dubbed ‘Realist–Skepticism’
(Bauer et al., ‘Public’). Next it discusses rhetorical genre theory as a way
to understand texts as discursive objects, then it describes the specific
analytic framework I use to identify relevant characteristics of popular
science writing in specific texts. Finally, it explains my text selections.


Philosophical Foundation: Traditional–Idealism Versus
Realist–Skepticism

Different stances toward science communication are based on differ-
ent understandings of what science is and how it works. Therefore,
in talking about science communication it is helpful to identify what
constructs of ‘science’ are being used. Science and Technology Studies
scholars have identified two general constructs of science. While ter-
minology varies between (and even within) different science studies
disciplines, I find it useful to refer to the two constructs as ‘Traditional–
Idealist’ and ‘Realist–Skeptical’. Both terms come from Bauer et al. who
describe Traditional–Idealism as a view in which science is ‘the rational

                                     18
                                      Theoretical and Analytical Framework 19


pursuit of objective “truth” about the world, which can only be good’
(Bauer et al., ‘Public’, p. 42). In contrast, Realist–Skepticism tends to
be aware of how science functions—including the international and
highly team-oriented nature of most science—as well as the constraints
and contingencies that affect knowledge production in science (Bauer
et al., p. 44). The following sections explore these two constructs.

Traditional–Idealism
The Traditional–Idealist construct of science, which Ziman refers to as
‘the Legend’, is characterized by realism, rationalism, and objectivism.
   Realism is the belief that science has access to ‘a single, fixed, deter-
minable Truth’ (Branscomb, quoted in Anson, p. 23). This idea is based
on a Platonic notion of truth as abstract, enduring, unchanging, and
findable. It not only says that objective truths about the natural world
exist (which I agree they do, as discussed below), but also that science
can discover them. That is, realism is characterized by the ‘belief that
good data give direct access to the truth of the material situation’ (Allen
et al., p. 476). In the history of science, we see this kind of positivist
confidence in a form of ‘historical whiggishness, which finds in scien-
tific papers the march toward rational truth’ (Bazerman, Shaping, p. 156)
as opposed to a process of trial and error with false starts, dead ends,
certainties later found to be uncertain again, and certainties later found
to be certain for different reasons than were originally given.
   Rationalism, also referred to as instrumental rationality, favors
‘scientific-instrumental reason’ (Knorr-Cetina, p. 247) to the exclusion
or detriment of other ways of reasoning. Thus, in seeking rationalism,
‘the logical positivists erred in restricting scientific rationality to rea-
soning based on formally logical criteria’ (Prelli, p. 2) while the instru-
mentalist ‘equates rationality with means/ends calculation’ (Carlton,
p. 57). Rationalism holds up universal, mathematical claims as ideal
knowledge, and, in doing so, devalues kinds of knowledge that can-
not be quantified (even scientific knowledge). It also devalues the par-
ticulars that are important for applying scientific generalities to specific
situations, as when the typical course of a disease is altered by unusual
physiological or anatomical aspects of a patient’s body.
   Objectivism is, in Ziman’s words, ‘the central dogma of the Legend…
the doctrine that scientific knowledge is, by its very nature, perfectly
objective, and that if it were not objective it would not be perfectly
scientific’ (p. 176).
   A realist, rationalist, objectivist view of science can lead to scientism,
a belief in scientific knowledge that ignores the limits of that knowledge
20 Communicating Popular Science


and attempts to apply it in areas where its relevance is dubious, at best.
The ‘principal tenet [of scientism] is that science is producing a com-
plete, comprehensive, “scientific world picture”, which will constitute
the ultimate “reality”’ (Ziman, p. 321). For example, scientism can be
seen in what philosopher of science Zammito calls ‘new biological
reductionism’ in claims by various sciences (such as sociobiology and
evolutionary psychology) to be able to provide a scientific explanation
for the nature of human existence (p. 276).
   In addition, seeing science as the final arbiter of knowledge can lead
to seeing scientific expertise as superior in all decision-making, a tech-
nocratic point of view that says ‘collective decision making in society
is better left to experts, as they are the only ones who have mastered
rational methods for problem solving’ (Kolstø, p. 297). For example,
mathematician Norman Levitt admits that democracy requires public
decision-making, but argues that ‘If there are any irreconcilable con-
flicts between the democratic ethos and the ability of science to pursue
and promulgate knowledge, and…have that knowledge respected, then
my first instinct is to say that it is democracy, rather than the scientific
culture, that ought to be modified’ (quoted in Truscello, p. 349). This
calls to mind Lessl’s comment that ‘rather than conceiving of public
science as the popularization of technical knowledge, we might better
conceive of it as the scientization of the public consciousness’ with
the goal being ‘to make people suitable for science’ (quoted in Taylor,
p. 129)—except, in this case, it’s not just people, but democracy itself,
that supposedly needs to be revised.
   Decades of social scientific and philosophical work demonstrate the
gaping chasm between this ideal of science—Ziman’s ‘Legend’—and
the reality of scientific practices. Its failure to accurately describe the
scientific enterprise and its institutions makes Traditional–Idealism an
unworkable basis for theorizing about science or about science communi-
cation. A more accurate and more useful construct is Realist–Skepticism.

Realist–Skepticism
Realist–Skepticism combines a belief that objective reality exists inde-
pendently of human awareness with skepticism toward knowledge
about that reality. That is, a Realist–Skeptical point of view acknowl-
edges the existence of objective reality, but does not assume that any
knowledge-making practice, scientific or otherwise, has access to that
reality except in indirect ways that are mediated by human senses,
human cognition, and the technological affordances of our tools and of
language. In contrast with ‘the positivist view of the inexorably rational
                                     Theoretical and Analytical Framework 21


and progressive nature of scientific knowledge’ (Taylor, p. 115), Realist–
Skepticism takes a social constructionist position that our understand-
ing of nature is created via knowledge-making activities, and that no
method can ‘guarantee total freedom from the influence of subjective
factors’ (Bowler, p. 13).
   Even as seemingly neutral an activity as gathering data is affected by
the theories guiding the data selection and the tools used in collecting
it. For example, the rise of quantitative measurement systems in the
eighteenth and nineteenth centuries altered what people looked at in
science by encouraging practitioners to focus on problems that were
amenable to these methods; historian Lorraine Daston describes this as
‘the contraction of nature to the communicable’ (quoted in Atkinson
and Royal Society, p. 156). Similarly, people often talk about evidence
as neutral, but technical communication scholar Dorothy Winsor uses
Latour’s notion of ‘inscriptions’ to point out that what scientists present
to one another as evidence is never nature itself; rather, they rely on ‘the
conversion of physical reality into inscriptions’, such as ‘written data,
charts, tables, and so on’ (p. 128). Although this inscribed evidence is
based on nature, it ‘is not, however, nature itself; rather it is a humanly
produced and ordered representation of it. Anyone who has ever looked
for, selected, and arrayed pieces of a larger reality knows how much
shaping has happened before such a figure is produced’ (Winsor, p. 134).
   Of course, this process of shaping is necessary to science, and nature
does provide ‘constraints’ on scientists that ‘shape and restrain their
theories’ (Winsor, p. 134). However, Winsor warns, it is important to
also acknowledge the role of interpretation to avoid the fallacy that
evidence from nature is objective, unaffected by human agency. As
Winsor says, ‘even perceptions of nature, which one would assume to
precede the writing of an article, are amenable to shaping in the schol-
arly discussion of scientists’ (p. 135). Even the language that scientists
use often reflects a particular worldview set in disciplinary assumptions,
and, ultimately, she says, nature ‘is not something in front of which
scientists passively sit. It is material whose shape they work together
to construct’ (p. 137). Another way to understand this is in terms of
philosopher Ian Hacking’s useful distinction between an object and the
idea of the object; a riparian area is not socially constructed—the water,
trees, plants, animals, insects, fungi, and nonliving elements that make
up a riparian area are, objectively, indubitably real whether or not peo-
ple are aware of them. The idea of a riparian area, however, is socially
constructed through and through, from its definition to its significance
to how it is legislated and by whom.
22 Communicating Popular Science


   Haraway neatly sums up the idea that science is affected by its
context with her statement that ‘Facts are theory-laden; theories are
value-laden; values are history-laden’ (p. 77), a point she illustrates
by describing how studies in primatology changed between the early
twentieth century and the 1980s. Haraway explains that primatol-
ogy has traditionally been seen as a way to understand what human
nature is like when not constrained by culture; thus, findings in
primatology are used to explain and understand the essential traits
and characteristics of human nature and society. Once a trait or char-
acteristic has been labeled as ‘natural’, it is no longer susceptible to
criticism as a social–cultural construct. At the same time, Haraway
explains, assumptions about what is natural have affected what prima-
tologists observe. This cultural–scientific reciprocity means that early
primatologists approached their studies with certain preconceptions
about social structures, specifically that society is held together by
male dominance and hierarchies. They found what they were look-
ing for—hierarchies based on systems of male aggression—and these
findings were then used to explain and naturalize hierarchies and
male dominance in human society. Later, after the shift in cultural
consciousness caused, in part, by feminism, primatologists began to
see other aspects of social order among primates, such as the impor-
tance of female–female bonds and the organization of groups around
infant care.
   Ethologist Frans de Waal makes a similar point in comparing prima-
tology in Europe and the USA with primatology in Japan. Evolution,
he points out, ‘was never controversial in the East: it was a logical
and welcome thought’, and led to advances in primatology that were
impossible for the European and American scientists who were so busy
avoiding being anthropomorphic that they instead erred into mech-
anomorphism and missed what was obvious to scientists in Japan—
that other primates share many characteristics with humans. Thus, he
writes, ‘In 1952, when European ethologists still worked on instinct
theories and American behaviorists still trained rats to press levers,
[Japanese researcher] Imanishi wrote a little book that criticized the
view of animals as mindless automatons’. Indeed, ‘Until well into the
1960s, the Western view was positively Rousseauian: apes were autono-
mous “noble savages,” free of social ties and obligations’. As a result of
this view, American scientists overlooked the social relations among
chimpanzees, for example. Meanwhile, another Japanese research team
‘worked under quite different assumptions’, including the idea that
                                     Theoretical and Analytical Framework 23


‘a species that supposedly fills the gap between ourselves and other
animals’ would have ‘a community life, like us’ and, over time, was
able to show ‘that chimpanzees live in large communities with a stable
membership’.
   What Haraway and de Waal describe are, in Ziman’s parlance,
assumptions embedded within research traditions as ‘“domain assump-
tions” which were built deeply into their [scientists’] minds during their
research apprenticeship’ (Ziman, p. 196). Examples in ethology—a field
with implications for how we understand ourselves as humans—are
easy to see, but domain assumptions also affect research in physical
sciences like ecology. In ‘Ecological Theories as Cultural Narratives: F.E.
Clements’s and H.A. Gleason’s “Stories” of Community Succession’,
rhetorician Debra Journet explains that scientists use narrative to
understand and explain physical phenomena, and that these narratives
are affected by assumptions in the larger culture of which science is a
part. She illustrates these points by analyzing the work of two early-
twentieth-century ecologists, showing a correspondence between each
scientist’s view of ecological succession (the change from one ecological
community to another) with broader cultural assumptions about how
changes take place. One worldview (the Victorian) is more teleologial
and coherent, while the other (the modernist) is more fragmented
and contingent. This leads her to conclude that scientists ‘are, in part,
constructing and testing plausible narratives’ and that ‘the structures
of these scientific narratives resemble structures of other cultural
narratives…in reflecting, at least to some extent, cultural assumptions
and values’ (p. 447).
   A Realist–Skeptical view of science helps make these tacit assumptions
more visible, not only in historical studies such as Journet’s, but in the
present as well. For example, David Keller and Frank Golley’s Philosophy
of Ecology: From Science to Synthesis describes the different mindsets
informing ecological research today: autoecology, which ‘focuses on the
analysis of the components of ecological entities’ and is exemplified by
systems modelers (as in population ecology), and synecology, which
‘focuses on the relationships between entities in an ecological system,
rather than on the entities themselves’ and is exemplified by field
naturalists (as in ecosystem ecology) (p. 11). Knowing about these dif-
ferent stances can help readers understand the background assumptions
affecting the design and findings of ecological studies. This understand-
ing, in turn, helps us avoid expecting science to always produce rational
and consistent findings.
24 Communicating Popular Science


Cold Versus Hot Science
The social constructionist position that creating scientific knowledge
is not a purely rational, objective march toward an ever-better under-
standing of reality is sometimes caricaturized as a belief that science
is an arbitrary collection of opinions or claims, a position dubbed
radical relativism.2 In fact, this is not the case; as the ‘realism’ part of
Realist–Skepticism suggests, material reality is part of the picture. What
the social constructionist part of Realist–Skepticism does is allow us to
avoid the ‘positivist endeavor’ that ‘casts the success of science in terms
of universal logic and scientific methods’ while still realizing that the
various sciences have, over the years, ‘resulted in considerably reliable
knowledge claims’ (Ford, pp. 406–7).
   One way to avoid confusion about how knowledge can be both
socially constructed and reliable is to distinguish between ‘cold’ and
‘hot’ science. Cold science, also known as ‘textbook’ science, includes
those facts and methods that are well established and are accepted
by the majority of scientists in a given area. Although questions may
remain about the application of this science, its factual basis is strong.
The longer the knowledge holds up, and the more tests is it subjected to,
the more reliable it is considered to be, to the point that we can safely
rely on it as ‘cold science’. In contrast to cold science, ‘hot’ science, also
called ‘frontier science’, includes those aspects of scientific knowledge
that are still uncertain and still not the subject of a consensus. Media
scholars Jane Gregory and Steve Miller describe hot science as ‘science-
in-the-making’ and point out that when ‘science is in-the-making, the
“right answer” is unavailable to everyone, including the scientists’
(p. 243, original emphasis). When PAST (Public Appreciation of Science
and Technology)-oriented popular science texts attach the confidence
of cold science to the much less established findings of hot science, they
create problematic discrepancies between how science is represented
and how it actually works, and they create expectations that are bound
to be disappointed.



Realist–Skepticism and Science Communication

The shift from Traditional–Idealism to Realist–Skepticism outlined
above is echoed by a shift in science communication studies from the
deficit to the CUSP (Critical Understanding of Science in Public) model.
   In the early scholarly studies of science education and populariza-
tion, consistent with a Traditional–Idealist construct of science, increas-
ing public understanding of science was ‘conceived as an educational
                                      Theoretical and Analytical Framework 25


challenge (the aim is to explain science) with a propagandist pay-off
(successful popularizing will lead to increased public support for sci-
ence)’ (Mellor, p. 510, original emphasis). It was rooted in a deficit
view of ‘a perceived divide between a knowledgeable group of scientific
experts and an ignorant public, poorly trained in the facts and mini-
mally interested in the effects of science and technology’ (van Dijck,
p. 178); research on science communication took a descriptive approach
focused on measuring what the public knew, and teachers and science
writers were expected ‘to fix the knowledge deficit’ (van Dijck, p. 181).
In the 1990s, this deficit model evolved into a contextualist model that
included factors such as the role of local knowledge and that acknowl-
edged the ‘social, emotional, and political aspects of science’ (van Dijck,
p. 181). More recently, conversations about science popularization have
shifted to conceptualizing goals not in terms of public understanding
or literacy, both of which suggest a deficit in the public, but in terms of
engagement around science in public, the CUSP movement described
in the introduction.
   CUSP, being based in a Realist–Skeptical view of science, moves past
narrow rationalism to encompass broader standards of agreement and
to ask not about rationality but about reasonableness. In A Rhetoric of
Science: Inventing Scientific Discourse, Lawrence Prelli explains the distinc-
tion: ‘The rational is valid only in an abstract theoretical domain and
is characterized by rigid adherence to a priori standards. The reason-
able, in contrast, is concerned with making judgments and engaging
in actions that are commensurate with the dictates of common sense’
(p. 28). The rational–reasonable distinction is, in rhetorical terms, the
difference between episteme and doxa, where ‘episteme’ describes
specialist knowledge (including scientific knowledge and other forms
of technical expertise), and ‘doxa’ refers to public knowledge that is
produced by ‘the “civic epistemologies” that civil society organizations
and individual citizens construct’ (Einsiedel, p. 175). In these terms,
the shift in science communication goals can be understood as a shift-
ing valuation of episteme and doxa. The early, deficit-based movement
took a hierarchical view, in which episteme is superior to, and should
be given charge of guiding, doxa. In other words, it was technocratic.
In contrast, the CUSP-based approach to science communication offers
a view of episteme from the point of view of doxa and seeks to facilitate
conversations that value both.
   Doxa is the product of civil society, not of a particular community.
As Hauser explains, the ‘tradition of civil society’ is based not in the
idea of ‘community, which values common beliefs and shared social
practices’, but, rather, ‘is concerned with relationships among diverse
26 Communicating Popular Science


groups and interests’ (Vernacular Voices, pp. 21–2). In other words, doxa
has to do with the ways that different constituencies work out meanings
and priorities via public discussion. Coming back to the PAST and CUSP
models, the former is based on a belief that society in general should
adopt the values and methods of the scientific community—or at least
a particular concept of those values and methods. In contrast, CUSP
recognizes that there is not one public, but many publics that make up
civil society, and it therefore recognizes that doxa is formed through
participatory discourse. The challenge of popular science writing is to
foster that participation.


Theoretical Lens: Rhetorical Genre Theory

In keeping with the idea of inclusiveness as the litmus test for popular
science texts, Holliman et al. base their hope for ‘a reinvigorated social
contract for science’ (p. xviii) on improved communication. To effect
that improvement, we need a framework for understanding popular
science texts in terms of PAST and CUSP—a framework this section
describes.
   In creating this framework, I followed the example of Prelli’s A Rhetoric
of Science which set out, in the early years of rhetoric of science, to ‘use-
fully identify the kinds of discursive decisions scientists must make
when they seek to render their claims reasonable in the eyes of scientific
audiences’ (p. 8). Adapting this goal to popular science writing, I set out
to identify the kinds of decisions science communicators make when
writing to nonspecialist audiences, to develop an analytic framework
that lets us ‘discover the organizing standards of invention and judg-
ment that are already in play’ in this rhetorical arena (Prelli, ‘Empirical’,
p. 424). This, in turn, helps us reach the goal of CUSP in popular sci-
ence writing by illuminating what Bawarshi and Reiif call ‘the available
typifications’ a reader might find in texts and take to other situations.
As they explain, ‘How we determine a situation is based not so much on
our direct perception of the situation but…on our ability to define it by
way of the available typifications, which then shape our perceptions of
how, why, and when to act’ (Bawarshi and Reiff, p. 68). Thus, the goal is
to create a framework that lets us understand how popular science texts
can make available typifications that encourage critical engagement
with science and technology.
   Rhetorical genre theory (RGT) suits this goal because it views texts as
‘mediating artifacts rather than things in themselves’ (Bazerman and
                                     Theoretical and Analytical Framework 27


Russell, p. 1). As noted in the introduction, RGT views genres in terms
of how they help create ‘social and discursive reality’ (Bawarshi and
Reiff, p. 101) or, in classical terms, as they help define what is possible,
as well as what is good. Texts in a given genre do this by employing
textual strategies that suggest and normalize roles (writers, readers, sub-
jects) and the relationships between them. That is, they affect how peo-
ple relate to each other and how we perceive our own and others’ social
roles. Additionally, they influence action by way of cognition because
‘how we know and how we act are related to one another’ (Bawarshi
and Reiff, p. 79).3
   Genre theory is also useful for showing how genres occupy an inter-
mediate place between the context of culture and the specific rhetorical
situations. Devitt describes these interacting levels of meaning-making:
at the macro level, the ‘context of culture’ refers to the sociocultural
context, consisting of ‘existing ideological and material contexts’
(p. 26). At the micro level—the context of situation, also known as the
rhetorical situation—is the set of factors at play in a particular text. It
includes the specific exigence, the writer’s goals, the writer’s understand-
ing of audience, the constraints (whether material or ideological), and
any other factor that influences the choice of particular rhetorical strat-
egies. Analyzing rhetorical situations lets us analyze genres; in fact, all
genre study involves an awareness of the rhetorical situation, as genres
‘are shorthand terms for situations’ (Devitt, p. 16). Thus, understanding
the rhetorical situations implicit in a genre’s texts lets us understand
that genre’s functions, from what problem it addresses or need it meets,
to which roles and actions it suggests and which it discourages or elides.


Analytical Framework: The Rhetorical Situation

Given that rhetorical situations can be used to understand genres, the
next question is how to understand rhetorical situations. One way to
conceptualize rhetorical situations is with the communication triangle,
frequently represented as a speaker, audience, and subject matter, with
a surrounding circle to represent the context (Figure 2.1).
   To highlight the relationships between different elements of a rhetori-
cal situation, I have added four labels (Figure 2.2).
   These labels emphasize the relationships between the components
rather than the components themselves.
   Demarcation includes general boundary work and the more specific
scope of particpation created by particular concepts of expertise.

• Boundary work refers to how popular science texts situate science
  in relation to its social, cultural, and political context. Expressed
  in terms of the Traditional–Idealist and Realist–Skeptical constructs
  described above, a text may attempt to demarcate sciences from
  society or to show the how sciences are embedded in their contexts.
                                     Theoretical and Analytical Framework 29


  This is addressed in Chapter 5, ‘Boundary Work: Presenting Science
  in Context’.
• Scope of Participation focuses on demarcation at the individual level,
  referring to how narrowly or broadly a text defines whose opinions
  count in discussions of science-in-society, a topic covered in Chapter 6,
  ‘Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation’.

   Ethos describes the writer’s credibility and the stance that he or she
takes toward science, while persona describes the writer’s relation-
ship to the reader, both discussed in Chapter 7, ‘Ethos: Establishing
Relationships with Readers’.
   Finally, subject position describes the reader’s relationship to a par-
ticular domain, in this case science, described in Chapter 8, ‘Rhetorical
Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement’.
   Each of these also comes up in Chapter 9, ‘Technocracy and Democracy:
Talking about Risk’.

Two Models
As these descriptions suggest, answering the question ‘What rhetorical
situation does a text create?’ helps us understand the ‘activities, identi-
ties, and institutions’ that are being ‘continuously and actively rebuilt’
(Gee, p. 10) through popular science texts. Two models4—PAST and
CUSP—operationalize two ends of the spectrum that popular science
texts occupy in these various areas.
   At the PAST end of the spectrum, the goal is to generate support for
science, especially among nonspecialists. In the rhetorical situation in
these texts, therefore, authority is concentrated in the science point of
the triangle, with beneficial effects flowing out from science to society,
to writers, and (indirectly, via science’s effects on society and via popu-
lar science writers) to readers (Figure 2.3).
   In this model, congruent with the traditional social contract, science
is separate from society, affecting but not affected by it, and expertise
is restricted to people with formal credentials. The writer’s role is to
translate the information, as accurately and objectively as possible,
to the readers. The reader’s role is to absorb the information so he or
she can better understand the scientific point of view and, presum-
ably, value it more strongly. The public is thus involved passively at
best, getting scientific information from popular science writers, but
not positioned to do anything with that knowledge. An anecdote by
Rick Borchelt, a communications director at Johns Hopkins University,
illustrates the PAST model in action. Borchelt tells about talking with

creators of a high energy physics exhibit. When the creators discovered
that visitors did not understand their writing, they concluded not that
the material needed to change, but ‘that physics education needed to
be better so that audiences could understand their exhibit’ (quoted in
Blanchard, p. 291). This anecdote exemplifies how, in the deficit model,
the exigence shaping the rhetorical situation is a belief that public igno-
rance about science is the root of all problems, even problems caused by
a weakness in the science communication itself.
   At the CUSP end of the spectrum, the goal is to foster engagement
of specialists and nonspecialists alike with science in public; the focus
is not on getting readers up to speed with a particular knowledge set,
but on developing ‘public means of coming to a public understanding
concerning public issues’ (Gross, ‘The Roles’, p. 5) having to do with
science. Therefore, in the CUSP version of the communication triangle,
authority is not concentrated in the scientific point of the triangle, but
is shared by the writer, the reader, and other social actors depicted in
the text as well. In this view, the rhetorical situation shows a great deal
of reciprocity along each leg of the triangle (Figure 2.4).
   Additionally, where a deficit model of popular science writing
obscures the interplay between science and the larger society of which
it is one part, the CUSP model foregrounds these relationships and
explores the interactions between science and society, readers and sci-
ence, writers and science, and writers and readers. It shows that science

is part of society, that readers are actively engaged in deciding what
science means, and that writers value science, but also understand it as
one knowledge system among many.
   The CUSP model does not deny the positive aspects of the PAST
model, but rather subsumes them. For example, the PAST model calls
for communicating information about science to people who don’t
know that information, while CUSP includes the informative function
of popular science writing but also addresses other aspects. Similarly,
the PAST model calls for an appreciation of the benefits of science, and
CUSP includes that appreciation when it is appropriate. Ultimately, the
CUSP model recognizes that doxa (public knowledge) must set—and, in
fact, does set—the framework within which episteme (scientific knowl-
edge) works. If communicators are unhappy about that doxastic frame-
work, the answer is not to bludgeon the public with more episteme, or
with messages about the superiority of episteme. The answer is to talk
about doxa in terms of doxa, to address episteme from civil society’s
point of view and thereby to engage people as interlocutors rather than
as passive audiences.


Corpus Selection

My selection of study texts involved several decisions, from the choice
to focus on print media to the selection of specific popular science texts.
32 Communicating Popular Science


Why Print Media?
Within rhetoric of science, there are many useful approaches to study-
ing science communication, from field research such as Ken Baake’s
study of metaphor use at the Santa Fe Institute (Metaphor and Knowledge)
to Lynda Walsh’s historical study of scientific hoaxes (Sins Against
Science). I focus on current print media (meaning texts intended for
print, even if they are also delivered electronically) for a few reasons.
   First, in genre study, textual analysis is essential. Although genre stud-
ies will ideally have two kinds of research—one dedicated to analyzing a
set of texts and the other focused on analyzing interview data’ from prac-
titioners (Schryer, p. 46)—textual analysis has primacy. Practitioner inter-
views can add valuable insights into texts and contexts, but the ‘close
reading (objective) of specific texts to describe and critique the strategies
evoked within these discursive events…takes priority’ (Schryer, p. 39).
   Second, textual studies offer the researcher and his or her audience
the same access to the object of analysis. That is, people reading this
can get copies of the texts analyzed and can read those texts themselves.
   Third, even in an increasingly multimodal age, analysis of print media
remains important because written texts exemplify the ‘stabilizing
effects of artifacts that change only slowly’ (Bazerman, ‘Discursively’,
p. 300) in contrast to the more transitory texts that influence a few
people and then disappear from sight. Print media, in particular, offers a
repository of perspectives and positions, functioning as ‘an inventory of
cultural elements, such as events, dates, metaphors, frames and symbols
associated with a specific issue’ (Peters et al., p. 87).


Kinds of Texts
The next step was to choose a set of study texts. My choice was
informed by a desire to understand not just popular science texts, but
also the point of view of people writing them. Therefore, I looked at two
sets of texts. The first set consists of popular science texts, chosen from
the The Best American Science Writing and The Best American Science and
Nature Writing series, while the second is an anthology on how to write
about science, written by practising popular science writers, A Field
Guide for Science Writers.
  For the popular science texts, I drew from the Best American Science
Writing and the Best American Science and Nature Writing series, both of
which started in 2000, because these series fit the following criteria.
  First, they are intended for nonspecialist readers.
  Second, they offer a sampling of high-quality texts from the mass
media, and the ‘mass media constitute the most important forum for
                                      Theoretical and Analytical Framework 33


the public sphere in modern societies, providing an organising frame-
work for societal self-observation’ (Schäfer. ‘Taking Stock’, p. 650).
   Third, the pieces in these anthologies are not ‘perishable’ (Russell,
N. J., p. 138) like newspaper articles, and so serve the stabilizing func-
tion referred to above.
   Fourth, they contain widely read science articles and essays.5 Each
year the anthologies’ editors are chosen for their own prestige as sci-
ence writers, further increasing their popular appeal, and each series has
received positive reviews in scientific and generalist publications, includ-
ing Science News, Publisher’s Weekly, Booklist, Science Communication, The
New Scientist, Scientific American, and others.
   Finally, these texts are models of success in the field of science popu-
larization. Most of the pieces are drawn from specialty news magazines,
which ‘offer some of the richest and most sophisticated coverage of sci-
ence for general audiences’ (Weigold, p. 168), and the pieces selected favor
the elite media that function ‘as opinion leaders and inter-media agenda
setters’ (Schäfer, ‘Taking Stock’, p. 656). Prominent writers matter in
any field, but are especially important in science popularization; Turney
describes ‘more of a common interest among science writers than those
in many other genres’ (p. 87), as popular science writers borrow ideas,
analogies, and ‘even turns of phrase from one another’ to the extent that
‘Some are re-used so often that they become common property…We end
up with a common stock of explanatory stories, which are readily avail-
able for re-working and re-use’ (Turney, p. 87). Collections like the Best
American series, with their lineups of well-known scientists and writers as
guest editors, offer a convenient corpus of what leaders in popular science
writing consider the best work in their field over a dozen years.
   I chose A Field Guide for Science Writers as a practitioner text for three
reasons. First, this book is being used in many science writing classes,
both graduate and undergraduate. An informal survey of courses in
these areas indicates that the Field Guide is one of the most-assigned
texts and therefore has an influence on many science writers. Second,
as the ‘Official Guide of the National Association of Science Writers’,
the book comes with the imprimatur of the nation’s oldest and larg-
est science writing organization. It is also recommended by the
Council for the Advancement of Science Writing and by the American
Association for the Advancement of Science in their list of resources
on ‘Communicating Science: Tools for Scientists and Engineers’. Third,
as an edited collection with short pieces by forty-eight contributors in
addition to the editors, this book includes many perspectives and pro-
vides a good broad-spectrum view of what the luminaries of the field
think science writing is and should be.
34 Communicating Popular Science


Topical Focus on Ecology
Within the set of texts in the Best American series, I chose to focus on
texts about ecology, broadly construed, for six reasons.
   First, ecology affects everyone. Given an interest in science-in-
society, it makes sense to look at areas of science that have far-reaching
implications for society. Examples of studies and research areas that fit
under the umbrella concept of ‘ecology’ include—in addition to the
obvious fields such as ecology, conservation biology, and watershed
management—specialties such as aquatic toxicology, bioeconomics,
entomology, environmental microbiology, herbivory, insect biodemog-
raphy, marine population dynamics, plant sciences (including plant-
animal and plant-insect interactions), and viticulture.6 These fields,
although varying greatly in their focuses, have two things in common:
they all deal with complex systems, and they all deal with issues that
affect people directly or indirectly.
   Second, much work being done in ecology—unlike the work in, say,
quantum physics—can be expressed in everyday language, and so ecol-
ogy is an area in which science writers should be able to engage readers
relatively easily. The burden of explanation being less, the writer should
be able to concentrate more on the strategies that promote conversa-
tions between different groups of people.
   Third, it’s an area of science in which problems cannot be reduced
to small, isolated questions. Most problems we confront as a society do
not have clear-cut, singular solutions, and most questions do not have
singular answers. Allen et al. offer this example:

  For instance, one cannot calibrate the way to ascertaining the exact
  degree to which human activity is responsible for global warming.
  First one needs to define ‘human activity,’ then what one means by
  ‘responsible,’ not to mention global ‘warming’ scenarios...All those
  decisions produce alternate answers, and each is necessarily value
  laden. When the model is complex and values cannot be fixed by
  fiat, conventional paradigmatic approaches to scientific modeling
  and forecasting are inappropriate. Orthodoxy will fail when one is
  trying to predict the behavior of complex systems, whether or not
  the calls of judgment are overtly value laden. (p. 476)

Because CUSP means being able to handle complexity and the difficulty
of making decisions under conditions of uncertainty, I wanted to avoid
the ‘ideological function of impeccable work in perfectly controlled lab-
oratory science’ (Haraway, p. 31) that artificially clears waters that are,
                                      Theoretical and Analytical Framework 35


in many areas of science, necessarily muddy. Ecology is ideal because
it is a field ‘in which it is often especially difficult to bring closure to
important questions’ because of the ‘the great diversity of entities’
involved in an ecological study (S.H. Jenkins, p. 182).
   Fourth, ecology offers a useful foil to the traditional ‘model’ science,
physics, and so allows insights into what counts as science and what
doesn’t, and about who is and isn’t an expert. Traditionally, physics has
been ‘a kind of gold standard’ of science, which has been used ‘to exem-
plify how rigorously deductive analysis of a few general laws, confirmed
or falsified by induction from controlled experiment, provided predic-
tive knowledge’ (Collini, p. xlvii). Scientific disciplines fit this model to
greater or lesser degrees, and ecological sciences provide an example of
‘lesser’. In just one example of how ecology differs from physics, one can’t
experiment with a watershed; there are no controls available, no identical
watersheds to be left unaltered and used for comparative purposes.
   Fifth, it’s an area that allows for nonscientist involvement, and
therefore an area in which writers can include a range of perspectives.
Contrasting ecology with physics again, it is easy to see why. Although
the nonastrophysicist is unlikely to be involved in debates about the
existence or nonexistence of weakly interacting massive particles, one
does not have to be an aquatic toxicologist to learn and have relevant
opinions about the effects of chemical contaminants in a water system,
especially a system from which one’s own drinking water comes.
   Sixth, and last, I wanted an area where I am in agreement with the
science’s overall aims and can therefore be wholehearted in working to
improve its communication.
   To identify texts related to ecology, I went through several steps. The
first was to create a database listing all texts in all issues of both collec-
tions, to eliminate articles whose titles indicated they were clearly about
another topic area (e.g., Weed’s ‘The Very Best Telescope’ or Ferris’s
‘Interstellar Spaceflight’), and to eliminate more from the Best American
