Science and Nature Writing series by reading the summaries preceding the
texts. For the rest, I skimmed the article to determine its focus. In most
cases the focus was clear on the first page, but I skimmed through to the
end to be sure to identify any articles that changed topic. The result was
about thirty texts loosely focused on ecology. These I read closely, and
excluded texts that dealt with natural phenomena without discussing
scientific aspects (such as research on the phenomenon or scientists’
involvement of any kind). Finally, from the texts that address ecological
issues, I chose as examples those that illuminate, through their typical-
ity or atypicality,7 the strategies available to public science writers. Thus,
36 Communicating Popular Science


the study texts do not necessarily represent the genre or even subgenre
as a whole, but rather were chosen for how well they illustrate the
strategies identified from within the larger set of ecology-related texts.


Conclusion

Rhetoric is, above all, pragmatic, a ‘socially useful discipline for further-
ing community aims by influencing the thought and action’ of com-
munity members (Prelli, A Rhetoric, p. 35). I wrote this book because
I wanted a way to understand what science writing adds to conversa-
tions about science in public and to contribute to making those con-
versations more productive. While I question problematic aspects of
popular science writing as a genre, the goal is not criticism, but critique.
In order to do this, I develop a critical apparatus for examining popular
science texts in terms of how they foster, or fail to foster, CUSP.
   Public understanding is created through public conversations.
Conversations draw on existing ‘cultural resources’ (Locke, p. 97).
When one set of cultural resources is widely shared, there is little prob-
lem. However, when different sets of cultural resources are involved,
public conversations can become confusing or even contentious. Miller
describes a state in which ‘with a wealth of stimuli and a dearth of
shared knowledge, we hardly know how to engage each other in dis-
course’ in part because we have ‘few effective orientation centers for
joint action’ (‘Genre’, p. 158); equally difficult are situations when we
have too many, or conflicting, orientation centers. This is the case with
issues of science-in-society, as two orientation centers—two sets of cul-
tural resources—are built on different understandings of the nature of
science and the role of popular science writing. I hope to help change
the terms of the conversation by revealing the tacit meanings in exist-
ing conversational resources.
   A Traditional–Idealist construct of science promotes technocratic
rather than democratic decision-making. In addition, it is bad for sci-
ence; this kind of ‘extreme positivism is as damaging to science as the
extreme scepticism of its arch-enemy, “strong” relativism’ (Ziman, p. 326).
If popular science writing is to help forge a new social contract, it must
leave behind this unrealistic and uncritical view of science. Instead of
‘worshipping the fetish of scientific objectivity’ (Haraway, p. 18), popu-
lar science writing can orient itself toward a Realist–Skeptical construct
of science. Recognizing the interpellation of science and society, far
from devaluing science, actually produces texts that base their concep-
tual framework on a realistic look at what scientific facts mean.
3
A Brief History of Science
Popularization




This chapter looks at the origins of science popularization and how it has
changed as the scientific and social contexts have changed. Understood in
terms of the rhetorical situation, the history of science popularization is a
history of a changing set of rhetors, exigences, and audiences. Understood
in terms of kinds of knowledge, it is a history of shifting relationships
between episteme (scientific or specialist knowledge) and doxa (public
knowledge). In general, the trend over the last four-and-a-half centuries
has been a movement from episteme and doxa being inseparable, to their
being balanced, to a relatively recent elevation of episteme to special status.
   I take the mid-1600s as my starting point, knowing that science is
much older. As historian Anthony Alioto points out, science ‘arises from
the need to solve problems, the greatest of which was simple survival’,
and to meet that need, humans have been creating and testing hypoth-
eses about the natural world probably for as long as there have been
humans (p. 2). Thus, although the history of science is often presented
as beginning in Western Europe in the seventeenth century, this start-
ing point is, in many ways, both arbitrary and ethnocentric. However,
my focus is on science communication in English, and that is gener-
ally considered to have begun in mid-seventeenth-century London.
Specifically, I start with the Royal Society of London. Founded in 1665,
the Royal Society is the world’s oldest surviving scientific institution,
and their texts are considered the beginning of ‘the public production
and reception of scientific knowledge’ (Payne, p. 96) in English.1


Early Science: 1600s

Scientific practices in the 1600s were, as in previous eras, eclectic,
as people in many walks of life employed a variety of strategies for

                                      37
38 Communicating Popular Science


understanding the natural world. This eclecticism, far from being anti-
thetical to science, was part of the point. Part of what Bacon had called
for in the previous century, and the Royal Society wanted to do, was
to amass information, ‘including the exhaustive cataloguing of both
eyewitness and previously published accounts of natural/experimental
phenomena by a widely distributed group of scientific reporters’
(Atkinson and Royal Society, p. 18). What shifted at this point, then,
was not so much how people found out things about the natural world
as it was the organized attempt to bring together and make sense of that
information en masse and in a systematic way.
   Also continuous with past practices, episteme and doxa were not
distinguished, as science was not demarcated from other practices. The
Society’s ‘identification with the Baconian goal of a cooperatively con-
structed universal natural history’ (Atkinson and Royal Society, p. 20)
meant it was open to information from any and all people who offered
insights; in fact, Bacon had explicitly mentioned the importance of
learning what craftsmen knew about the natural world (Conner, p. 250),
and the Society’s first secretary, Robert Hooke, affirmed that the point
was for ‘No Intelligence from Men of all Professions, and quarters of the
World, to be slighted’ (p. vi). Anchoring the newly organized endeavor
to social values of the time is the fact that science was intended to help
people; the Baconian ideals that were at the heart of the Royal Society’s
mission included a commitment to making knowledge available to
serve human uses. In this desire to share knowledge openly, the Royal
Society was building on the earlier ‘general movement’ in the previous
century ‘in which various “secrete knowledges” (e.g., alchemy), till then
the provinces of insider initiation and learning, were becoming suspect
for those very reasons’ (Montgomery, p. 78).
   The inseparability of science and other activities was reflected, too, in
the Society’s membership, a ‘fairly large, loose-knit group of amateurs
and “natural philosophers”… some extraordinarily talented, some with
nothing more extraordinary than an above-average curiosity about the
natural world’ (Gross et al., p. 372). In fact, in its early years, the Royal
Society was eager enough for money that fellows were elected as much
for their finances as for their scientific acumen (Inwood, p. 24). In the
first ten years of the Society, only 57 of the 261 members had scholarly
or medical training, with the rest characterized by Hooke’s biographer,
Stephen Inwood, as ‘well-off dabblers without substantial scientific or
mathematical backgrounds’ (p. 26).
   Given that the Society’s ‘identity as the first public forum for organ-
ized empirical science was based partly on its efforts to communicate
                                    A Brief History of Science Popularization 39


its work to the learned community at large’ (Atkinson, p. 18), it is no
surprise that texts were written to be accessible. Practising scientists,
such as Hooke, understood the importance of making sure their dem-
onstrations and articles were accessible to their less-specialist colleagues;
indeed, Hooke ‘wanted to persuade his readers that anyone with resolu-
tion and integrity, “a sincere hand and a faithful eye”, could do what he
had done’ (Inwood, p. 61). Science was to be the province of all.


Enlightenment Science: 1700s and Early 1800s

Through the late 1600s and the 1700s, science and other activities
remained inseparable. The most famous American example is Benjamin
Franklin—scientist, inventor, statesman, philosopher, publisher, and
so on, but even academic scientists of the time were still involved in a
variety of pursuits, as exemplified by Joseph Priestley. Known in the his-
tory of science mainly for his work in chemistry, Priestley also studied,
taught, and wrote about many other topics. Montgomery notes that
Priestley’s ‘fame as the discoverer of oxygen (“dephlogisticated air”) has
unfortunately overshadowed an extremely wide-ranging authorship.
Besides writing on chemistry, he penned works on theology, education,
government, and grammar’ (pp. 100–1).
  Although today we might be inclined to see these as distinct areas,
and to see Priestley as an impressive polymath with a variety of separate
interests, it is important to understand that seeing such a separation is
an artifact of living in our own, highly specialized time. For Priestley,
as for Franklin and other Enlightenment-era scientists, the various
realms overlapped and intermingled freely. For example, Priestley was
a devout Christian clergyman, but his three-part Institutes of Natural
and Revealed Religion was an argument for judging Biblical statements,
in part, according to whether or not they accorded with understand-
ings of the natural world. Also during this time, in what historian
Bernadette Bensaude-Vincent terms the ‘tradition of mundane science’
(p. 101), members of the public continued to be involved in science,
both as amateur scientists and as participants in civic decision-making.
Science, Bensaude-Vincent tells us, was one facet of the Enlightenment
movement toward a broad-based rationality and civic engagement, and,
during this period, writers did not distinguish between studying nature
and studying society.
  During this time, there was ‘no dramatic change’ in terms of what
kinds of articles were being written, with observation still the main
textual focus (Gross et al., p. 93). However, there was a shift in the form
40 Communicating Popular Science


of ‘heightened interest in the experimental and methodological’ and
toward arguing for facts (Gross et al., p. 93), and, by the end of the cen-
tury, the scientific literature shows a shift away from the conventions of
personal trust and personal prose toward a more information-oriented
approach to science (Atkinson, p. 154). This approach, characterized by
the move toward more quantitative research, was part of a larger move
to a more professional and impersonal science that continued in the
1800s. Even so, the writing was still highly personal as ‘the “I” is still the
central epistemological agent. It is still an entity that does not merely
report on events but that introduces, performs, and receives the action
all at once, and then reflects upon it, for the purposes of inscribing
knowledge’ (Montgomery, p. 100).


Professionalizing Science: Mid- and Late-1800s

In the early 1800s, as in the 1700s, there was little distinction
made between scientific and philosophical knowledge, and most
representations of science focused on what it had in common with
‘complementary truth-seeking activities’ (Thurs, p. 4). However, the
late 1700s had seen signs of a shift toward greater specialization in
science, and that shift continued, speeding up through the middle
and later 1800s.2
   During the early 1800s, work we now call science was being done
by ordinary people—‘farmers, tradesmen, clerical workers, and manual
laborers’—as much as by what we now think of as scientists (Thurs,
p. 28). For example, ‘Informal botanists often had a cooperative rather
than a deferential relationship with specialists…In fact, amateur prac-
tice was one of the major routes to becoming engaged in more formal
scientific activity’ (Thurs, p. 28). Similarly, there was little to differen-
tiate science from other practices. Thurs explains that, at this time,
science’s ‘methods were the same as those that artists, theologians,
and even ordinary people used every day. Its practitioners were not
distinguished as a special class’ (p. 11). However, the continuity that
characterized the early part of the century gave way during its course to
an increase in professionalization and the demarcation of science from
other activities, and of scientists from nonscientists. Specialization was
encouraged by the development of many science-focused institutions,
such as national academies of sciences, and, as the century progressed,
science began to become more professionalized and more specialized;
‘science’ was becoming ‘sciences’.
                                    A Brief History of Science Popularization 41


   During this time episteme and doxa went through a similar separa-
tion, but a balance between the two was maintained. The century began
with a relationship between episteme and doxa in which ‘scientific
knowledge was not important because it was different. Instead, science
was significant because it harmonized with truth of all kinds’ (Thurs,
p. 24). Even as demarcation of science from society increased, ‘con-
tinuity between science and common sense’ remained intact, and
the public ‘was expected to correct the scientists who were too much
inclined toward esotericism’ (Bensaude-Vincent, p. 104). Although
episteme and doxa were starting to separate, neither was privileged
above the other.
   In terms of science communication, the professionalization of the
sciences was reflected in increasing distinctions between texts writ-
ten for scientists and those written for amateurs; like science itself,
scientific communication became more specialized, and writing about
science for nonspecialist readers began to emerge as a separate area of
practice. By the end of the 1700s, while the ‘author-centered approach’
to writing about science was still common, Atkinson identifies some
‘signs that a different, essentially object-oriented rhetoric was beginning
to assert itself’ (p. 77, emphasis original), and, over the course of the
1800s—accelerating in the later part of the century—scientific commu-
nication narrowed as specializations developed their own journals and
as the ‘popular passion for natural philosophy was undermined by its
increasing professionalism’ (Russell, N. J., pp. 7–8). In keeping with the
move to a more professional and impersonal science, scientific writing
shifted further and further away from personal trust and personal prose
and toward a more information-oriented approach (Atkinson, p. 154)
in which the experimental report became a staple, and publications
exhibited an increased concern with correct scientific methods.
   As scientific writing was growing more esoteric, some writers began
doing science communication for nonspecialists; Thurs identifies the
1830s and 1840s as a ‘crossroads in the formation of separate “elite”
and “popular” forums in the Anglo-American world’ (p. 31). Even so,
scientists were still writing these texts and ‘no specialist trade of science
journalism had yet emerged’ (Russell, N. J., p. xxiii). Furthermore, sci-
ence communication was still in keeping with democratic principles;
‘Elitist depictions of science were rare’ (Thurs, p. 34) and public science
communication was rooted in ideas of democracy and citizenship. In
an important distinction, Bensaude-Vincent notes that ‘popular science’
had not yet turned into ‘popularized science’ (p. 104).
42 Communicating Popular Science


Big Science, Scientism, and the Traditional Social Contract

What was Going On
The shift that began in the nineteenth century set the stage for the rise
of scientism in the twentieth century. By the late 1800s, science had
started organizing around ‘problem-statements’ as research was ‘becom-
ing contextualized in communities of researchers working on similar
problems’ that was to foster the growth of ‘big science’ at the expense
of ‘high-level scientific amateurism’ (Atkinson, p. 97) in the twentieth
century. Thurs explains that in the early 1900s, ‘the once nascent sci-
entific establishment matured into an increasingly organized system of
practices and institutions’ (p. 93), an organized system that included
the development of graduate programs and professional organizations,
as well as an increasing number of specialized jobs, academic and indus-
trial, in scientific fields. Eventually, this led to what historians have
dubbed ‘Big Science’, the large-scale and resource-intensive projects that
characterize much of science today.
   Increased organization also led to increased demarcation and to
the traditional social contract of the mid-20th century. Big science is
rooted in a positivist belief in scientific objectivity and a modernist
drive to categorize, organize, and maintain boundaries, and demarca-
tion was accompanied by a shifting value structure in which science
was understood not just as separate from but as better than other forms
of knowledge making. In other words, it was accompanied by a strong
confidence in the Traditional–Idealist construct of science, and, conse-
quently, by scientism of various sorts—political (in claims that science
could solve social problems), ontological (in claims that science alone
could describe the nature of reality), axiological (in claims that science
offered a better alternative to traditional moral structures), and so on.
   LaFollette uses the government’s decision about funding in the late
1940s as an illustration of the pro-science ‘prevalent cultural images
of science’ at the time (p. 15). She explains that the funding model
‘allocated significant control to the organized scientific community’
(p. 15) rather than holding it accountable, as some opponents argued it
should be, to what President Truman’s assistant, John Steelman, called
‘traditions of democratic government’ (quoted in LaFollette, p. 15). In
other words, the decision was to give scientists more autonomy in use
of government funds than was given to other groups. This was possible,
LaFollette explains, because of ‘Americans’ trust in and expectation of
science and scientists’ (p. 15).
   For many years, this trust was given nearly unconditionally. Pardo
and Calvo describe the relationship between science and society from
                                   A Brief History of Science Popularization 43


its roots in early modernity to the present as a parabola. The first stage,
which lasted until after World War II, saw an ever-increasing confidence
in science, and

   strong social consent to the scientific mission, limited regulation,
   and a high degree of legitimization that was granted due to the
   almost universal belief in both the inherent value and the material
   benefits of scientific progress. This belief was a crucial component of
   modernist mentality and culture, and it is in this context that the
   subculture of the scientific community evolved, which still exists
   today.
                                                 (Pardo and Calvo, p. 160)

The relationship that evolved is the old social contract, an understand-
ing in which scientists were given funding and autonomy, and were
expected to produce goods, from the creation of material items to
answering questions both within and outside of the sciences’ traditional
domains. With the rise of this ‘solubility ethos’ (as David Tietge calls
it in Flash Effect), science came to be considered ‘the most legitimate
discourse on matters of public concern’ (Brown, R. H., p. 2) and other
forms of knowledge were diminished.
   In terms of texts, an obvious consequence and driver of increasing
scientific specialization was the fact that scientific writing became
incomprehensible to outsiders; first meaning those outside of science
writ large, but eventually coming to mean anyone outside of a given
narrow niche. Whereas in the century before scientific communication
was accessible to the average educated person, by the end of the twenti-
eth century, a scientist in biology could not expect to understand a pub-
lication in chemistry, and—even more—a specialist in one branch of
chemistry often could not understand the technical material of another
branch. Thus, Atkinson describes the twentieth century in terms of
a ‘continued development of rhetorical conventions for embedding
research’ in the context of a discourse community (p. 97)—and who
counted as a member of each discourse community became more and
more narrowly defined.
   The combination of esoteric scientific writing with the solubility ethos
led to the invention of the popular scientist as science booster. LaFollette
ties the solubility ethos to periodicals that ‘presented science to their
readers not in isolation, but as an integral part of modern life which
affected and sometimes usurped the traditional authority of morality,
social custom, and politics’ (p. 7). In a parallel shift, as demarcation
separated science from society to the detriment of the latter, the public
44 Communicating Popular Science


came to be seen in deficit terms which resulted in ‘depriving the public of
all possible qualification’ (Bensaude-Vincent, p. 101) to address science-
related issues. By mid-century, Bensaude-Vincent writes, this view had
solidified, and popular science had been replaced by what I call PAST
(Public Appreciation of Science and Technology) model popular science,
characterized by unidirectional communication and a belief that ‘public
opinion has nothing to teach the scientists’ (p. 107). Instead, science was
being reconstructed as a guide for public decisions of all kinds. Although
there were counter-trends to the rampant scientism during this time, this
‘realization that each new advance carried the potential for both benefit
and harm’ was peripheral early in the century, growing only as the cen-
tury progressed (LaFollette, pp. 12–13, emphasis original). The growth of
this counter narrative is the topic of the next section.

Post-Academic Science and the Need for a New Social
Contract

What was Going on in Science
The early 1900s saw a shift in how science was done, from most of the
work being done by individuals to the advent of big science, character-
ized by large teams and large amounts of money. In the later part of
the century and into the twenty-first, big science further morphed into
what Ziman calls post-academic science, that is science ‘organized on
market principles’ (p. 173) with all that implies—proprietary instead of
shared knowledge; problems selected based on their having potentially
marketable solutions, instead of based on their theoretical or social
importance; and so on.
   In Ziman’s words, post-academic science can be understood as a proc-
ess of ‘increasing subordination to corporate and political interests that
do not put a high value on the production of knowledge for the benefit
of society at large’ (p. 116) and that do not consider themselves account-
able to the public for what they do. For example, while post-academic
scientists are expected to be honest in the sense of telling the truth about
their research, they are not expected, as publically-funded scientists
traditionally have been, to tell the whole truth. In fact, Ziman points
out that they often are not allowed to tell the whole truth even when
they want to, ‘prevented, in the interests of their employers, clients or
patrons, from revealing discoveries or expressing doubts that would
put a very different complexion on their testimony’ (Ziman, p. 176).
Although such control is contrary to the ethos of science, it is becoming
common in many areas of science (e.g., pharmaceutical research).
                                    A Brief History of Science Popularization 45


   Despite its name, post-academic science also affects research at public
universities. The terms of how science is done are changing as public
funding no longer keeps pace with the expanding scope of science
(including the staggering number of new fields and subfields), and
researchers are forced to find money elsewhere. At times, Ziman says,
competition for funding ‘takes precedence over competition for scientific
credibility as the driving force of science’ to such an extent that ‘Research
groups are transformed into small business enterprises’ (p. 76) as energy
is siphoned off from research work and into the pursuit of money to keep
the research going. The scramble for funds should be familiar to anyone
affiliated with a university today, with the pressure rising.
   Changes in the nature of scientific inquiry have paralleled changes
in public reactions to scientific research. Although during much of the
twentieth century ‘the hegemony of the sciences went unquestioned…
as a form of knowledge which was self-evident, self-regulating, and
beyond the reach of human subjectivity’ (Atkinson, p. xvii), the last
few decades saw a decline in that automatic trust as people began to
ask more critical questions about science and to challenge the assump-
tions underlying the solubility ethos and the traditional social contract.
Although direct cause–effect relationships don’t exist in the complex
world of civil discourse, several factors have influenced this change.

• First, increasing ties between science and the private sector have
  undermined science’s reputation for unbiased truth-telling. As cor-
  porate influences on research and corporate control or ownership of
  research results have grown, science is seen as having lost its objectiv-
  ity, and the ‘credibility of the scientists is thus tainted by the equivoca-
  tions of their political or commercial masters’ (Ziman, p. 177). A recent
  example in California centers on the energy industry’s sponsorship
  and, in some cases, control of energy-related research (e.g., on biofuels
  and carbon emissions). At Stanford, for example, research projects
  are authorized by a five-panel committee: four representatives from
  ExxonMobil and one non-voting representative from Stanford (Bohan,
  ‘Corporate U’).
• Second, revelations about unethical research have raised questions
  even about public science and the ability of sciences to regulate
  themselves. Nelkin cites government studies in which scientists gave
  plutonium-laced milk to developmentally disabled children living in
  a state home (‘The Science Wars’, p. 119), and other examples are,
  regrettably, not hard to find (e.g., the Tuskegee syphilis experiment,
  the Willowbrook study, and, more recently, the exporting of risky
46 Communicating Popular Science


  clinical trials to poor countries to circumvent US regulations; for the
  latter, see Barlett and Steele).
• Third, a growing awareness of the environmental and human health
  costs of scientific ‘progress’ has brought challenges to the scientistic
  worldview. Starting with Rachel Carson’s 1962 Silent Spring, envi-
  ronmental historian Ralph H. Lutts says there has been a ‘growing
  public awareness of a variety of environmental problems, including
  water and air pollution’ caused by human products (p. 19). Several
  popular science texts I address later point out problems that were the
  result, sometimes unforeseen and sometimes not, of earlier scientific
  accomplishments, such as the damage to honeybee populations by
  pesticides.

As these examples suggest, the rising skepticism about technoscientific
progress is based on reasonable concerns. However, even in the current
climate of increased skepticism, a general trust has not been replaced
with distrust. As the National Science Board’s Science and Engineering
Indicators show, science in general continues to be held in high regard,
with skepticism arising in specific areas and in relation to particular
problems. The nature of that skepticism, and to what extent it is shared
by popular science writers, is the topic of Chapter 4, ‘Practitioner
Perspectives’.


Conclusion

Big science and post-academic science make up the context in which
‘the subculture of the scientific community evolved, which still exists
today’ (Pardo and Calvo, p. 160), and, despite challenges to scientism
over the last few decades, we now see the public excluded from most
science-related decisions. Couched in rhetorical terms, we have seen a
shift from valuing both scientific knowledge (episteme) and common-
sense opinion (doxa) to a situation characterized by a hierarchical
divide between science and nonscience with technical experts holding
the only epistemically valid coin in the realm. In the time span covered
here, from the 1660s to now, we have seen a shift in scientific prose to
greater and greater opacity, and also a change in science-society rela-
tions such that the sciences’ ‘conscious and broad-scale relatedness to
other areas of culture is also mostly gone’ (Montgomery, p. 118).
   At the same time, there are many indicators that the role of doxa
is increasing again as people outside of specific scientific fields assert
their right to have a voice about how those fields affect them and as
                                  A Brief History of Science Popularization 47


people within many scientific fields join the call for a return to a rela-
tionship in which, as in the earlier years of modern science, the public
is expected to ‘participate in the evolution of science as gatekeepers of
the unity of knowledge, providing the orientation of science policy and
enduring its regulation’ (Bensaude-Vincent, p. 105). As science’s effects
on civil society become more ambivalent, citizens from many arenas are
starting to talk about those effects and about how post-academic science
fits into a democratic society.
   Granted, responses to this increase in criticism have been mixed.
Some scholars, especially in physical sciences, have resisted the idea
that doxa should have a guiding role. Some have gone so far as to attack
not only critics, but also the entire enterprise of Science and Technology
Studies and democratic engagement itself; recall Levitt’s comment,
quoted in Chapter 2, that if science and democracy are in conflict, it is
democracy that should change.3 However, others have embraced what
they see as a positive trend toward greater critical engagement with sci-
ence. Scientists such as Michael Gibbons and Robert Winston are good
examples; Winston, for example, emphasizes the idea that engagement
with the public ‘involves listening to and responding to the ideas, ques-
tions, hopes and concerns the public may have’ (p. 518).
   A similar range of opinions also exists among popular science writers.
A view of science as inherently ennobling continues today in moralistic
messages from science writers who equate lack of scientific knowledge
with lack of personal development. Sometimes they do so tacitly and
sometimes with brazen confidence, but the message is the same: peo-
ple who know about science have not only a greater knowledge of the
natural world, but also a superior intellect, nobler motives, and better
decision-making abilities in all realms of life. At the same time, the
critical perspective that looks at science in context, weighing costs and
benefits and implications on a case-by-case basis, also appears to be
important to some popular science writers. The next chapter explores
this range by looking at what popular science writers say about their
craft.
4
Practitioner Perspectives
on their Craft




Chapter 3 ends with a conundrum. On the one hand, there are posi-
tive recent developments, such as the development of Science and
Technology Studies (STS) in academic circles, the call for a new social
contract from within science, and the call for a new model of commu-
nication from within the world of science popularization. These many
sources agree with Nicholas Russell that the public ‘must be able to
critique science as well as support it’ and that popular science writing
‘therefore has twin duties, to inform and educate the public about sci-
ence on the one hand, but also to probe and criticize it on the other’
(p. xiii). Like Russell, they see the science writer as a ‘watchdog’ with
a threefold role: ‘to check on government and business investments
and responses to science, correct misrepresentations of science by non-
scientific pressure groups, and expose over-enthusiastic manipulation
of the media and other unethical behaviours by scientists themselves’
(p. xvi).
   On the other hand, studies show that popular science writing hasn’t
kept up with these calls for change; instead, they show that popular
science texts are still in a deficit mode and that the replacement of the
deficit model with the dialogue model ‘is more nominal than real’, as
the deficit ‘remains the default option’ in much science communication
(Trench, ‘Towards’, p. 122). For example, Schäfer cites studies showing
that popular science writers ‘exhibit a rather strong trust in science’ and
‘are not very critical towards science’ (p. 403). Although the studies he
cites are dated, ‘surveys still show that science journalists strongly view
themselves as the ones who explain science to the audience’, which sug-
gests that their views of science communication have not shifted much
(p. 403).1 These claims raise the question of how popular science writers
see the job of popular science texts. As Nelkin asks, ‘Is science only to

                                    48
                                      Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   49


be popularized, praised, and promoted?’ (‘The Science Wars’, p. 122). Or
is science writing accepting that the ‘challenges and responsibilities of
independent science journalism lie much more in proposing meaning,
or meanings, and in locating new information in relevant contexts’
(Trench, ‘How’, p. 137)? To find out, I looked at what popular science
writers say about their craft. In general, I found that while popular sci-
ence writing is still pretty solidly in deficit mode, there are shifts toward
a more CUSP (Critical Understanding of Science in Public)-oriented
approach. This chapter describes those points of view.


Practitioner Roles
Why Look at What They Say?
STS scholars agree that science writers are ‘a vital link between the
scientific world and the public’ (Winston, p. xiii) and ‘in effect, are
brokers’ (Nelkin, Selling, p. 171) who create ‘the reality of science’ for
the public (Nelkin, Selling, p. 2). But how do science writers themselves
view their roles? How do they view science and its role(s) in society?
The rest of this book infers answers to those questions from reading
popular science texts. Before that, though, I want to examine what
they say when talking directly about their craft. Because they ‘serve as a
crucial link between the activities of scientists and the public that sup-
ports such activities’ (Treise and Weigold, p. 310), it is important to get
their perspective on what they do. We also need to know what writers
think of science popularization because ‘how social agents classify their
text types or speech events…tell[s] researchers a great deal about what a
group values and recognizes as assisting in accomplishing its social pur-
poses’ (Schryer, p. 34). To find out, I look at two kinds of texts in which
science writers describe the genre, ‘how to’ texts, and introductions to
the Best American collections.
  I look at A Field Guide for Science Writers: The Official Guide of the
National Association of Science Writers in order to find out how popular
science writers describe their craft to each other. Practitioner texts like
the Field Guide are a good way to find out how writers see their jobs
because what we tell others to do speaks volumes about what we con-
sider proper courses of action; Schryer suggests that genres should be
studied ‘at the point when they are being learned’ because that is ‘one
of the few times that tacit strategies become more overt’ (p. 44). Also,
as a meta-genre, a ‘form of shared discourse about genres’ (Bawarshi
and Reiff, p. 94), practitioner texts exert a considerable influence on
50 Communicating Popular Science


the genres they describe. Bawarshi and Reiff explain the conservative
effects of such meta-genres, which ‘can be quite durable…sometimes
working against attempts to change genres within a genre system,
sometimes carried consciously or unconsciously by individuals beyond
the contexts of their use and affecting how individuals engage with
genres in different systems of activity’ (p. 94). Finally, the Field Guide is
widely used as a text2 in science writing classes, extending its influence
to future science writers.
   I supplement patterns found in the Field Guide with examples drawn
from the forewords and introductions to the Best American collections.
Where Blum et al. offer a look at what writers say about their craft to
each other, the forewords and introductions to the Best American collec-
tions offer an idealized view of what popular science writing is or could
be in how those volumes’ editors present it to their readers.
   Within these two sets of texts, I looked for two kinds of information.
One way to identify what writers think popular science writing should
do is to look at their descriptions of the job. LaFollette found that
the ‘bias, the perspective from which the communicator approaches
science—as supporter or skeptic—most determines the message the
audience will receive’ and that writers ‘can choose their roles freely:
to be investigators, skeptics, passive reporters, or promoters of science’
(p. 181). Another way is to look at what writers say about the exigence
for their writing. A genre, as Carolyn Miller explains in ‘Genre as
Social Action’, is a set of shared motives, or a way to channel indi-
vidual intentions into a socially recognizable and shared form. With
that in mind, another way to identify the purpose of popular science
writing is to see how writers describe the perceived social needs they
are trying to meet.
   Using these approaches, I found that writers tend to see themselves
in terms of the three kinds of roles LaFollette describes, which I label
boosters, translators, and critics (grouping investigators with skeptics),
with the most emphasis being on the booster role, but with some
counter-emphasis on the critic role from writers who view popular sci-
ence writing in terms consistent with a CUSP model.


Role #1: Boosters

The most common role, that of the boosters, fits with the Traditional–
Idealist construct of science. Writers overwhelmingly describe their
role and science in enthusiastic terms. Their descriptions fall into two
general categories: science writing as a celebration of the wonders of
                                      Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   51


science and science writing as a cure for some perceived lack in the
nonspecialist public.
   I begin with the wonder theme, represented by many writers in the
Field Guide. Kunzig’s chapter, for example, characterizes popular science
writing as being ‘about truth, and beauty’ and about ‘the little nuggets
of joy and delight that draw all of us, scientists and science writers alike,
to this business, when with our outsized IQs we could be somewhere
else pursuing larger slices of power, influence, and money’ (p. 127).
Goldberg claims that science is ‘the closest thing there is to humanity’s
job description’ (p. 46) and quotes Bill Nye (the “Science Guy”) who
told her that what ‘really count[s]’ is ‘the PB&J—“passion, beauty, and
joy”—of science’ (p. 46). The adjectives keep piling up: McKay Jenkins
praises one writer’s descriptions for ‘the arching wonder of the mysteries
they evoke’ (p. 233), Hotz alludes to ‘the curious wonder of the research
realm’ (p. 58), Chang crows that his job lets him ‘loll around in the
stratified airs of Wonder and Joy of Human Knowledge’ (p. 210), and
Lemonick exults that his job entails watching for ‘trends glittering at
the edges of the universe’ (p. 208).
   The Best American introductions show a similar pattern. For Angier,
the goal is to showcase science, and scientists as noble and overlooked
laborers: ‘Yes, I love science, and I can’t think of anything that is more
worth writing about, or in greater need of good explanatory writing. For
all their passion and productivity, scientists labor in profound anonym-
ity’ (p. xvii). She also wants writers to be so invested in their stories that
‘the story is part of the latticework of their cells’ because that will allow
them to ‘write with the passion of converts’ (p. xxii) so that readers feel
they ‘have learned something and fallen in love all at once’ (p. xxiv).
For Ridley, science writing is better than any novel because it offers
us ‘new mysteries, undreamt of in our philosophy’ (p. x), and Folger
apparently agrees, as he presents us with this nifty little syllogism in the
foreword to The Best American Science and Nature Writing 2003.

1. The light from both sides of the Andromeda galaxy, which is 150,000
   light-years across, hits an observer’s eye at the same time, which
   means that ‘In a single glimpse… your eyes capture light that encom-
   passes a span of 150,000 years,’ which is about how long humans
   have existed (p. x).
2. Nothing in human history and mythology ‘can match the grandeur
   of that reality’ as ‘no prophet ever imagined a universe built on such
   a scale, a cosmos so vast that fleeting light itself becomes a mere
   yardstick’ (p. x).
52 Communicating Popular Science


3. There is something that is ‘Perhaps more wondrous’ even than that:
   ‘the fact that we can actually measure its size and even pin down, to
   within a few tens of thousands of years, its age’ (p. x).

Just in case a reader missed the point—that the abilities of science are
more glorious than the Andromeda galaxy, which is itself even more
wondrous than any other ideas or abilities humans have ever come up
with—recall Folger’s urging readers to ‘sit, read, and wonder. Above all,
wonder. That’s what science is all about’ (p. xii).
  Kolata has ‘become increasingly convinced that science is the greatest
journalistic subject of our time’ (p. viii) because of the fantastic discov-
eries science has made, and says ‘The exhilaration of those discoveries,
and their impact, is what science writing can convey’ (p. ix). And for
Wilson, the goal is ‘to make science human and enjoyable without
betraying nature’, and, to do so, writers should ‘treat the scientists as
protagonists in a story that contains, at least in muted form, the mythic
elements of challenge and triumph’ (p. xvii). For these writers—at
least based on these statements—science is to be admired, praised, and
celebrated.
  Another theme that signals a ‘booster’ role is that popular science
writing exists to alleviate a range of supposed public failings. One per-
ceived problem it remedies is a lack of interest in science; Ridley, like
Angier, wants us to appreciate ‘the astonishing dedication of the indi-
vidual scientist’ and ‘the unsung heroes of science: the men and women
who put in long hours, fill in small details, and take great trouble’ on
whose work ‘great breakthroughs stand’ (p. xiv). Lemonick specifically
wants people to like astronomy as much as he does: ‘to make everyone
else—those who aren’t passionate about the inflationary universe—
share that fascination’ (p. 217). Greene looks to popular science writing
to make ‘difficult and technical subjects not only accessible but mov-
ing and compelling’ (p. xix) and to ‘cultivate excitement while greatly
deepening their [students’] working knowledge of science’s achieve-
ments’ (p. xxi). And Goldberg urges science writers to ‘Dare to be sexy’
because ‘Readers must be seduced into reading a science story’ (p. 47).
Apparently, these authors think interest in science is so low that only
sex or other nonscience stimulation will capture their interest.
  Another malady that science writing is supposed to cure is ignorance.
Dawkins suggests that popular science texts could help lawyers, judges,
and ‘politicians up to the highest level’ get better at their jobs (p. xxii),
and Wilson writes, hopefully, that ‘science writing is bound to grow
in influence, because it is the best way to bridge the two cultures into
                                      Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   53


which civilization is still split’ (p. xx). Lest readers wonder which culture
needs remediating, he adds ‘Most educated people who are not profes-
sionals in the field do not understand science and technology’ (p. xx).
   For Gleick, science writing is a cure for a kind of irrationality that
must be more common in his neck of the woods than in any neck of
the woods I have ever inhabited (or heard of, for that matter): ‘This stuff
matters. There’s a lot of confusion out there. Otherwise sane, educated
people find themselves not quite knowing whether millions of their
friends and neighbors have been abducted and probed by almond-eyed
aliens. Not a healthy state of affairs (and I don’t mean the abductions)’
(Gleick, ‘Introduction’, p. ix). Roach also worries about ignorance,
though of an unspecific nature, as she says that good narrative ele-
ments ‘are the sugar, to be all cliché about it, that makes the medicine
go down’ and adds ‘Make no mistake, good science writing is medicine.
It is a cure for ignorance and fallacy’ (p. xv–xvi). In curing these mala-
dies, ‘Good science writing peels away the blinders, generates wonder,
brings the open palm to the forehead: Oh! Now I get it!’ (Roach, p. xvi,
emphasis original). What is being ‘gotten’ is unclear.
   Dawkins invokes the power of science writing to cure not only igno-
rance but fear: ‘Science…has the power to reduce ignorance and dispel
fear. We should all read science and learn to think like scientists, not
because science is useful (though it is), but because the light of know-
ledge is wonderful and banishes the debilitating and time-wasting fear
of the dark’ (p. xiv). Ferris, of course, also sees science writing as the
‘cure’ for what he claims is a widespread ‘fear and loathing of science’
(p. v), and Lightman agrees that popular science writing is a way to over-
come the fact that ‘science terrifies some people’ (p. xii). Rebecca Skloot
and Floyd Skloot agree with others’ claims that ‘scientific illiteracy is
rampant’, and science writers ‘bear a responsibility for communicating
facts accurately and clearly, for avoiding the sensational, for educating
readers and public increasingly unversed in (and sometimes even afraid
of) science’ (p. xii). Folger also sees science writing as a ward against the
forces of irrationality and as a way to improve science’s image. He urges
people to listen to ‘cold truths from the unflinching, vast perspective of
science’ and to ‘set aside the myths and legends that still sustain—and
divide—so many of us’ (‘Foreword’, 2002, p. xiii); in another foreword
he praises Angier who ‘takes the scientific community to task for not
mounting a spirited and public defense of rationalism’ (‘Foreword’,
2005, p. xii). Like Dawkins, he worries about creationists, and about
how ‘Science—and the liberal culture of tolerance and dispassionate
inquiry that makes possible its pursuit—has many enemies’ (‘Foreword’,
54 Communicating Popular Science


2002, p. xiii). He hopes the articles in The Best American Science and
Nature Writing ‘will help win it a few more friends’ (‘Foreword’, 2002,
p. xiii). Nowhere in these descriptions is there a suggestion that science
is anything but beneficial, or that disinterest about or distrust of science
might ever stem from legitimate reasons.
   Finally, science writing is presented as a cure for civic problems,
including terrorism. Gleick, in addition to worrying that people believe
in alien abductions, also recommends science writing for its civic func-
tion, writing that ‘For better or worse, modern life demands a certain
amount of sophistication about science, if we are to function properly
as individuals and as members of the polity’ (Gleick, ‘Introduction’,
p. ix) and that ‘our technocratic age requires urgent messages from the
sometimes baffling, sometimes tumultuous frontiers of knowledge—the
place we call science’ (Gleick, ‘Introduction’, p. x). For Pinker, whose
childhood ‘favorite literary genre was the hagiography of a famous sci-
entist’ (p. xix), goals include ‘an understanding of science’ (p. xv) and
‘an extension to human affairs of the scientific mindset itself’ (p. xvii).
Indeed, the ‘amoral analysis’ of a scientist is ‘a mode of thought that we
may have to get used to if we want to improve human affairs’ (p. xviii).
   Greene also wants us to think like scientists so we can be good citizens
because ‘science and technology play an increasingly pervasive role in
everyday life…full participation in the global conversation requires
a familiarity with the major advances in science and technology as
well as an understanding of the scientific way of thought’ (p. xvi).
Apparently, these benefits extend even beyond science: ‘Even on issues
that seemingly bear no direct relation to science, a scientific mindset
can have a radical impact’ (p. xvi). His example, that statistically-
minded Americans would have understood the futility of re-counting
votes in the 2000 presidential election, is eyebrow-raising—but only
until compared with Dawkins’ example of how thinking like scientists
can not only make us more science-loving, but could even disarm ter-
rorists. Talking about an article written shortly after 11 September 2001,
he says that ‘If we all took a dose of [the authors’] no-nonsense statisti-
cal common sense, bigots like bin Laden would be impotent (think of
Germaine Greer’s robust advice to women exposed to flashers: laugh at
them)’ (p. xxiv). Indeed, the article is, he says, ‘an example of how the
scientific way of thinking might influence our lives for the better, quite
apart from the more familiar ways in which scientific methods of doing
can benefit us in practice’ because it represents ‘Carl Sagan’s candle,
and science’s power to banish our fears of the dark’ (p. xxiv, emphasis
original). Here, coming from a scientist and popular science writer, is
                                     Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   55


the solubility ethos in a nutshell, a faith in science as ‘a way to truth,
civilization, morality, and other constructive values’ (Burnham, p. 261).
No wonder Trench describes Dawkins’ work as ‘the most visible example
of an unreconstructed deficit model’ (Trench, ‘Towards,’ p. 121).
  These examples, while ranging in their opinion of precisely how sci-
ence is wonderful, all share the view that popular science writing can,
and should, help promote changes in its readership that will amend
their various failings—from the personal minor sin of not liking science
enough to the more serious civic sin of not thinking like a scientist. They
exemplify a ‘missionary zeal’ (Logan, p. 139) approach to science com-
munication and indicate that the solubility ethos is still alive and well in
science communication circles. They also show that boosters agree with a
deficit view of readers and a Traditional–Idealist view of science.
  The boosters’ view, with its adherence to ‘the affirmative default’
(Peters et al., p. 85) of popular science writing as celebration, is prob-
lematic for a few reasons. First, as shown in Chapter 2, assumptions
of public distrust and hostility toward science are not supported by
research; on the contrary, the National Science Board’s Science and
Engineering Indicators show that Americans hold science and scientists
high regard. These findings are similar to those of a Pew survey, which
found that ‘84% of Americans agree that science is having a mostly
positive effect on society with this strong agreement relatively consist-
ent across every major demographic, political, and religious segment’
(Nisbet and Scheufele, p. 1769). In addition, research shows that when
people are motivated, they can, and do, learn a great deal in particular
areas of science. Indeed, as Ziman, Irwin and Wynne, and others argue,
what really matters for CUSP is ‘knowledge in context, which emerges
from local controversies and people’s concerns’ (Bauer, ‘Survey’, p. 118),
knowledge that people develop when they have a personal interest or
stake in a given aspect or area of science.
  Second, boosterism deflects attention from real problems. Boosterism
based in a positivist understanding of science as ‘the rational pursuit of
objective “truth” about the world, which can only be good’ (Bauer et al.,
‘Public’, p. 42) obscures the aspects and applications of science that are
not good. For some writers this may well be the goal; as Matthew Nisbet
and Robert Goidel report, ‘Sociologists argue that the heavy focus on
science literacy persists because it serves an important political purpose
for scientific institutions’ (p. 422), that of keeping the focus away from
problems in science and on trying to solve a purported literacy crisis
outside of it. For those interested in CUSP, this aspect of boosterism is
a distraction, at best.
56 Communicating Popular Science


   Third, even when the goal is to support only beneficial and sound sci-
ence, an excessively glorious picture of science is ultimately harmful to
science itself as it sets up unreasonable expectations. ‘Oracular science’,
as Turney calls it, is doomed to disappoint people; if excitement about
popular science really is fading, he suggests, ‘perhaps it is because the
public has caught on to the fact that readers were promised things that
science really cannot deliver’ (Turney, pp. 85–6).
   Fourth, scientists are not the only people who can deploy the dis-
cursive defenses created by hagiographic science popularization; stories
about science become part of the common store of knowledge that
people use to understand and respond to science, and they are not
always used in the way that science boosters might want. One par-
ticularly painful example is that of Andrew Wakefield, the doctor who
claimed that vaccines cause autism, who ‘has successfully hijacked the
narrative of the brave scientist fighting against establishment dogmas,
casting himself as Galileo’; likewise, ‘creationism has dressed itself in
clothes that scientists had tailored for themselves’ (Hannam, p. 43).
Thus, although stories that glorify science can be nice in the short
term, ultimately they end up hurting it as they either create unrealistic
expectations that lead to disappointment or create an image that, when
co-opted by others, works against the best interests of the science that
image was supposed to support.


Role #2: Translators

Some writers try to take a more distanced approach, viewing their role
as that of a translator. Scientists often expect writers to take this role,
one in which the writer ‘is simply transcribing what they say’ and will
‘trust them even when they are not specific, when they make state-
ments without numbers to support them, or when [writers] ask for a
“second opinion” on their work’ (Palmerini, p. 122). Several writers
appear to agree that this is an appropriate position, or at least that the
translation metaphor accurately describes their role. For example, Glick
says that science writers in industry can not only understand scientific
facts, but ‘can make them understandable to others’ and can ‘accurately
and efficiently translate the function and value of a product or service’
(pp. 299–300). Likewise, Henrichsen’s and other writers’ work at the
National Institutes of Health included ‘translating what we learned into
plain English’ (p. 281), while Chui quotes Randy Showstack’s advice
that spending time with scientists can lead to ‘a better chance to trans-
late their work into good, colorful language’ (p. 239), and McFarling
                                      Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   57


characterizes science writers in terms of ‘their technical expertise, ability
to translate jargon, and patience with details’ (p. 244). Cook also refers
to the struggle ‘to successfully translate the arcane jargon of science into
a story for the general reader’ (p. 111). Folger concurs about ‘the hard
labor of translating the work of scientists into something that people
will pay to read’ (‘Foreword’ 2002, p. xii), and Groopman refers to the
writers in The Best American Science Writing 2010 and their ‘shared talent
for interpretation’ (p. vii). The translator metaphor is clearly popular
among popular science writers.
   The idea of the popular science writer as translator avoids some of the
drawbacks of the booster role, but is still problematic. Even when writ-
ers don’t subscribe to the complete uncritical role Palmerini describes,
the metaphor creates an image of language as a transparent container
for meaning, a neutral way to convey content. In reality, though, ‘infor-
mation is neither disembodied nor neutral. Information is rhetorical.
It is created and deployed in particular historical circumstances for the
use of particular individuals and groups’ (Bazerman, ‘Nuclear’, p. 290).
In such cases, claims of neutrality generally serve to obscure (whether
deliberately or with good intentions) the ideological underpinnings of
a given endeavor.3
   The problems with this are pointed out by another writer in the Field
Guide, Brownlee. She describes how when she started out as a science
writer in the 1980s, she and other medical writers ‘liked to talk about
ourselves as translators’ and thought the job ‘was to sort through the
medical journals, decide what was newsworthy, and then put the jar-
gon of science and statistics into language that ordinary readers could
understand’ (p. 155). She adds that she and others have done ‘a superb
job of translating and conveying information’ (p. 155)—in theory, at
least, an ideal way to communicate—but then adds a twist:

   In fact, we might have done the job too well, because in simply
   reporting each newsworthy finding in the professional journals, the
   lay press has helped sell medical products and procedures to a public
   eager for the good news about their health. The upshot is that we’ve
   inadvertently helped put a high gloss on medicine, rather than actu-
   ally keeping the enterprise honest (pp. 155–6).

Brownlee recognizes that acting as an uncritical translator means essen-
tially offering advertising for products, especially as medical science
has become more and more commercialized. As a result of this realiza-
tion, she and others have ‘gone through a period of soul searching, a
58 Communicating Popular Science


reappraisal of our role as journalists and members of the fourth estate’
(p. 155) and emerged in a new role, that of the writer as critic.


Role #3: Critics

The last role is that of the critics, the science writers who approach their
subject with both interest and skepticism. These writers tend to empha-
size a gatekeeping function (asking questions about the science itself)
or a public service function (providing readers with the information
they need to make up their own minds). Some writers combine the two,
describing the popular science writer’s job as a combination of skeptical
inquiry and reader empowerment.
   Antonio Regalado, writing about genetics research, offers a good
example of the gatekeeping function as he warns writers against being
‘complacent’ and ‘too trusting of scientists’ good intentions’ such that
they ‘forget to be skeptical’ and instead let major scientific publications
‘spoon-feed’ news to them (‘Human’, p. 119). While acknowledging the
importance of that news, Regalado also says that simply accepting and
translating what press releases say is not acceptable: ‘To be a science
“detective” requires a more critical view of things’ and a willingness to
ask about ‘hidden motives’ and ‘the scientific data’ (p. 119). While these
questions don’t usually reveal problems, ‘More often than you might
think, a little digging will turn up evidence of dodgy ethics, commercial
entanglements, or simply bad research’ (p. 119). He adds that ‘particu-
larly in biology, financial ties with biotechnology firms are now com-
mon’ and suggests that ‘At a minimum, a reporter should always ask a
scientist if he or she has financial interests in the outcome of ongoing
research’ (p. 122).
   Several other writers in the Field Guide recommend asking similar
questions and digging around to find hidden connections. For example,
Squires says to ‘Add a dash of skepticism’ especially of findings that ‘had
not gone through the peer review required for publication in a scientific
journal, were not statistically significant’ and hadn’t ‘been accepted for
publication in a scientific journal’ (p. 174). Likewise, Knudson suggests
that science writers be able to read science articles and understand sta-
tistics, thereby becoming ‘equipped to ask challenging questions that
test whether the scientific skeleton on which the study was built sup-
ports its conclusions’ (p. 3). Hall brings up similar concerns; he notes
that ‘the most important journalistic asset’ for a science writer is ‘criti-
cal judgment’ (Hall, p. 199) and remarks on ‘the annoyingly necessary
job of assessing whether the science [that sources] are promoting is
                                     Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   59


any good’ (pp. 199–200). Hotz also says to ask ‘the questions that arise
with every newsworthy scientific development’, questions that ‘center
on the validity of the work, its importance to the general public, and
whether independent scientists can vouch for it’ (p. 56). He explicitly
refers to the gatekeeping function: ‘Acting as a gatekeeper to sort the
sense from scientific nonsense, a science writer ordinarily can spend
almost as much time chasing down a misleading claim as publicizing
valid work’ (p. 56). This is writerly skepticism in full flower, with the
writer’s role including making judgments about the merit and validity
of scientific work.
   Writers like Regalado, Squires, Knudson, Hall, and Hotz say to ask
questions not because they assume they will find wrongdoing, but
because they understand that any study should be subject to critical
inquiries. Other writers go a step further, talking about the importance
not only of exercising their own judgment, but also of making sure
readers have the information they need to make their own judgments.
Chase writes of how science writers can ‘play a key role in bringing
accurate and balanced public health information to light’ (p. 164). In
the Best American series, Groopman, while rather hagiographic in some
of his descriptions of science, also argues that nonspecialists should
learn not only ‘of the mystery and marvels of scientific advancement’,
but also ‘should hear as well the voices of skeptics who question
whether the mystery has actually been solved and whether the marvel
is truly so marvelous’ (p. xiii). Likewise, Gawande talks about ‘science
writing as a public service’, including ‘“nuts and bolts stories,” stories
that reveal the scientific process itself in all its uncertainty and human
complexity’ (p. ix).
   The most CUSP-oriented writers are those who combined the gate-
keeping and public empowerment roles. In the Field Guide, Seely
describes a need to ‘ferret out the scientific studies that were sound and
those that were not’ while also representing the interests of his readers
as he let their ‘questions and concerns’ shape the questions he asked
university and government scientists (p. 52). Lewis Cope also values
both writers’ and readers’ skepticism. In his ‘Five principles of scientific
analysis’, Cope says the role of the science writer is to ‘separate the
probable truth from the probable trash’, a process that calls for writers
to have ‘some healthy skepticism, and the ability to ask good questions’
(p. 18). He also talks specifically about the need for readers to make
their own judgments; given that uncertainty is a normal (in fact, essen-
tial) part of science, Cope sees the writer’s job in terms of empowering
public action: ‘Some uncertainty need not impede crucial action if the
60 Communicating Popular Science


public understands why at best almost all a scientist can say is: “Here’s
our strong evidence that such-and-such is probably true.”’ (pp. 19–20,
emphasis original). He specifically says to make sure readers have the
information they need, noting that if a study’s findings are not ‘based
on statistically significant findings’, the writer should ‘Be leery…and
warn your readers or viewers’ (p. 20).
   Cook says to ask many questions about many things, including the
study’s significance, who will disagree with it, the evidence, and the
funding. Answers to these questions, Cook says, will ‘serve as a kind of
rudder’ and will make sure a writer doesn’t miss ‘a basic question that
readers (and editors) are bound to want answered’ (p. 112). Begos addi-
tionally says to include ethical aspects in writing about science. Begos
notes that ‘There’s a sad history of scientists, at times, misusing their
positions of authority to promote racist, sexist, or other discriminatory
behavior—and of journalists unquestioningly going along for the ride’
(p. 187). Begos cites the racism of the eugenics movement and notes
that ‘The publication of The Bell Curve in 1994 showed that academic
racism is still alive’ and that writers ‘need to be even more careful when
covering the revolution in genetics’ (p. 188) than in others. Ultimately,
Begos says, writers must share with readers ‘not just the science and
technology behind this amazing genetic revolution, but the people and
the ethics, too’ (p. 188).
   Writers such as Begos, Brownlee, and others represent a move back
toward a view of citizenship and popular science writing that character-
ized the late 1800s, one in which ‘Visions of an informed and compe-
tent citizenry also provided powerful motivations for diffusing scientific
knowledge among the masses rather than allowing it to be controlled
by an intellectual aristocracy’ (Thurs, pp. 30–1). In other words, these
writers represent a move toward the kind of critical engagement that
can make popular science writing a potent force for democratic decision
making regarding issues of science in society.


Conclusion

Critics differ from boosters and translators in ways that are not only
conducive to CUSP, but also more realistic about how science works
and how it fits into civil society. For instance, critics seem aware that,
in many cases, more knowledge ‘can lead to queries, to criticism, and
even to rejection’, and that appreciation of science does not necessarily
follow from learning more about it (Montgomery, p. 207). Montgomery
mentions that this counter-trend happens with nuclear power—the
                                     Practitioner Perspectives on their Craft   61


more people learn, the less they trust it—but studies confirm that
greater knowledge doesn’t lead to greater support in other areas. In
fact, ‘it has never been proved that the more science is communicated,
the more the level of public awareness increases, and even less so that
attitudes towards specific scientific and technological issues grow more
positive as a consequence’ (Bucchi, p. 5). Perhaps because they do not
equate knowledge with appreciation, writers who adopt the critic role
avoid the trap of ‘enlightened cognitivism’ that ‘often attributes signs
of social resistance to certain scientific advances to the public’s or the
decision-makers’ lack of scientific knowledge and not, for example, to
differences in values or in the assessment of the potential usefulness
of the scientific application in question’ (Pardo and Calvo, p. 156).
Instead, they acknowledge these other factors—ethics, applications, dif-
ferent stakeholders with different interests—and view popular science
writing as a way to engage in conversation readers from all backgrounds
and points of view.
  Given that ‘dogged defence of every feature of “the Legend”—the
stereotype of science that idealizes its every aspect—is almost as dam-
aging as the attack it is supposed to be fending off’ (Ziman, p. 2), it is
important for popular science texts to present science as it is, not as
some idealized, positivist vision would have it be. In doing so, they
can also promote a new social contract. Although popular science texts
cannot by themselves effect the changes needed for the new social con-
tract to be forged, they can contribute to it by helping create new pos-
sibilities. As Bawarshi and Reiff explain, ‘through our social practices,
we reproduce the very social structures that subsequently make our
actions necessary, possible, recognizable, and meaningful, so that our
practices reproduce the very structures that consequently call for these
practices’ (p. 80). One place our social practices are codified is in texts,
such as popular science writing, and to change those texts is to change
the potential terms of the conversation.
  The range found throughout the Field Guide and the Best American
books’ forewords and introductions is indicative of the range of roles
that popular science writers envision for themselves and their field.
This range also reflects the tension outlined in Chapter 2 between
Traditional–Idealist and Realist–Skeptical constructs of science, and
between the corresponding PAST (Public Appreciation of Science
and Technology) and CUSP views of what science writing should do.
Bringing this tension to the surface helps us understand how much the
PAST tendency in popular science texts is there by design. While some
of the excesses of popular science texts are no doubt due to pressures
62 Communicating Popular Science


of the publishing industry—a point that Nelkin (Selling Science), Turney,
and others have made—analysis of the Field Guide suggests that the
deficit model lives on at least in part because many popular science
writers believe in it.
   Identifying Traditional–Idealist and Realist–Skeptical views of science
in practitioner texts is not difficult, but seeing how these values are
articulated in popular science texts can be, as values are often tacit, with
the connections between points of view and the textual strategies that
suggest and promote them hidden from view. It is the role of the rheto-
rician to reveal what may not seem so clear to practitioners; in fact,
the resources they draw on and strategies they use ‘seem like “common
sense” to their users, but once they are opened up for critical reflection,
then even their users might want to challenge some of their practices
and attendant ideological values’ (Schryer, p. 45). Therefore, the next
five chapters seek to identify the ‘tacit sets of resources’ in popular sci-
ence texts, partly to ‘make them more visible, but also to open them up
for critique’ (Schryer, p.45) so that writers who want to foster CUSP will
have more resources at hand for doing so.
Part II
Applications
This page intentionally left blank
5
Boundary Work: Presenting Science
in Context




The preface and introduction said this book would talk about how
popular science writing can do a better job of fostering critical conver-
sations about science, especially science that affects, or is of interest
to, civil society. After pausing for few chapters to talk about rhetorical
theory (Chapter 2), the history of scientific writing (Chapter 3), and
what popular science writers say about their craft (Chapter 4), I am turn-
ing my attention to that promised topic with these five ‘application’
chapters. The first four chapters in this part look at relationships—the
relationships between the sciences and the other parts of civil society
(this chapter), between different kinds of expertise (Chapter 6), between
writers and readers (Chapter 7), and between readers and science
(Chapter 8). The final application chapter draws together strands from
Chapters 5–8 in a discussion of applying CUSP (Critical Understanding
of Science in Public) principles to controversial areas of science and
questions of risk.
   I begin Part II with demarcation, understood as the discursive activi-
ties shaping relationships between the sciences and the other parts of
civil society because how that boundary is constructed affects the rest of
the relationships analyzed in later chapters. Specifically, the traditional
social contract is based on a separation of science from other arenas;
that separation also underlies the PAST model of science communica-
tion, as all elements of the PAST model flow from the privileging of the
science side in the presumed science-society split. In contrast, a new
social contract involves challenging boundary lines, showing how sci-
entific and social arenas overlap and blur together, as a necessary step
toward greater critical engagement in all the relationships I discuss.
   This chapter sets the pattern for the rest of Part II. After this introduc-
tory section, it does five things. The first section explains boundary work

                                     65
66 Communicating Popular Science


in terms of the PAST (Public Appreciation of Science and Technology)
CUSP continuum. The second section revisits the practitioner texts ana-
lyzed in Chapter 4, focusing on how they show science as separate from,
or continuous with, its material and epistemic contexts. The third and
fourth sections describe a variety of CUSP-conducive rhetorical strate-
gies, illustrating each one with a brief analysis of an exemplary popular
science text, and then offer an integrated analysis of how several strate-
gies work together within one popular science text. Finally, the conclud-
ing section discusses how the strategies in the chapter can help us move
toward the new social contract.


Boundary Work and the PAST–CUSP Continuum

Demarcation efforts are generally most energetic and contested when
they relate to arenas that exercise cultural authority. ‘Science’ is a
category worth arguing over because calling something scientific has
implications for whether or not it receives various social goods, from
tangible things like grant funding to intangibles like the high level of
respect afforded to scientists. For example, scientists have been arguing
over whether or not the space program is doing ‘science’ since at least
the start of the Apollo Program in 1961. At the time, ‘the scientific
establishment fought the Apollo program on the grounds that it was
political showbiz and not science’ (Munevar quoted in Taylor, p. 134),
and this point of view is currently being propounded by Nobel-winning
physicist Steven Weinberg. In ‘The Missions of Astronomy’ (published
in The New York Review of Books and in The Best American Science Writing
2010), Weinberg inveighs against ‘a vastly expensive NASA program
that often masquerades as science. I refer, of course, to the manned
space flight program’ (p. 29). He is not simply saying that human space
travel is a less cost-effective way of learning about the world—though
that is part of his argument—but that it is not science at all.1
  Demarcation is also used to distinguish science from other arenas,
though in an oddly flexible way. Gieryn, in Cultural Boundaries of
Science: Credibility on the Line, explains that science is simultaneously
held up as something apart from society and as something beneficial
to society, and that which of these gets emphasis depends on what is
at stake. When scientists seek autonomy, Gieryn explains, ‘then science
gets “purified,” carefully demarcated from all political and market con-
cerns, which are said to pollute truth’ (p. 23). However, he says, when
scientists seek more resources, ‘science gets “impurified,” erasing the
borders or spaces between truth and policy relevance or technological
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   67


panaceas’ (p. 23). Gieryn further argues that to understand how sci-
ence’s cultural authority is created and maintained, we need to look at
how science is communicated, and interpreted and understood in the
culture at large. Thinking about this specifically in terms of popular
science writing, we can identify demarcation by looking for two things:
the ways that sciences are acknowledged to be materially interdepend-
ent with their contexts and the degree to which writers use ‘various
epistemic markers that delimit the nature of science’ (Taylor, p. 9).
   At the PAST end of the spectrum, it is assumed that, in Taylor’s words,
there is ‘a unidirectional process of influence, from the technical to the
public, without a clear recognition of the synergistic mutuality of the
two domains’ (p. 128). This sharp demarcation shows up in the tradi-
tional social contract’s metaphor of science as a reservoir, nourishing
but untouched by society. It is also apparent in claims by scientists that
corporate funding has no effect on research outcomes, a claim belied,
in many cases, by independent comparisons of privately and publicly
funded studies.2
   In contrast, the CUSP model recognizes the mutuality of sciences
and their surroundings, and the fact that in post-academic science the
always-blurry lines are getting even blurrier. For example, the legend
says scientists explore whatever seems important and interesting to
them. However, the reality is that funding affects everything from the
choice of topics—as in the ExxonMobil/Stanford case mentioned in
Chapter 3—to decisions about how to publish findings. Schenkelaars
describes this effect on biotechnology research, as even in the 1980s
‘research institutions were increasingly forced to get research contracts
from commercial organisations, to apply for patents on inventions and
to start companies’ (p. 206). More than two decades later, ‘concentra-
tion in the agro-food biotechnology, food-processing industries and
retail sectors had further increased. Moreover, ties between industries,
governments and research institutions had grown closer’ (p. 213). The
point is not that such ties are inherently good or bad (though, like many
others, I have reservations about them), but that it is misleading to rep-
resent science as somehow separable from its material environment.
   The direct effects of funding on science are easy to see, but no less
important is the epistemic continuity between sciences and their con-
texts.3 As discussed in Chapter 2, scientific practices and the knowledge
they produce are always a product of a specific time and place. The
fact that much scientific knowledge endures across different times and
locations attests to the importance of cold science, but the ‘coldness’ of
some scientific information does not negate the context boundedness
68 Communicating Popular Science


of how scientific knowledge is made, tested, rejected, accepted, and
revised. Against the belief ‘that our senses and our science directly
convey information about the material world’, Hawking and Mlodinow
