argue that ‘There is no way to remove the observer—us—from our
perception of the world’ (p. 187).
   Hawking and Mlodinow are physicists, but their point applies equally
well to ecology, and is similar to the one that Journet makes in her arti-
cle about Victorian versus modernist narratives shaping ecological meta-
phors in the early twentieth century. As explained in Chapter 2, both
Clements and Gleason based their theories on data, but the narrative
Clements created from his data was ‘based on assumptions about the
orderliness of natural systems and a balance of nature’ (Journet, p. 452),
while Gleason ‘produced a story less predictable and directional and
more dependent on chance and history than Clements’s’ (p. 456). These
narratives reflected two competing early-twentieth-century worldviews
at the time, and the shift in their relative importance demonstrates how
changes in science echo larger social changes over time. Journet writes
that while ‘Holistic answers to questions about complex community
systems formed ecology’s first paradigm’, there was also ‘a reductive cri-
tique’ that has ‘since become paradigmatic itself’ (Journet, pp. 451–2).
Clements and Gleason, and the change from a holistic to a reductive
root metaphor in ecology, illustrate how cultural mindsets affect indi-
viduals and, in turn, affect the science those individuals do.4 Given all
of these factors affecting science, from the material to the cultural/epis-
temic, a blurring, questioning, and crossing of boundaries is important
for CUSP and for the new social contract, especially with hot science,
such as new research, and new challenges to previously-cold knowledge.


Boundary Work Described in Practitioner Texts

To find out how popular science writers view the boundary work
that popular science texts do, I looked at the Field Guide and the Best
American introductions with two questions in mind:

• How do popular science writers talk about or imply separation of
  science from other arenas?
• How do they talk about or imply continuity of science with other arenas?

Not surprisingly, given the way many writers talk about science as some-
thing exalted and wonderful (see Chapter 4), practitioner texts often
imply or even state outright that science is in a class by itself. These
                               Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   69


PAST model texts fall into two categories: those that idolize science for
its epistemic superiority and those that demarcate more subtly by ignor-
ing contextual factors beyond the bounds of the research arena within
which given scientific work takes place.
   Claiming epistemic superiority for science—privileging it as the ulti-
mate and best source of knowledge about reality—separates science
from other forms of knowledge-making. Dawkins, for example, claims
that science is ‘the systematic method by which we apprehend what
is true about the real world’ and that ‘if you want to know what is
true about reality, science is the only way’ (pp. xiv–v, emphasis added).
Likewise, Wilson defines science as ‘the totality of what humanity can
verify about the real world, testable by repeated experiment or factual
observation, bound to related information by general principles’ (p. xvi,
emphasis added), implying that scientific knowledge is, by definition,
true. Furthermore, there is no room for social constructionism if, as
Wilson claims, scientific results are ‘delivered raw’ (pp. xvii–xviii).
   Along the same lines, Kunzig says that the ‘real story’ in science writ-
ing ‘is what makes it different from other human affairs, not the same’
(p. 127, emphasis original), suggesting that the separation of science
from other kinds of knowledge-making is not only a given, it is the point
of science writing. Kunzig also exemplifies another way of elevating the
intellectual status of science: talking about scientists as special people
whose work stands out from that of other, ordinary thinkers.5 He says
that although scientists may be ‘ordinary people (most of them)’ they
‘are doing something extraordinary. They’re out there beavering away
on the edge of knowledge’ (p. 131). This characterization suggests that
while scientists are (mostly) like other people, science is qualitatively
different from other activities. Other examples abound: Lemonick says
that scientists seek ‘to answer the most profound questions imaginable’
(p. 216), Pinker describes them as ‘passionate eccentrics’ (p. xvi), Skloot
and Skloot say they are driven by ‘obsession’ to make their ‘important
discoveries’ (p. xi), and so on.
   Another form of demarcation is to present science as acontextual or
as existing only in the realm of scientific research. In this vein, Siegfried
defines ‘the background that provides context’ as ‘basic information
and previous relevant findings’ (p. 17)—in other words science as the
context for science. Likewise, Rodgers, writing about supposed crises in
medical science, refers to context as a knowledge of ‘what’s going right
as well as what’s still going wrong’ in medicine (p. 275). Each of these
writers, while listing ‘context’ as an important element of science writ-
ing, provides a view of context that never leaves the realm of science.
70 Communicating Popular Science


   Similarly, many Best American introductions separate science from
its material and political context, for example Groopman writes that
‘some readers may be surprised by the appearance of selections from
The Nation and Salon, usually viewed as political publications’ (p. xiii),
as if science has no relationship to politics. Bilger says that scientists are
leaving ‘science proper’ and cites as examples the fact that ‘microbiol-
ogists have defected to the biotech industry in droves; mathematicians
are programming computer games and chemists are creating laundry
detergents’ (p. x). Describing the move of scientists into industrial jobs
as a ‘defection’ signals belief that science is what happens in enclaves
(presumably academic) that exist apart from corporate applications
and uses. When ‘providing context’ means situating a particular story
within the context of science as a whole, or in the particular scientific
field, science is its own and only context. When scientists who work
in industry are seen as having ‘defected’ from science, that context is
restricted to public sector science.
   In a variant on science being its only context, some texts evoke the
reservoir metaphor of the traditional social contract by depicting science
affecting society without acknowledging any reciprocal influence. In the
Field Guide, this trend begins with the editors, whose opening ‘Editors’
Note’ says that since the first edition of the book, science writing has
become ‘at once more esoteric’ as science has grown more complex, and
‘more a part of daily life’ (Blum et al., p. vii). They add that ‘Some of the
leading ideas in today’s political marketplace…are informed by scientific
ideas’ (Blum et al., p. vii). In addition, science is everywhere, affecting
all of us. Blum later reminds us that science is ‘essential to everyday life’
(‘Ten’, p. 27); Seely says ‘science in one form or another permeates every
corner of our lives’ (p. 50), and Kathryn Brown says that ‘from cloning
and stem cells to space exploration, food, and the environment, the
visibility of science in society is strong’ (p. 80). The same trend shows
in the Best American introductions in the contrast between a glut of
talk about how much effect science has on daily life and a lack of talk
about how other arenas affect science, for example editors Skloot and
Skloot repeatedly mention that ‘Science is everywhere, and we wanted
stories that showed just how present and personal it is, how intimately
it affects everyone’s lives’ (p. ix), and the need to ‘show readers how sci-
ence impacts them’ (p. x) without any references to reciprocal effects.
   Throughout these comments we see science affecting politics and
society, but being unaffected by them, and this one-way representation,
in which science is presented as ‘an essential backdrop to a larger human
story’ (Skloot and Skloot, p. ix), but the reverse is not true, discouraging
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   71


attention to science-making activities and what shapes them. This is a
form of demarcation work that upholds the image of science as a black
box, with ‘its hegemonic otherness sustained’ (Michael, p. 331). In other
words, contextual demarcation reinforces epistemic demarcation.
   Moving along the spectrum toward CUSP are texts that show the
material contexts for science, texts that include the cultural–epistemic
contexts, and texts that do both. In the Field Guide, Regalado offers two
chapters which recognize that material influences—never far from sci-
ence even in the best of times—are now an inextricable part of much
research. In his first chapter, ‘Investigative Reporting’, Regalado writes
that ‘there’s often more to science than just science. While theories
should be based on data, in practice science can be heavily influenced
by factors ranging from money to researchers’ egos’ (p. 121). In his
chapter on ‘Human Genetics’, he puts this in concrete terms by pointing
out that ‘it’s very often new technology that determines what research
gets done, as well as the agenda of funding agencies’ (p. 190). He
emphasizes the importance of material condition when he repeats that
‘The tools dictate the science’ (p. 190). Hall also mentions the need to
‘explore the deeper scientific history, corporate interests, and personal
rivalries that shaped the present-day debate’ (p. 198), and Squires is
explicit about how much influence interested industries have on nutri-
tion research and the need to ‘make it a point to indicate when industry
has sponsored research’ (p. 173). Further acknowledging the blurring
of lines between commercial and academic research, she warns ‘don’t
think that these connections between industry and scientists are limited
to obscure, backwater universities. Some very well known research-
ers receive support from major industry groups’ (p. 173). Brownlee is
equally clear about the nature of post-academic science in the medical
arena: ‘As medicine has become increasingly commercial and political,
medical writers have increasingly assumed the role of critic and watch-
dog’, scrutinizing everything from ‘clinical trial design’ to ‘the role
that industry plays in driving medical science’ (p. 156). These writers
acknowledge the importance of placing science within its material and
sociopolitical context—a placement that softens the perceived boundary
fencing off the scientific from the public spheres.
   While most writers talk about connections of science to its context
in material terms, a few talk about science being affected by its histori-
cal and cultural milieu, and discuss knowledge in social constructionist
terms. Brownlee does this, as noted in Chapter 4, and Zimmer’s chapter
of the Field Guide also says to bring in contextual factors that show how
culture has affected science; the examples he provides are ‘how Aristotle
72 Communicating Popular Science


influenced Darwin’ and ‘how alchemy helped give rise to a science of
the brain’ (p. 87). Shute also says that readers ‘need to know the broader
context’, including ‘how the science fits into human history’ (p. 37). In
fact, although Shute says she doesn’t have more than a few paragraphs
in which to show the complexity of an issue, ‘those few paragraphs
may be the most important ones. They show readers not only how the
science has developed—what’s worked and what hasn’t—but also how
the changing attitudes of the public, the imperatives of culture, change the
science’ (p. 38, emphasis added). Similarly, Hall says in writing about a
controversy to ‘examine its basic assumptions’ (p. 199), and points out
ways that different definitions, for example of what ‘success’ looks like,
can cause people to reach different conclusions about the same observed
phenomenon. These writers recognize that scientific research has par-
ticular aims in mind, and that these aims, and the research they guide,
are no less culturally embedded than other knowledge-making activities.
   Ziman points out that ‘Even in its most exploratory mode, scientific
research is always carried out according to a conscious plan’ (p. 14), and
for popular science texts to promote CUSP, writers need to recognize
that the plan guiding research does not emerge ex nihilo from an acon-
textual place of pure objectivity, and implying that it does—that science
is not driven by particular needs and interests—makes science appear
to be something it cannot be. Thus, when Dawkins refers to science as
‘useful’ (p. xiv) and praises ‘science’s impact’ on the world (p. xix), while
also saying that when science ‘arouses fears’ it is ‘usually because of a
confusion with technology’ (p. xiv), he attributes benefits to science, via
its application in the form of technology, while claiming that problems
come from technology alone and not from science.
   While some chapters in the Field Guide apparently agree with this
double standard, others urge popular science writers to create more
realistic representations of science. Some chapters say to acknowledge
the post-academic nature of science by showing material but not epis-
temic interactions between scientific and other spheres, while a few
are fully CUSP in advocating for a social constructionist understanding
of science. The next section shows how popular science writers who
foster CUSP deploy a range of strategies to show the blurriness of lines
between science and society.


Boundary Work in Popular Science Texts

The practitioner texts that say to show science in its full context have
some suggestions about how to accomplish this goal. Analyzing popular
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   73


science texts themselves also reveals a variety of strategies for construc-
tive boundary-crossing work. In this section, I build on the suggestions
from practitioners, draw on science studies scholarship, and provide
examples from popular science texts to elaborate on specific strategies.
Following this individual treatment of each strategy, I provide an inte-
grated analysis of how they come together in Elizabeth Kolbert’s ‘The
Sixth Extinction?’.
   The context that texts create for readers, the context in which they
show science happening, can include the ‘whole network of social and
epistemic practices where scientific beliefs actually emerge and are sus-
tained’ (Ziman, p. x). These contexts shows up in popular science texts
in a few ways—as information about the material and sociopolitical
conditions under which science is done, in discussions of the cultural–
epistemic effects on science of its sociocultural milieu, and in texts that
show the epistemic continuity between science and other social spheres.

CUSP 1: Whitty Illustrates How to Talk About Material/
Sociopolitical Effects on Science
Whitty’s ‘BP’s Deep Secrets’ demonstrates one way that popular sci-
ence texts can talk about material and sociopolitical effects on science.
Writing about the Deepwater Horizon oil spill, she describes three ways
that commercial and political interests intersect with science. One of
these ways has to do with how the potential for profit drives what kind
of research is done. Whitty describes the work of Luis Vega, manager of
the Hawaii National Marine Renewable Energy Center at the University
of Hawaii-Manoa, who ‘has a new grant, via the Department of Energy
and Lockheed Martin, to essentially see if the technology is suitable
for commercial investment’ (p. 102). In this simple statement, Whitty
first sums up a driving dynamic in the world of post-academic science.
Second, she shows the perils of corporate money in science when she
describes how funding can come with strings attached; among those
not ready to cope with the spill are ‘the scientists whose research is paid
for by the oil cowboys’ (p. 86) and among BP’s other sins in handling
the spill is their ‘buying the silence of scientists with lucrative pay and
confidentiality clauses’ (p. 91).
   And yet, on the third point, Whitty also shows how science also
benefits from developments in the private sector. Talking about the
work of marine ecologist Kelly Benoit-Bird and physical oceanographer
Margaret McManus, she notes that ‘Ironically, the tools [they use] were
developed by the offshore oil and gas industry and the military’ (p. 98).
Benoit-Bird explains the trickle-down effect with technology in which,
74 Communicating Popular Science


as oil and gas wells became deeper and deeper, ‘the once-prohibitively
expensive gear attending them became cheaper and more accessible,
to the point where the smallest players, the research scientists like
Margaret and me, can now afford some of it’ (quoted in Whitty, p. 99).
   In ‘BP’s Deep Secrets’ as a whole, Whitty demonstrates how popular
science texts can show that science is a ‘part of the practices of human
communities’ and, as such, it ‘takes a dynamic and changing course, is
penetrated by the experiences and interests, conflicts and power rela-
tions present in social life’ (Calsamiglia, p. 142). The piece is complex
because it shows reasons for seeking private money, as well as the prob-
lems this can bring. Whitty does not offer a solution to these problems,
but she alerts readers to one facet of how science works today.

CUSP 2: Corson Shows Knowledge-Making as a Process
Representations of knowledge-making helps readers understand that
‘research is not just discovery. It is conscious action to acquire a particu-
lar kind of knowledge for some particular purpose’ and that ‘scientific
research is always carried out according to a conscious plan’ (Ziman,
p. 14, emphasis original). Thus, popular science texts open the door
for CUSP when they talk about how scientific knowledge is devel-
oped. Trevor Corson’s ‘Stalking the American Lobster’ illustrates the
knowledge-making process by showing three communities—laboratory
scientists, field ecologists, and lobstermen—trying to understand lobster
population dynamics.
   One way Corson shows scientific research as a process is by describing
what people do physically. In the case of lobster population research,
the variety of activities is itself instructive. Two researchers spend time
in a submarine, using lasers to measure lobsters in deep water, and
one of them also studies lobster behavior in an artificial habitat in a
laboratory. A different ecologist focuses on tiny lobsters and invents a
sort of vacuum-cum-counting device that sucks the baby lobsters out
of the water, counts them, and spits them back. Yet another ecologist
develops a lobster trap with a built-in recording device that lets him
study how lobsters act as they enter (and often leave) the trap, and
another looks at how sea currents move lobster larvae from place to
place. Even the lobstermen involved in the research wear many hats,
as in Corson’s description of Fernald as ‘an oceanographer, a sea-floor
geologist, and a detective’ (p. 142) with years of experience learning
to understand lobsters and their lifecycles. Although many popular
science texts show the physical settings in which research takes place,
Corson goes a step further by talking about how these settings affect
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   75


the research itself, both enabling and constraining what scientists can
find out.
  In addition to showing the range of activities involved in constructing
a picture of lobster populations, Corson shows the importance of tools
for knowledge-making in science. As noted, researchers develop physi-
cal tools (such as the video trap and the vacuum), and they also develop
conceptual tools, for example one researcher ‘developed a record
keeping system’ (p. 152) and teamed up with two others ‘to design an
alternative to the government’s modeling system’ (p. 154). Especially
important for this aspect of scientific knowledge making is the fact
that Corson shows that models are only as good as their assumptions.
Regarding the government’s model, he explains that

  Fisheries scientists who gauge the effects of commercial lobster har-
  vesting do so using techniques originally designed for tracking fish
  populations. Because fish are elusive and hard to study in the wild,
  estimates of how well their populations are faring rely heavily on
  mathematical models. But lobsters aren’t fish. Many of them dwell in
  shallow coastal water and are easy to observe, though until recently
  few scientists had bothered to observe them. (p. 139)

This critique, combined with Corson’s description of the model devel-
oped by ecologists and lobstermen, might seem to suggest that the
problem is bad models versus good models. However, Corson also shows
that the new, presumably better, model is still a work in process; it ‘had
yet to be proved predictive’ when the researchers decided to announce
some of what they had learned (p. 156), and was to be tested further,
as ‘the catches of the coming decade will reveal whether the system the
ecologists have developed is indeed predictive’ (pp. 157–8). What the
article suggests, then, is not that one model is bad and another good,
but that all models are subject to revision, as is the knowledge they help
scientists produce.
  Corson shows the complexity and number of factors involved just
in understanding population dynamics in one species. In the process
of showing scientists and fishermen interacting with lobsters and with
each other, Corson creates an image of science as a set of dynamic,
socially embedded actions in which many kinds of knowledge are nec-
essary to generate an accurate picture. Showing the behind-the-scenes
work is especially important with hot science; however, given that
sometimes even cold science gets warmed up again as people think
of new questions or devise new methods for testing it, showing how
76 Communicating Popular Science


knowledge-making works in science is important for CUSP-oriented
popular science writing in general.

CUSP 3: Mann Includes Cultural Effects on Knowledge-Making
Identifying cultural–epistemic effects on science is not quite as easy as
identifying material or sociopolitical effects, especially because such
influences are often much clearer in hindsight than they are the time.
Mann’s ‘1491’ shows how writers can recognize those effects, both his-
torically and as they continue to the present time. In this article, Mann
describes current debates in anthropology and archaeology about the
size of the indigenous population in the Americas before Columbus
arrived. The argument between the ‘low counters’ and the ‘high
counters’ is, on its surface, about the numbers each group claims—
figures range from a low of 1.8 million people in North America to a
high of 18 million—and about the methods and methodologies being
used to calculate those numbers, and to gather and interpret the data
being used to argue for a particular figure. That debate is itself fascinat-
ing, but what makes ‘1491’ interesting is how Mann shows that the sci-
entific debate cannot be separated from the cultural contexts in which
it began and continues.
   Regarding its origins, Mann shares a nineteenth-century historian’s
quote that the native peoples were ‘feeble barbarians, destitute of com-
merce and of political connection’ (p. 38) who could not have had the
civilization some scholars claim. This historian’s preconceptions—the
prevailing view of his time—kept him from seeing otherwise. And,
Mann adds, this ‘characterization lasted, largely unchanged, for more
than a century…As late as 1987, American History: A Survey, a stand-
ard high school textbook by three well-known historians, described
the Americas before Columbus as “empty of mankind and its works”’
(p. 38). Mann also explains that the strength of the ‘empty’ continent
view was given a boost in 1910 by Smithsonian Institution ethnog-
rapher James Mooney’s conclusion ‘that in 1491 North America had
1.15 million inhabitants’ (p. 30). Mann adds that ‘Mooney’s glittering
reputation ensured that most subsequent researchers accepted his figure
uncritically’ (p. 30).
   Although hindsight shows clearly how cultural biases affected the
work of scholars whose pronouncements were, in their own time, con-
sidered reputable, Mann also shows cultural forces at work in the debate
even now. For example, the debate between two leading archaeologists
shows how political issues affect what scientists are comfortable say-
ing and what stance some of them take toward each others’ scholarly
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   77


claims: the debate, Mann reports, ‘has featured vituperative references
to colonialism, elitism, and employment by the CIA’ (p. 44) because of
the potential uses to which their findings might be put.
  So far this has demonstrated the interplay of human-centered
research and human cultures, but Mann also ties the issue to other
areas of science. The argument about pre-Columbian indigenous civili-
zations—how large they were and how people lived—has implications
for how to interpret much ecological research happening today. Mann
points out that much environmental work

  is animated, consciously or not, by what William Denevan, a geog-
  rapher at the University of Wisconsin, calls, polemically, ‘the pris-
  tine myth’—the belief that the Americas in 1491 were an almost
  unmarked, even Edenic land, ‘untrammeled by man,’ in the words
  of the Wilderness Act of 1964. …Yet if the new view is correct and
  the work of humankind was pervasive, where does that leave efforts
  to restore nature? (p. 28)

In other words, findings in archaeology could potentially overturn a
long-held understanding of indigenous populations in the Americas—
an understanding that provides the substrate for many ecological
research projects. By calling attention to this possibility, Mann is break-
ing down the purported wall between science and its cultural context,
showing how the two permeate each other.


Boundary Work in Kolbert’s ‘The Sixth Extinction?’

Kolbert’s ‘The Sixth Extinction?’ illustrates material and cultural–
epistemic effects on science and provides insights into how scientific
knowledge making happens. The article describes the mass extinction
currently under way, focusing on the particularly puzzling global die-off
of amphibian species.
  On a seemingly mundane level, Kolbert reminds readers that research
is dependent on funding, for example she mentions that El Valle
Amphibian Conservation Center in Panama ‘is financed largely by the
Houston Zoo, which initially pledged twenty thousand dollars to the
project and has ended up spending ten times that amount’ (p. 206).
Kolbert also shows how scientists are affected by social factors, such
as the tendency for researchers to avoid controversial research topics.
When one amphibian researcher was looking for a research project,
for example, she ‘purposefully tried to steer clear of the controversy’
78 Communicating Popular Science


because, in the researcher’s words, ‘the last thing you want to do is get
involved when you don’t know what’s going on’ (p. 211). These exam-
ples fly directly in the face of the image of scientists as driven only by
curiosity and the quest for truth, unaffected by mundane matters, such
as money and reputation.
   When researchers did start investigating the amphibian die-off,
Kolbert shows that their progress was erratic and confusing, a messy
process involving researchers in different fields all over the world. She
describes how when ‘researchers first noticed that something odd was
happening to amphibians, the evidence didn’t seem to make sense’
(p. 210). Instead of leading researchers in a logical march to the truth,
as a Traditional–Idealist construct of science would suggest evidence
should, ‘as the number of examples increased, the evidence only
seemed to grow more confounding’ (p. 210). Some of the loss could
be attributed to factors like habitat fragmentation and other effects of
human development, but even these factors did not seem enough to
explain what was happening. Eventually, researchers identified another
culprit, a fungus of a type that ‘had never been known to attack verte-
brates’ (p. 214).
   Instead of treating this finding as the great ‘aha!’ moment that char-
acterizes some ‘gee whiz’ science writing, though, Kolbert digs deeper,
showing the collaborative effort by many scientists to construct a
picture of what might be happening. She describes four stages in the
process. First, two pathologists ‘identified a weird microorganism grow-
ing on dead frogs’ and learned that this type of fungus could affect
vertebrates. Second, they sent the fungus to a specialist who cultured
it for them and sent it back. Third, once they had the cultured fungus,
the pathologists tested their hypothesis by exposing healthy frogs to
it. When the frogs died, they had a discovery that ‘explained many of
the data that had previously seemed so puzzling’ (p. 214). However,
this was not the end of the story. Kolbert next introduces a different
pathologist who wanted to know whether and how the fungus spread
around the globe. Testing a hypothesis that it might have spread with
a species of frog that had been bred and sold for medical purposes for
several decades (e.g., for use in pregnancy tests in the 1940s and 1950s),
he collected and tested samples of the frog species, both live and also
from museum collections. His finding that the frog was, in fact, carrying
the virus in the early 1900s, and that it can live with a fungus that kills
other amphibians, established the probable vector of the disease. Even
at this stage, Kolbert does not end with a triumphant declaration that
the mystery has been solved, the obdurate nature of reality revealed; she
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   79


connects these researchers’ work to that being done in other parts of the
world on the questions that remain unanswered.
   In addition to digging into the nature of research more than many
writers do, Kolbert goes a step further as she shows the connections
between the broader culture and the development of scientific con-
cepts through her description of changes over 300 years in concepts of
extinction. In the process, she does two things: she shows how theories
guide interpretation of evidence and she does so without falling into a
Whiggish trap of depicting the now-debunked theories as having been
somehow unscientific. She begins in the eighteenth century, when
extinction was ‘a much contested concept’ and ‘even as extraordinary
fossils were being unearthed and put on exhibit, the prevailing view was
that species were fixed, created by God for all eternity. If the bones of
a strange creature were found, it must mean that that creature was out
there somewhere’ (p. 203).
   In the nineteenth century, scientists started talking about catastro-
phism—the idea that missing species must have been wiped out by
some cataclysmic event; this was the view promoted by Cuvier, a French
naturalist and zoologist, and promoted in the English translation with
an introduction by a Scottish naturalist who ‘interpreted it as proof of
Noah’s flood’ (p. 204). Darwin knew Cuvier’s work and shared his belief
in extinction, but Darwin didn’t believe, as Cuvier did, that extinction
could happen suddenly. In fact, he thought that it was a slower process
than speciation, and On the Origin of Species ‘heaped scorn on the cata-
strophist approach’ (p. 204). Darwin’s ‘resistance to catastrophism meant
that he couldn’t accept what the fossils seemed to be saying’ (p. 215)—
that catastrophes can be, and have been, responsible for some extinc-
tions. This mistaken interpretation, which made perfect sense given the
understanding of extinction and evolution prevalent at the time, per-
sisted for nearly 100 years. It became solidified into disciplinary biases,
such that Kolbert describes the uniformitarian view’s enduring influence
on research into the 1960s as ‘paleontologists continued to give talks
with titles like “The Incompleteness of the Fossil Record”’ (p. 216).
   One geologist who was taught to see the world through a uniformitar-
ian lens, Walter Alvarez, would end up reversing the century-long misap-
prehension, but not until after struggling with the disciplinary blinders
himself. Kolbert explains that ‘Having been taught the uniformitarian
view, Alvarez wasn’t sure what to make of what he was seeing, because
the change, he later recalled, certainly “looked very abrupt”’ (p. 216).
Even when he did start to think in catastrophist terms, positing that the
dinosaurs’ extinction might have been triggered by an asteroid impact,
80 Communicating Popular Science


his colleagues, steeped in the same uniformitarian theory he had been
taught, responded ‘with incredulity’ (p. 217).6
   Resistance continued for a decade, even as ‘evidence in favor of an
enormous impact kept accumulating’ (p. 217), until, finally, in 1990,
scientists found an enormous impact crater in the Yucatan. In 1991
‘that crater was dated, and discovered to have been formed at precisely
the time the dinosaurs died off’ (p. 217), confirming the theory that
Alvarez (by then working with his father, the physicist Luis Alvarez) had
been promoting. This discovery, Kolbert explains, ‘wreaked havoc with
the uniformitarian idea of extinction’ and put a new interpretive spin
on what was known about evolution and extinction; ‘The fossil record,
it turned out, was marked by discontinuities because the history of life
was marked by discontinuities’ (p. 218). In this section of the article,
Kolbert shows how interpretations later found to be incorrect were per-
fectly reasonable given what was known, and how that knowledge was
framed, at the time. In doing so, she demonstrates why historically situ-
ated representation is important to a CUSP. Because it emphasizes that
decisions about what is true are always made in a particular context,
based on particular information and conceptual tools, such representa-
tions signal that even when interpretations later turn out to be wrong,
that does not necessarily mean the science was bad.
   In sum, ‘The Sixth Extinction?’ does not, as many popular science
texts do, paint an unrealistic picture of science as having direct access to
natural reality, of stripping away the veils until the truth is revealed in a
process during which each veil is recognized as a veil and the truth, when
reached, is recognized as the truth. Instead, it shows how scientific knowl-
edge is made. For example, it shows that research involves many people,
and that complex ecological issues involve people in many specialties.
Even more significantly, it shows that science involves speculation that
sometimes turns out to be right and sometimes wrong, and that the same
logic and rational thought processes come up with the wrong, as well as
the right, answers. Through these strategies, ‘The Sixth Extinction?’ mod-
els how popular science texts can present a Realist–Skeptical construct
of science—one that acknowledges its material and epistemic connec-
tions to its context, and the contingent nature of its claims—while still
recognizing the power and importance of ecological research.


Conclusion: Boundary Work and a New Social Contract

This chapter has provided a vocabulary and an analytical/heuristic
framework for talking about how science relates to its context. Such
                              Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context   81


a framework is important because, despite decades of social science
research demonstrating that science is materially and epistemically
continuous with its cultural context, rhetorical demarcation of science
still shows up in many places, from the National Science Education
Standards to popular accounts of what science is and how it works.7 In
science popularization, texts in the PAST tradition demarcate science
from other practices, clinging to a vision of ‘the purity of scientific
results’ (Greene, p. xxii), while others show science’s effects on society,
but not how science is affected by society, a limited perspective that
reinforces the specialness of science.
   While analysis shows that many popular science texts focus on rein-
forcing the perceived boundaries between science and other arenas, it
also shows ways that popular science texts do connect science to its
material, social, and epistemological context, and thereby depict the
complicated ways that science is a part of society. These CUSP texts
present frontier science as a process of knowledge-making, rather than
knowledge-finding, and avoid a problematic ‘representation of science
as quest for a general truth, independent of contexts, of history and
of projects which made these particular models interesting’ (Fourez,
p. 925), thereby helping move us toward a context in which a new
social contract is possible.
   As Gibbons notes, this new social contract must respond to the nature
of science today, specifically to the fact that ‘there are no longer clear
demarcation lines between university science and industrial science,
between basic research, applied research and product development, or
even between careers in the academic world and in industry’ (Gibbons,
p. C81). A CUSP approach to demarcation work does this by showing
the ways that science constructs knowledge, and by showing how con-
textual factors affect that process.
   In addition, a CUSP approach to boundary work encourages reflective
practice within the sciences—something that can help them adapt to
their changing environment. Byerly and Pielke (a science policy adviser
and an environmental scientist respectively) argue that the social contract
as understood is preventing changes that are necessary for a sustainable
future of science, and that the ‘situation demands more than defense of
the status quo—if faced constructively, it is an opportunity to develop
a sounder social contract, to develop an ecology in which science can
thrive’ (p. 1531). One step toward developing that ecology is moving
past the myth that science is a realm apart and understanding how it
is embedded—drawing from, influenced by, and accountable to—civil
society.
6
Expertise: Broadening the Scope
of Participation




The science–society relationships described in Chapter 5 are echoed
in miniature in how texts treat expertise. Just as demarcation work at
the societal level depicts science as separate from (and nearly always
superior to) other forms of knowledge-making, demarcation work at
the individual level determines who does and doesn’t have authority
to talk about a particular issue. Often this determination is based on
claims of expertise, with categorization of knowledge as ‘expert’ set-
ting the ‘scope of participation’ (Nisbet, p. 44). Thus, representations
of expertise in popular science texts signal readers about what ‘systems
of knowledge and ways of knowing are relevant (or irrelevant)’ (Gee,
p. 112) in science-related conversations.
   To examine ways that texts can foster CUSP (Critical Understanding of
Science in Public) by increasing the scope of participation, this chapter
does five things. First, it explains where different concepts of expertise fall
on the PAST (Public Appreciation of Science and Technology)–CUSP con-
tinuum. Second, it revisits the practitioner texts analyzed in Chapter 4,
focusing on whether they offer a narrow or an inclusive understanding of
expertise. Third, it describes a variety of CUSP model rhetorical strategies,
illustrating each one with a brief analysis of an exemplary popular science
text. Fourth, it offers an integrated analysis of how several strategies work
together to create a broad scope of participation within one popular sci-
ence text. Fifth, it discusses how the strategies discussed in the chapter
can help us move toward the new social contract.


Expertise and the PAST–CUSP Continuum

A binary of expert/nonexpert is often used in technocratic arguments,
those based in assumptions that ordinary people and politicians do

                                      82
                              Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 83


not understand science and that ‘complex issues should be decided by
those who have the capabilities to do so: in short, the experts’ (Bucchi,
Beyond, p. 2). Furthermore, ‘experts’ tends to be defined, in such situa-
tions, as people with specific institutional credentials and affiliations.
Such an expert, ‘recognized by professional membership, is assumed to
have privileged access to knowledge and is deferred to in its interpreta-
tion’ (Burgman et al., p. 82). This is certainly appropriate for debates
within scientific fields, but when science-related discussions leave the
realm of specialized journals, other kinds of expertise become relevant.
   However, in PAST-oriented texts, only scientists are shown as quali-
fied to judge science, suggesting that any kind of social or political
oversight is unnecessary and harmful. In addition, the myth of science
as a source of rational, unbiased perspectives on reality often leads to its
being applied outside of the specialist realm, as when scientists’ points
of view are given special consideration in the public realm more gener-
ally. Weigold describes how science writers ‘tend to trust scientists even
when scientists speak on topics on which they are not experts’ and
cites research showing that ‘about one-third of science-reporter interac-
tions deal with issues having little or no relationship to the scientists’
research’ (p. 182).
   In the CUSP model, the limits of specialist knowledge are recognized,
and the scope of participation is broader; in Gibbons’ words, the valid-
ity of scientific knowledge in a new social contract is ‘achieved through
involving an extended group of experts, including lay “experts”’ (p. C82).
CUSP texts create an appropriate scope of participation and recognize
the diverse perspectives and interests that make up civil society. That is,
they promote CUSP when they broaden the scope of participation by
including a greater variety of perspectives.
   In explaining expertise as a broad category, rhetorician Beverly J.
Sauer defines experts as people who ‘have more knowledge, experience,
and education than novices’, a definition that ‘does not presume that
scientific expertise constitutes the sole criterion for expertise’ (p. 79), but
that also avoids the free-for-all claim that everyone is an expert on every-
thing. Instead of focusing on scientific expertise, an area that already has
plenty of authority, she advocates for ‘communication which reflects
the viewpoints of diverse stakeholders, both lay and scientific’ (p. 175).1
Following her lead, I discuss four general kinds of expertise.

• Contributory expertise is the ability to ‘contribute to the knowledge base
  of the topic’, whether the contribution is in ‘abstract/generalizable
  or local/practical knowledge’ (Carolan, p. 663). Scientific expertise
84 Communicating Popular Science


  in a given field is, by definition, contributory, but others kinds of
  expertise can be as well. A gardener may know things about certain
  plants that a plant biologist does not, for example.
• Meta-expertise is the ability to critically evaluate work in an area
  in which one is not a specialist (Collins and Evans, p. 7). Meta-
  expertise is always applied in a given situation, with a particular
  goal. Within science, National Science Foundation grant commit-
  tees use meta-expertise when reviewing proposals from outside their
  own immediate areas; even though they are not expert in the topic
  being proposed, they are able to meet the goal of assessing the qual-
  ity of the proposal. We also exercise meta-expertise in our everyday
  lives; the gardener, reading university extension bulletins on a pest,
  decides what information is relevant and trustworthy, whether or
  not to consult a specialist and if so which one, weighs what she or
  he learns against her own experience, and so on.
• Experiential expertise is ‘tacit or craft knowledge’ (Sauer, p. 79) that
  can only be learned through direct, personal experience. Athletic
  skill is a form of experiential expertise, as are handicrafts. Intimate
  familiarity with a place is another kind of experiential expertise, as
  is the above-mentioned gardener’s experience with growing roses in
  a rainy climate.
• Interactional expertise is the ability to communicate one’s expertise
  to people with a different kind of expertise, for example the ability
  of someone with contributory expertise to ‘interact interestingly’
  (Carolan, p. 663) with those who have local/practical knowledge, and
  vice-versa.2 When a gardener sits down to talk with a plant biologist
  who works for the university extension, the more interactional exper-
  tise each has, the more productive the conversation is likely to be.

As the example of the gardener shows, these kinds of expertise are not
exclusive, and a person can have one or several at the same time. CUSP
texts do not necessarily include all of these kinds of expertise—among
other things, every kind might not be relevant in a given situation—but
they do expand the scope of participation beyond specialist experts to
include an array of other perspectives.


Expertise Described in Practitioner Texts

Understanding expertise as a complex construction raises the question
of how popular science writers view expertise. To see whether they
think of expertise in narrow or broad terms, I considered two questions.
                             Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 85


1. Do popular science writers understand the limits to scientific expertise?
   That is, do they depict expert scientific knowledge in Traditional–
   Idealist or Realist–Skeptical terms?
2. Do popular science writers think the views of nonscientist experts,
   including lay experts, are relevant to science-related issues?

At the PAST end of the spectrum are texts that answer ‘no’ to both
of those questions, while the CUSP end is characterized by an under-
standing of limits and a discussion of why different points of view are
important.
   The first question—whether popular science writers recognize
limits—was answered in Chapter 4’s finding that many popular science
writers say scientific expertise extends well beyond specialist domains.
These writers who see science as a ‘cure’ for all kinds of deficits outside
the realm of scientific research per se—who claim, for example, that the
world needs ‘an extension to human affairs of the scientific mindset
itself’ (Pinker, p. xvii)—clearly assume that the authority and expertise
developed in an area of scientific research generalizes beyond that area,
and beyond science more generally.
   In keeping with this idea that scientists are the best experts on every-
thing, some writers in the Field Guide only recommend consulting
with scientists. Even when they seem to suggest talking to a range of
people, those people are nearly always specialists. For example, Revkin
suggests that when a writer runs into trouble with ‘barriers to effective
communication with the public’, the solution is ‘to communicate more
with scientists’ (p. 227). Indeed, all the writers need in order to satisfy
readers is to spend more time with scientists: ‘The more journalists and
scientists talk outside the pressures of a daily news deadline, the more
likely it is that the public—through the media—will appreciate what sci-
ence can and cannot offer to the debate over difficult questions about
how to invest scarce resources or change personal behaviors’ (Revkin,
p. 228). Revkin does acknowledge that science writers need to commu-
nicate not just what science can offer but what it can’t, but the overall
message is that writers should get this sense from talking to scientists;
there is no suggestion that writers might also find this out from talking
with nonspecialists.
   In an even more extreme statement, Gleick writes in the Field Guide’s
‘Epilogue’ that science has ‘a unique place in our culture’ and ‘a special
claim on the truth’ (p. 305), for example ‘What a nuclear physicist dis-
covers may be wrong. It will be questioned; it must be provisional. And
yet it deserves a kind of authority that should be denied’ (pp. 305–6) to
86 Communicating Popular Science


anyone who isn’t a nuclear physicist. ‘Not fully understanding nuclear
physics’, Gleick asks, ‘how can they really judge its veracity?’ (p. 307).
While most nonphysicists probably don’t understand the specifics of
cutting-edge physics research, Gleick’s suggestion that they therefore
have no authority to speak about nuclear physics at all is rooted in an
all-or-nothing view of scientific knowledge that ignores the many other
kinds of knowledge that may be useful in discussions of the topic.
   Folger says that common sense is useful if we ‘want to fool ourselves
or be lulled into complacency by myths’, but that if we want ‘to under-
stand the universe—and our place in it—we need a healthy exposure
to the uncommon sense of science’ (‘Foreword’ 2004, p. x, emphasis
original). This denies the validity of any knowledge system other than
institutional science and illustrates ‘a hierarchical concept of forms
of knowledge’ in which ‘scientific knowledge is superior to popular
everyday-knowledge, so-called common sense. Science has a monopoly
on truth in society’ (Weingart, p. 869).3
   The CUSP end of the spectrum is also represented in the Field Guide.
In terms of science’s limitations, Begos warns against assuming a scien-
tist in one area has special insights in other areas. Begos cites the case of
physicist William Shockley who ‘was not an expert in genetics, biology,
sociology, or anything to do with the human mind, behavior, or repro-
duction. Yet he was able to use his status as a “Nobel laureate” to get
vast amounts of media coverage’ (p. 183) for his racist statements about
intelligence and his eugenic agenda. Begos cautions writers that scien-
tists ‘will have specific areas of expertise, but some may (consciously
or not) attempt to make grand statements about how a particular idea
or discovery may affect humanity’ (p. 184). Begos’ answer to question
one—does scientific expertise have limits—is an emphatic ‘yes’.
   Begos also says ‘yes’ to question two in suggesting writers should
think about including in their texts people who ‘will look at the science
from the perspective of real-world society. That doesn’t mean you get
a social worker to comment on the science in the study, but rather on
how the science might be used—or misused’ (p. 186). Getting another
angle helps writers provide ‘a view that goes beyond simply stringing
together different scientists in a “he said, she said” battle over whether
the research is correct’ (p. 186). Raeburn likewise suggests going beyond
the usual sources, as reporting only what scientists say would convey
‘only a small piece of the story’ (p. 177). Raeburn recommends that
those writing about ‘psychiatry, psychology, and mental illness’ must
‘do more than peruse the scientific journals and attend neuroscience
meetings. We need to get out there in the trenches, by which I mean
                             Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 87


the homes and the minds and hearts of the families who are suffering
from mental illness’ (p. 177). He adds that ‘it’s critically important to
talk to patients, and their families, to get the complete story’ (p. 180)
and mentions that the American Psychological Association provides
references ‘to experts both on research and on the problems with the
health care system’ (p. 181). Similarly, Brownlee suggests drawing on a
variety of resources, including ‘patient advocacy groups’ and the Center
for Medical Consumers (p. 159).
  Here we see the broad scope of participation to which Nisbet refers, and
a heartening counter narrative to the emphasis on scientific expertise.
In rejecting a narrow definition of relevant expertise, these authors
open the door to CUSP, or at least to public discussions about the impli-
cations of science-in-society.


Expertise in Popular Science Texts

Like practitioners’ texts, popular science texts vary in how they repre-
sent expertise, a variation that reflects the range of purposes from PAST
to CUSP. Some writers assume that only credentialed scientists have
valuable knowledge to offer. Others show the limitations on specialist
knowledge, show how to weigh their input critically, and include differ-
ent kinds of authority in a text—rhetorical strategies I address in turn.

CUSP 1: Homans Talks About Expertise as Field-Specific
Charles Homans’ ‘Hot Air’ shows how a blanket valuing of ‘science’
gives meteorologists too much play in the climate change conversa-
tion. In doing so, it illustrates why popular science texts should dis-
tinguish between different kinds of scientific expertise and be clear
about what each can and cannot offer. Homans traces the histories of
weather-casting and meteorology. In postwar America ‘the first genera-
tion of weathercasters…was mostly military men, recently discharged
World War II veterans who had trained in meteorology in the Navy and
the Army Air Corps’, but, in the 1950s, ‘the Army men gave way to enter-
tainers: scantily clad “weather girls” abounded, as did puppets’ (p. 56). In
response, the American Meteorological Society developed the ‘meteoro-
logical certification system’ (p. 57), which distinguishes meteorologists
from weathercasters, ensuring a level of quality in weather forecasting
and reporting, which has garnered credibility for the profession.
   The problem, Homan explains, is that many meteorologists are now
using that credibility to speak out against the fact of anthropogenic cli-
mate change. Homans explains that for people ‘who don’t understand
88 Communicating Popular Science


the difference between weather—the short-term behavior of the
atmosphere—and climate—the broader system in which weather hap-
pens’ a meteorologist’s pronouncements are taken as authoritative
(p. 53). Homans is not saying meteorologists are bad scientists or bad
at communicating about science. On the contrary, he says that ‘the
achievement weathercasters have pulled off as science educators is
remarkable—ask anyone with a television to name some meteorological
terms, and odds are they will be able to rattle off half a dozen: low pres-
sure systems, wind shear, cumulonimbus clouds’ (p. 57). Rather, he is
simply warning against the kinds of problems that can happen when, as
it has with weathercasters, a ‘narrow professional authority [is] miscon-
strued as a sort of all-purpose scientific legitimacy’ (pp. 60–1).
   Although ‘experts stray easily outside the narrow limits of their core
knowledge…once outside an expert is no more effective than a layper-
son’ (Burgman et al., p. 82), and Homans’ article demonstrates the value
in recognizing that expertise is always limited in some way, including
the scope of its applicability.

CUSP 2: Folger Includes Various Kinds of Expertise
A second rhetorical strategy for expanding the scope of participation
is to include various kinds of expertise. Showing different kinds of
expertise works against the ‘instrumental view of public discourse’
(Brown, R.H., p. 4), which assumes that nonspecialists cannot engage
intelligently with technical issues, and which ‘reverses the democratic
impulse by encouraging citizens to occupy themselves with private
matters while remaining beholden to their expert representatives in
public affairs’ (Brown, R.H., p. 5). In place of this Traditional–Idealist
approach, popular science texts that include a range of experts illustrate
the democratic ideal of informed citizens coming together from various
backgrounds to collectively decide matters relevant to civil society.
   Folger’s article ‘The Data Trail’ focuses on Dave Bertelsen, a retired
probation officer who has spent almost three decades hiking in the
Sonoran Desert and gathering information about what he sees on his
regular hikes. In those years, through thousands of miles of hiking,
Bertelsen has ‘amassed an enormous amount of information on the
elevation, distribution, and bloom dates of some 600 plant species and
subspecies; in 1997 he began keeping equally detailed records of the
reptiles and mammals he has encountered during his weekly 10-mile
hikes. Last year he added birds, and he has, Folger reports, 195,000
observations (‘Data’, p. 194). Bertelsen’s ‘careful observations have been
honed over the years as what started as casual hikes became something
                             Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 89


more’ (‘Data’, p. 198) and have made him ‘a leading expert on the
Sonoran Desert’s unique flora and fauna’ (‘Data’, p. 194). Through these
descriptions, Folger shows that experiential experts can develop a deep
familiarity with local environments.
  ‘The Data Trail’ also illustrates how community members—people
with local, practical knowledge of an environment—can contribute data
that institutional scientists don’t have the time or resources to gather.
In Bertelsen’s case, his notes ‘are so precise and voluminous that a team
of scientists at the University of Arizona in Tucson is using them to
study the effects of global warming here’ (‘Data’, p. 194), and scientists
working with Bertelsen say they could never have collected so much
information. One of them, ecologist Theresa Crimmins, says she ‘just
about fainted when Dave explained what he had’, adding that ‘A paid
scientist could never collect something of this magnitude’ (‘Data’,
p. 199). Her colleague and spouse, Michael Crimmins, agrees that
‘A data set of, first, that quality and, second, that breadth, just doesn’t
exist. You would never see this in a funded project’ (‘Data’, p. 199). By
showing Bertelsen’s contribution to understanding the Sonoran Desert
ecosystem, Folger suggests that the social category of ‘expert’ does,
and should, include people whose knowledge comes via avenues other
than formal study, avenues that include their experience of living in a
particular place.

CUSP 3: Canby Includes Local, Nonscientific Expertise
Moving even further, some texts expand the scope of participation to peo-
ple whose knowledge does not fit into a scientific scheme as Bertelsen’s
does. Although it is good that Folger shows how expertise gained through
years of on-the-ground experience can fit into a scientific paradigm,
socially robust knowledge also calls for inclusion of experts whose knowl-
edge does not fit so neatly with standard research methods and models.
  Two articles, one by Peter Canby and another by David Quammen,
provide contrasting examples of how popular science texts represent
local expertise, with Canby including it and Quammen dismissing it.4
Canby, exemplifying the CUSP treatment of local expertise, writes
about the Bayaka who live in the forest, sometimes for years at a time,
and have developed a vast knowledge of the forest ecology in order
to survive. Canby shows that the American scientist doing research in
the park, Blake, learns from the Bayaka, valuing their knowledge and
acknowledging it as more comprehensive than his own. Canby tells
us that ‘Blake hires them [the Bayaka] because they know the forest
intimately. “I often think every Bayaka should be awarded a doctorate
90 Communicating Popular Science


in forest ecology,” Blake says. “They know what’s going on”’ (pp. 3–4).
Haraway reminds us ‘that the power of naming a thing is the power of
objectifying’ (p. 79), and Blake compares his own knowledge to that
of two Bayaka: ‘There are 4,000 to 5,000 plants in this forest’, Blake
says one day. ‘I know the botanical names of perhaps 400. Mossimbo
knows the Bayaka names for probably twice that. Zonmiputu knows
even more’ (p. 11). As Canby shows, the Bayaka are co-participants in
Blake’s research project—experts who know more about the forest than
the American scientist who leads the expedition. The implication is not
that Blake’s knowledge isn’t valuable, but simply that other forms of
knowledge exist and should be valued as well.
   Canby’s depiction of local, nonscience-based expertise contrasts
sharply with Quammen’s treatment of the same knowledge and the
people who have it. According to Quammen’s article, nobody lives in
the forest at all; the ‘Pygmies’—he does not use the name Bayaka—are
important to scientific expeditions mainly for their labor. All expertise
rests with the British researcher, Michael Fay, who is held in awe by all
who know him, including the Pygmy porters whose jobs are presented
as breaking trail, carrying supplies, and setting up camp. (In addition to
providing manual labor, the locals serve as props in Fay’s video record
as he works on getting ‘footage of major trees, posing a Pygmy among
the buttresses for scale’ [p. 245].) As far as understanding the environs,
Fay’s knowledge is superior, as we see in the fact that he teaches every-
one, ‘instructing me or quizzing Madzou and Morgan on the identity
of this tree or that. … Morgan and Madzou are earnest students. Fay is a
stern but effective teacher’ (pp. 250–51). Even the local, Madzou, learns
from Fay. As for Fay, he apparently learns from nobody; his only ‘Pygmy
mentor’, from an earlier expedition, is described as having provided
‘fastidious tracking’ which ‘allowed’ Fay to follow a group of gorillas
and gather data (p. 246). On the current trip, the Pygmies don’t provide
even physical guidance, as ‘Fay’s GPS tells us where we are, while his
map and our compasses tell us which way to go’ (p. 243).5
   Quammen and Canby’s articles together illustrate how different the
PAST and CUSP views of expertise are. As Haraway explains, demarca-
tion has to do with ‘who has authority to exercise effective power over
nature through knowledge of the word, control of the coin, cracking
the code of nature’s secret voice’ (Haraway, p. 74), and Quammen’s
‘Megatransect’ sends a clear signal that scientists alone have that power.
On the CUSP side, in contrast, Canby values not only a scientific view of
the forest ecosystem, but also the ‘other epistemic possibilities’ (Tietge,
p. 13), other ways of ‘cracking the code’ and understanding nature.
                             Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 91


Expertise in Corson’s ‘Stalking the American Lobster’

Corson’s ‘Stalking the American Lobster’, discussed in Chapter 5 for its
depiction of knowledge construction, also shows the benefits of valu-
ing different kinds of expertise. In the dispute between lobstermen and
fisheries scientists, Corson says the lobstermen’s knowledge is dismissed
because they tend not to have ‘advanced degrees and scientific data’ to
back up their claims (p. 140), a clear case of how the ‘empowerment of
experts yields a disempowerment of citizens’ (Brown, R. H., p. 2). His
description of the fishermen’s cooperative relationship with ecologists
shows an alternative way for different kinds of experts to interact and
also exemplifies how CUSP texts can promote these alternative relation-
ships by using the CUSP strategies described above.
   First, Corson explains the limitations of the fisheries laboratory’s
approach. Chapter 5 touched on this, talking about the fact that mod-
els are only as good as their assumptions, but Corson also brings out
another weakness in the fisheries model: it leaves out how the lobster-
men’s practices affect the lobster population. The Maine lobstermen,
Corson says, explain that the models ‘fail to factor in the margin of
safety that lobstermen have built into their fishery for decades: a pool
of large reproductive lobsters, protected not only by Maine’s maximum-
size restriction but also by a curious practice known as V-notching’
(p. 149). This practice involves cutting a notch in the tail of any female
lobster who has eggs; once a lobster’s tail has been notched, all lobster-
men know to release her back into the water. Fisheries scientists worry
about lobstermen harvesting too many smaller lobsters, but the lobster-
men ‘are convinced that brood-stock lobsters more than compensate for
any deficiencies in egg production by smaller lobsters’ (p. 151). Corson
presents some of the data that could support the lobstermen’s position
and concludes that while they may not be right, ‘They’re not necessar-
ily wrong’ (p. 151). Whether or not the lobstermen are correct—and
Corson is not taking sides—the point is that the fisheries scientists, by
ignoring their actions, may be missing an important variable affecting
lobster populations. In contrast with this laboratory view of scientific
knowledge as separable from the environment, Corson shows ecologists
who realize that natural phenomena happen in contexts, often contexts
that are affected by human actions.
   The relationship between the ecologists and lobstermen also illus-
trates that contributory expertise can stem both from specialist train-
ing and from experience. Corson’s description of the lobsterman
Fernald illustrates the importance of experiential expertise, as Fernald’s
92 Communicating Popular Science


understanding of lobsters has been ‘developed during the course of a
lifetime on the water’ (p. 140) and is specific to a particular place. This
knowledge includes an intimate understanding of the ecosystem in
which the lobsters live and of the lobsters themselves. Fernald works in
an area that is ‘150 square miles of underwater boulder fields, gravel,
and mud’ and even though he has never seen it from a submarine like
Steneck has, ‘like a blind man who can read a face, he knows what it
looks like—each gully, hillock, canyon, and plateau’ (p. 140). Fernald
‘maintains 800 traps across a twenty-mile-long swath of ocean…[and]
knows exactly where to place each one from one week to the next,
March through December’, for example keeping lobster traps ‘stra-
tegically placed in certain coves and kelp beds, and near underwater
boulders where he knows lobsters like to hide and hunt’ (p. 141) in the
summer, then moving the traps into deeper water when the lobsters
start to move outward in September. Corson’s detailed descriptions of
what Fernald does and knows bring home the fact that some knowledge
can be learned only through years of practice. This is especially true of
knowledge about a particular ecosystem, the complexity of which takes
years of in-person experience to understand.
   Likewise, another lobsterman combines experiential expertise with
meta-expertise. The lobsterman, Merrill, ‘studied both literature
and science in college’ (p. 148) and in the 1980s he ‘began collect-
ing scientific papers on the lobster fishery’ (p. 149), reading them
and comparing them to what he knew from his years of lobstering.
When the two didn’t match up, Merrill (in his role as president of the
Maine Lobstermen’s Association), questioned the government scien-
tists who were producing the papers. In describing Merrill’s critique
of the scientific research, Corson shows that people may offer valid
critiques of science even when they are not themselves practising
scientists.
   Key to the integration of different kinds of contributory expertise,
and the acceptance of meta-expertise, is interactional expertise. Steneck
and the other ecologist demonstrate this form of expertise as they com-
municate with the lobstermen, but, the relationship Corson describes is
not a simple reversal of the binary, with nonscientist expertise valued
over that of the scientists. Although the lobstermen are ‘thoughtful
and broad-minded stewards of a communal resource’ (p. 158), they do
not think they alone have useful knowledge about lobsters. Instead,
they cooperate with the ecologists in developing even better models
for managing the lobster population, mirroring the ecologists open-
ness to learning from them. The goal is shared knowledge-making, not
                            Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 93


replacing of scientific experts with local experts; thus, this interaction
epitomizes the goal of CUSP: not a different kind of exclusion, but more
inclusion. As Sally Eden, whose research focuses on environmentalism
and public policy, explains, ‘environmentalist critics of science are cri-
tiquing not science or technology per se but their domination through
scientism and technocracy’ (Eden, p. 194). While the conflicts between
the laboratory scientists and the lobstermen show the kinds of prob-
lems that can arise when people fail to ‘interact interestingly’ (Carolan,
p. 663) with those whose knowledge they discount, Steneck and
the lobstermen have both contributory and interactional expertise.
Corson’s article chronicles the benefits of those with different kinds of
contributory expertise being able to overcome differences to communi-
cate and work with each other.
   In sum, Corson’s article shows how interactional expertise, and the
inclusion of experiential and meta-expertise that it allows, produces
socially robust knowledge in several senses. First, it considers factors
in the local environment (in this case, the lobstermen’s practice of
V-notching), and thus is, in Gibbons’ words, ‘valid not only inside
but also outside the laboratory’ (p. C82). It is robust, too, because it
comes from a cooperative sharing of information among a variety of
experts, including both scientists and nonscientists. And in a third
sense, the cooperative genesis of the information means it is more
likely to be accepted by the local people whose lives are affected by
the decisions made based on this information. This third sense of
robustness is especially important with research that feeds into envi-
ronmental policy decisions. As Gibbons suggests, and as research (e.g.,
by environmental geographer Sally Eden) shows, taking into account
the traditions and actions of those who will be asked to change their
behavior, should that be necessary, increases the chances of the
changes actually taking place.


Conclusion: Expertise and a New Social Contract

Too often discussions of expertise fall into a binary of expertise as
something that some people (generally scientists) have and others
don’t. Corson’s representations of various kinds of expertise, and of
scientists and lobstermen developing interactional expertise together,
opens up possibilities for new kinds of knowledge-making. This chap-
ter has offered a way to talk about different kinds of expertise and
to consider the scope of participation in popular science texts as a
key factor in whether and to what extent the texts encourage critical
94 Communicating Popular Science


engagement with science by nonspecialists. That engagement is, in
turn, essential to a new social contract and the production of socially
robust scientific knowledge. In science policy, for example, the tra-
ditional social contract promotes a technocratic expectation that
scientific experts will guide policy decisions. The new contract is based
on the idea that scientific expertise should inform policy decisions,
but that decisions should also take into account other stakeholders’
perspectives.
   Drawing on multiple kinds of knowledge also supports the
knowledge-making mission of science. The new social contract does
not replace the old contract’s emphasis on producing useful and accu-
rate knowledge about the natural world. Rather, it includes and builds
on that aim and, in many cases, has the potential to work even better at
meeting it. As Sauer explains, gaps in scientific ‘knowledge frameworks’
may have answers, or possible answers, in other knowledge frameworks
(p. 102). Indeed, people who study the outcomes of community science
have found that such experience offers a productive complement to
standard scientific approaches to research, not only in data-gathering,
but also in designing studies and interpreting evidence. Thus, the popu-
lar science texts examined in this chapter promote a new social contract
by showing that when domain-specific expertise (e.g., in ecology) inter-
acts with other kinds of expertise (such as lobstering know-how) in a
rich, polyvocal conversation, benefits accrue to science, as well as to the
involved community.
   Finally, popular science texts that show a broad scope of participation
also encourages reflexivity about and in science. Studies show, for exam-
ple, that community involvement in science ‘enables a more reflective
discussion of the complexity and uncertainty of science (means-based)
as well as the wider public discussion of where science is headed (ends-
based)’ (Carr, pp. 841–2). Thus, including nonscientist authorities in
popular science texts can help specialists understand their own point
of view as one among many important perspectives on ecological issues
and other questions about the natural world.
   None of this is to say that such a process is always smooth. In fact,
it is almost certain to be less smooth than the technocratic approach
in which expert specialists speak, and a respectful media and public
listen. However, this bumpy process is what democratic decision-
making requires. A new social contract must recognize, in discussions
of science by interested citizens, ‘that public concerns and meanings
legitimately differ from expert ones (and amongst themselves very
often, too), and that every such citizen is in principle a legitimate
                           Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation 95


participant in what should be the deliberative negotiation of such
public meanings’ (Wynne, p. 27, emphasis original). To be clear, I am
not saying one form of knowledge is better than another, but that
when scientific issues impinge on the civil arena, then the many
people whose voices make up civil society must be heard in a delib-
erative democracy.
7
Ethos: Establishing Relationships
with Readers




So far the chapters in Part II have addressed relatively impersonal
aspects of popular science writing; Chapter 5 dealt with the relationship
between science and others arenas in civil society, and Chapter 6 looked
at how individual expertise is constructed as a social category. While
both have important implications for how texts invite readers to engage
with science neither explicitly addressed the roles of the writer and
the reader. This chapter and the next do so. Chapter 8 will examine the
kinds of relationships popular science texts invite readers to take toward
scientific issues, but first this chapter looks at how writers create ethos
and construct personas in popular science texts.
  The chapter begins with an explanation of the different components
of ethos, says how ethos is enacted in texts via personas, and explains
how ethos and persona contribute to texts’ position on the PAST (Public
Appreciation of Science and Technology)–CUSP (Critical Understanding
of Science in Public) continuum. The next section, on practitioner texts,
returns to Chapter 5’s description of the roles that popular science writ-
ers identify for themselves, which I gloss as ‘boosters’ and ‘critics’, and
shows their different stances toward readers. Next, it analyzes several
CUSP model popular science texts that have particularly good examples
of different strategies for creating ethos and persona. This is followed
by an integrated analysis of several strategies in one text. Finally, the
conclusion reviews the analytical framework developed in the chapter
and explains how it can help foster a new social contract.


Ethos and the PAST–CUSP Continuum

Ethos is about how readers perceive writers, and texts are most effective
when the reader is confident that the writer is credible (knows what he

                                    96
                               Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 97


or she is talking about) and trustworthy (has the reader’s best interests
in mind, rather than someone else’s, as is the case in advertising, for
example). Traditionally, ‘ethos’ has been described in terms of three
components: arete, phronesis, and eunoia. Although this tripartite divi-
sion remains useful, the terms themselves have taken on various mean-
ings. Because of this range of definitions, I briefly explain what each has
traditionally meant and how I use it.
   The first component of ethos is arete. This is generally translated
as ‘good character’ or ‘civil virtue’, but the more general meaning has
to do with excellence in fulfilling one’s role—what Hauser refers to as
‘virtuosity’ (Vernacular, p. 71). Because expertise and knowledge are the
standards of excellence in technical matters,1 I use ‘arete’ to refer to the
writer’s knowledge of the subject matter.
   The second component of ethos is phronesis, usually translated as
‘practical wisdom’. Unlike sophia (wisdom in general), phronesis is
practical because it requires the ability to apply one’s knowledge appro-
priately in a given situation. Phronesis can be understood as judgment,
as the ‘knowing how’ complement to sophia’s ‘knowing that’: wisdom
means knowing not to drive too fast on a snowy road, but practical
wisdom means being able to judge how fast is ‘too fast’ on a given road
at a given time. Because standards of judgment differ from one arena to
another—what a Monsanto-employed geneticist considers a good stand-
ard of acceptability for genetically-modified organisms is not the same
as what the European community considers acceptable—which means
that phronesis requires a commitment to a particular set of values.
   The final component of ethos is eunoia. This is often talked about as
‘good will’ toward one’s audience, but it has a more reciprocal compo-
nent as well, that is, it’s about how a writer with good will toward an audi-
ence creates good will with the audience by referring to shared values or
a common world view, and by showing respect for readers. I refer to this
combination of good will, identification, and respect as ‘friendliness’.
   Although each of these components is important, ethos is inevitably an
incomplete, hence selective, representation of the writer. All of who writers
are—what they know, think, value, believe, feel—can never fit into a text,
so they have to choose what to include and what to leave out. The set of
choices results in a particular textual presence, a persona. Just as people
enact different roles in different circumstances—colleague, friend, parent,
constituent, boss, employee, and so on—writers adopt different personas
in different kinds of texts and for different audiences.
   Keith Grant-Davie (using terms coined by Scott Consigny) explains
that ethos is a combination of ‘integrity—a measure of consistency
98 Communicating Popular Science


they take from situation to situation instead of putting on a completely
new mask to suit the needs of every new audience and situation’ and
‘receptivity—the ability to adapt to new situations and not rigidly play
the same role in every one’ (p. 270). Ethos therefore builds on a writer’s
enduring characteristics, but is expressed through the particular persona
the writer adopts to better reach a given audience.2 That is, the elements
of ethos make sense only in the context of the community that deter-
mines what information is relevant, what the standards of judgment
should be, and what the hallmarks of friendliness are, and so adapting
one’s persona to a given audience is a necessary part of creating a strong
ethos.
