   However, the fact that ethos is rooted in a community raises a com-
plication. For Aristotle, a rhetor belonged to a reasonably stable com-
munity in which citizens shared a worldview and an understanding
of what it meant to have judgment, practical wisdom, and good will.
Today, people belong to a variety of communities. Thus, although talk-
ing to an insider audience is still common—we see it all the time in
academic journals—it happens only in the context of conversations
internal to a given discourse community. In contrast, public discourses
engage a multitude of audiences. Even if we assume that all nonscientist
readers are one community, popular science writers still engage with
two communities at once—the specialists about whose work they write
and the (by definition) nonspecialist audience.
   This brings us to the PAST–CUSP continuum. PAST-model texts take a
Traditional–Idealist view in which serving science is seen as serving soci-
ety, and the only legitimate critiques of science are those that use the
standards of criticism developed within and by the scientific commu-
nity. The result for ethos is that popular science writers speak on behalf
of science and it is assumed that this will benefit readers. For the arete
component of ethos, it means that decisions about what knowledge to
include are based on the desire to show science as useful, interesting, or
valuable, and not necessarily on what readers need to know in order to
make informed decisions. For phronesis, judgment is absent (as when
writers simply report information without any evaluation3), based
on technoscientific criteria, or reduced to the ability to choose good
experts. As for eunoia, the deficit-based assumption that readers should
learn to think like scientists means that writers may have good will
toward their audience, but often lack the other aspects of eunoia (such
as respect for readers’ intelligence and knowledge)—a point I return to
later. The writers’ overall stance in these texts tends to be that of the
preacher trying to ‘save’ readers or a pedant trying to ease the burden
of their ignorance.
                               Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 99


   In contrast, CUSP-model texts exemplify the democratic ethos of pub-
lic interest writing. Information is included on the basis of what readers
want or need to know. Phronesis—practical wisdom—means writers
look at the scientific information presented and also at the situation
as a whole, that is at the question of science-in-society, using standards
generally accepted in civil society. Finally, writers in CUSP-model texts
use various strategies to signal their respect for readers and identifica-
tion with readers’ interests. The writers’ stance in CUSP model texts is
that of a knowledgeable peer or even friend.

Ethos as Described in Practitioner Texts
To get a sense of how practitioners think of ethos, I asked two questions:

1. What stance toward science do writers take?
2. What stance toward readers do writers take?

The first question has already been addressed in Chapters 4 and 5,
which found a range. At the PAST end were uncritical acceptance of
scientific information and calls for judging science only on science’s
terms. At the CUSP end were writers who said to judge science-related
issues in their broader context.
   From the point of view of ethos, the different stances described in
Chapters 4 and 5 suggest differences in allegiance, with PAST-type views
reflecting identification with the norms and goals of the scientific com-
munity. Along these lines, the scholarly literature suggests that popular
science writers often over-identify with their sources to the detriment
of their writing; Schäfer’s meta-analysis of studies indicates that popular
science writers ‘generally exhibit a rather strong source dependence’
(p. 404), while Gregory and Miller find that ‘journalists rely on the
scientists for accurate facts’, and that this ‘has resulted in science cover-
age that is more deferential to its subject matter and constituency than
would be acceptable in other fields of journalism’ (p. 107).4
   The problem of such deference shows how writers in the Field Guide
express concern about what scientists think of them. For example,
Lemonick says he always makes sure scientists like what he writes
because then they ‘will know they can trust me, and they’ll begin call-
ing me’ (p. 221). Holland agrees that the ‘key to success…is in the rela-
tionships we establish with individual researchers’ because ‘If we get it
right in their eyes, they trust us and are more likely to work with us for
years to come’ (p. 269). In keeping the view of popular science writing
as something done on behalf of science, Gleick writes that science writ-
ers must communicate clearly to nonscientists in order ‘to be advocates,
100   Communicating Popular Science


not for scientists, but for science, as a set of principles’ (‘Epilogue’, p. 308,
emphasis added). These writers suggest that Palmerini is correct in say-
ing that ‘science writers tend to consider themselves on the same side of
the barricade as scientists and they do not see it as a problem’ (p. 122).
Judged in terms of ethos, these writers may be able to demonstrate
expertise in some science-related areas, and almost certainly will be
able to demonstrate access to scientific experts. However, by taking an
uncritical view of science, they fail to exhibit judgment and are there-
fore lacking one major component of ethos.
  While these examples and those in Chapter 4 show that many writers
exhibit a ‘reverential attitude’ (Nelkin, Selling, p. 105) toward science
and scientist, others see their role in more critical terms. As described in
Chapter 4, for example, Seely talks about the need for writers to evaluate
science critically and to represent readers’, not sources’, interests. Cope,
Begos, and Cook, all of whom were quoted in Chapter 4, refer to readers’
interests. To quote Cope again, ‘Some uncertainty need not impede cru-
cial action if the public understands why at best almost all a scientist can
say is: “Here’s our strong evidence that such-and-such is probably true”’
(pp. 19–20, emphasis original). Another practitioner, Wilkes, warns his
science-writing graduate students against a tendency to be ‘too respect-
ful of big-name scientists—indeed, of all scientists’ because such over-
identification ‘can weaken their interviewing and writing’ (p. 32).
  On the second question—how writers view readers—the answers
again range from a deficit-infused view that dwells on readers’ inca-
pacities to a belief in readers’ capability to understand even complex
scientific issues. The PAST model view of readers is conveyed by many
of the practitioner comments, quoted in Chapter 4, about the purposes
of popular science writing: in many cases, the genre is presented as a
way to cure readers, to alleviate their ignorance, and to improve them
by making them more like scientists.
  Three other themes enforce the impression that many popular sci-
ence writers have a low opinion of their readers. The first is an idea that
readers must be tricked or seduced into reading about science. Thus,
Shreeve recommends creating ways to ‘fold in some science’ and ‘to
sneak the scientific exposition in under the rug’ so that readers ‘end up
so entranced with your story they don’t even notice they’re learning at
the same time’ (pp. 143–4).
  The second theme is that readers are easily frightened by science.
Johnson says he works hard ‘to create a mental picture of the basic
mechanism without scaring off too many people’ (p. 134). Blum
also worries about scaring her audience, which she characterizes in a
                            Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 101


description of an imaginary reader who is ‘is unnerved by science to
begin with and would stop reading my story the minute I threw a multi-
syllabic medical term in her face’ (p. 27) and warns against losing such
readers—once they have been ‘snared’—by writing ‘too loftily’ (p. 27).
  A third theme paints a picture of readers as incapable of understanding.
The epitome of this perspective comes from Kanigel, who says readers

  confront quarks and quasars, protons and prions. They stumble over
  scientific notation and organic nomenclature; are baffled by incom-
  prehensibly large distances and minutely small ones; get lost in the
  dark borderland between science and pseudoscience; confuse what
  they know, what they think they know but don’t, what they once
  knew but no longer do, and what they never knew at all. (pp. 147–8)

The verbs tell a tale of struggle and incompetence: readers confront,
stumble, are baffled, and get lost. Furthermore, what little knowledge
people have acquired is cast into doubt as they have either forgotten it
or don’t understand it properly, and they don’t even understand their
own ignorance, as they don’t even know what they don’t know.
   In these examples, writers are tricking readers into reading about
science; the readers have no agency, no personal motivation to learn
about science. Instead, they are passive recipients of whatever the writer
serves up, their choices about the text boiled down to two: read or don’t
read; accept or reject the entire package the writer offers. Viewed from
a Traditional–Idealist point of view, these positions make perfect sense.
If a writer believes that science is a superior form of knowledge, then
arete is well served by presenting scientific knowledge as unproblematic
and as an end in and of itself, and the demands of phronesis are met by
evaluating scientific activities and knowledge only in scientific terms.
Similarly, the writer’s stance toward readers—preacher or teacher—is
informed by a view of science and scientific knowledge as automati-
cally good, and of readers’ lack of knowledge or interest as failings to
be overcome with the benevolent guidance of those who know better:
science writers. A critical response to this boosterism is best summed
up by Bauer: ‘In what is seen as the battle for the hearts of the public,
the key question is: how can we make science “sexy”? The “consumer”
public is to be seduced rather than rationally persuaded. According to
this logic, there is little difference between science and washing powder’
(‘Survey’, p. 120). This is the deficit model in full stride.
   The examples above contrast with CUSP model depictions of readers
who are curious and capable and ‘have their own persuasive resources’
102   Communicating Popular Science


(Myers, ‘Discourse’, p. 269), such as the ability to check what they
learn against what they already know and against their own experi-
ences. Writers in the Field Guide who assume readers are curious include
Siegfried, who says to ‘Give details that answer all the questions you can
imagine a reader asking’ (p. 17), and Cope, who refers to readers’ desire
for information, specifically about ‘the price tag for any new medical
treatments’ (p. 23). Mary Miller, Cook, and Chui also refer to an audi-
ence’s curiosity. Chui says specifically that she likes writing stories about
earth sciences because her readers are eager for the information she pro-
vides; in her home state of California, people want to know everything
about earthquakes from ‘whether an earthquake is coming and when’
to ‘insights into the geological machinery, deep beneath their feet,
that is slowly grinding toward the next Big One’ (p. 237). In a similar
vein, Guyer, Seely, and Holland talk about readers as intelligent; Guyer,
for example, says to ‘envision a smart reader who is unfamiliar with
and uninformed about the subject, but not stupid’ (p. 30). Seely, who
works for a small newspaper in Wisconsin, writes that ‘It is a mistake to
underestimate the sophistication of today’s newspaper reader’ and that
‘to attract and keep readers’ a paper should ‘offer substantial, interesting
stories that make a difference in subscribers’ lives, that speak to some-
thing they care about’ (p. 51). Holland says that most readers, even the
huge number online, ‘can readily tell the difference between good jour-
nalism and hype’ (p. 270). Similarly, in the Best American series, Angier
doesn’t agree that science is too hard for the ‘meager layperson’ (to use
Alan Lightman’s phrase) to understand. She asks, ‘Is science really any
harder than, say, Middle East politics? Or the fashion industry, for that
matter?’ and answers no, that while it is true that science ‘has its insid-
ers who are possessive of their trade and expertise and use jargon like
porcupines use their quills, smugly and defensively’ at the same time,
‘with a little effort, just about anybody can become reasonably literate
about science’ (pp. xviii–xix).
   Angier and others who express a belief in the reader’s intelligence,
interest, and competence offer a heartening counter to their colleagues
who describe readers’ tones ranging from dismissive to contemptuous.
Their respect for and confidence in readers are the basis on which a
CUSP model ethos is built.


Ethos in Popular Science Texts

This section elucidates how popular science texts create a CUSP-oriented
ethos.5 It begins with technical credibility, which is created through a
combination of arete (knowledge) and phronesis (judgment), then talks
                             Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 103


about trustworthiness, which is created through a combination of phro-
nesis and eunoia (friendliness).

CUSP 1: Kolbert Crafts a Knowledgeable Ethos
Elizabeth Kolbert, whose article on mass extinctions was analyzed in
Chapter 5, exemplifies a CUSP model ethos in much of her work. Here I
use a piece on climate change, ‘The Curse of Akkad’, to illustrate strate-
gies that demonstrate a writer’s technical credibility.
   For nonspecialist writers, showing they have access to experts is an
important way to convey authority, and the more a writer draws on,
and the more credible those experts are, the better. Kolbert does this
very well. First, she recounts conversations she had with researchers at
the Goddard Institute for Space Studies (GISS), including its director, the
famous climate scientist James Hansen, and another climate scientist,
David Rind, who has been at GISS since 1978. In addition to talking
with individual researchers, she also ‘attended a meeting at GISS which
brought together members of the institute’s modeling team’, which
included Hansen and about twenty-five other people. Other experts she
quotes include Harvey Weiss, an archeologist at Yale, and his ‘team of
students and local laborers’ (p. 40), whose work was first published in
Science; Peter deMenocal, a paleoclimatologist at Columbia University’s
Lamont–Doherty Earth Observatory, and his graduate student Heidi
Cullen; and a scientist with the National Oceanic and Atmospheric
Administration. The picture of herself that Kolbert creates is of some-
one who has access to respected experts and who seeks out a variety
of perspectives, and who can therefore be trusted to provide reliable
information.
   However, access to experts is not sufficient for CUSP in and of itself;
if it were not backed up by other forms of persuasion, it would pro-
mote a PAST view that if we have enough scientists’ perspectives, we
won’t need to think for ourselves. Fortunately, Kolbert also establishes
credibility by exhibiting a mastery of the subject matter. Subject mat-
ter knowledge while ‘patently part of the logos…serves ethos as well’
(Fahnestock and Secor, p. 91), and Kolbert conveys mastery a few ways.
First, Kolbert provides ample background information on a range of
topics; her explanations about everything from archaeological dating to
the use of ocean sediment cores to study past rainfall patterns also con-
tribute to her ethos as a well-informed writer. Kolbert also shows herself
as knowledgeable when she uses and defines scientific terminology,
as when she writes that climate modelers talk about something called
‘forcings’ and then explains that ‘A forcing is any ongoing process or
discrete event that alters the energy of the system. Examples of natural
104   Communicating Popular Science


forcings include, in addition to volcanic eruptions, periodic shifts in
the earth’s orbit and changes in the sun’s output, like those linked to
sun-spots’ (pp. 46–7).
   A third strategy Kolbert uses is to talk about methods. In scientific
writing, an author gains ethos by showing that he or she is ‘a credible
witness, following all proper procedures thoughtfully and carefully’
(Bazerman, Shaping, p. 140). In popular science writing, describing
methods shows the writer’s grasp of how scientific work is conducted.
Kolbert does this by talking about the methods that climate scientists
use and how those methods relate to what they learn. For example,
she explains that ‘there are two types of equations that go into a climate
model. The first group expresses fundamental physical principles, like
the conservation of energy and the law of gravity. The second group
describes—the term of art is “parameterize”—patterns and interactions’
(p. 44). Next, she shows how these differences can lead to different
kinds of results: ‘All climate models treat the laws of physics in the
same way, but, since they parameterize phenomena like cloud forma-
tion differently, they come up with different results. (At this point, there
are some fifteen major climate models in operation around the globe.)’
(p. 45). From the ease with which she defines technical terms to her
casual description of processes that most nonspecialists would have
no reason to know, Kolbert exemplifies the first aspect of ethos: arete,
excellence in knowledge.

CUSP 2: Kolbert Demonstrates Judgment
Kolbert’s discussion of research methods also demonstrates another
component of ethos: judgment. Rather than accepting the use of climate
models simply because experts use them, she talks about their draw-
backs and how scientists work around those drawbacks: ‘The obvious
difficulty in verifying any particular climate model or climate-model
run is the prospective nature of the results. For this reason, models are
often run into the past, to see how well they reproduce trends that have
already been observed’ (p. 46). She also does not take the information
from one team, but instead compares what she learns from one group,
such as GISS, with what she learns from others, such as the Hadley
Centre (part of the British Met Office) and Japan’s National Institute
for Environmental Studies. The effect of this critical judgment is that,
in addition to providing readers with an important insight into how
differences within climate studies may rise, Kolbert gives readers more
reason to trust that she is not blindly reporting facts, but instead can,
and will, talk about how the science behind them works.
                             Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 105


   In a final step, one which solidifies her grasp of the practical part
of ‘practical wisdom’, Kolbert places these questions into their larger
social, cultural, and political context. The article begins with the story
of an ancient civilization that fell owing to what is now recognized to
have been a serious climate shift, and returns periodically to the various
civilizations whose collapse was caused or hastened by climate-related
changes in local conditions—the Mayan civilization, the Tiwanaku civili-
zation in the Andes, the ancient Egyptian ‘Old Kingdom’, to name a few.
As Kolbert notes with a studied understatement, ‘the discovery that large
and sophisticated cultures have already been undone by climate change
presents what can only be called an uncomfortable precedent’ (p. 41).
   In talking about the potential consequences of changes happening
today, Kolbert touches on many questions that, while related to the
science of climate change, are important for civil society (and for the
planet as a whole). For example, she pairs the abstract—‘that the world
will enter a completely new climate regime, one with which modern
humans have no prior experience’—with the highly concrete vision
of a drought map, potential hazards shaded progressively from yellow
(bad) to brown (catastrophic), in which ‘the Northeast was yellow, the
Midwest was ochre, and the Rocky Mountain states and California were
brown’ (p. 51). She also brings in the political dimension—she men-
tions a conversation one scientist had with President Bush’s science
adviser—and quotes another scientist’s comment that climate change is
‘potentially geopolitically destabilizing’ (p. 51).6
   Through these and other moves, Kolbert’s work shows a range of
authority-building strategies for CUSP texts. This begins with her sub-
ject area knowledge, but is not limited to an ability to report factual ele-
ments; her text models a CUSP ethos because it includes understanding
the questions and methods behind the facts and the ability to exercise
critical judgment that includes both standards of scientific evidence and
the values of the broader society.

CUSP 3: Gordon Shows Friendliness Toward Readers
Although authority and judgment are important to popular science
texts, a text must also make sure readers feel included in a conversa-
tion rather than preached at or talked down to. Writers use many
strategies to create an approachable, informative, sympathetic persona,
and a term from linguistic pragmatics, ‘positive politeness’, describes
a number of these moves. Positive politeness can help ‘minimize the
social distance’ and power differences between writers and readers
(Myers, ‘Politeness’, p. 43), a skill that is particularly useful, given the
106   Communicating Popular Science


obscurity of much science writing and the high status of scientists, in
popular science writing.
   The positive politeness strategies in popular science texts fall into
three general categories: strategies for putting readers at ease, for invit-
ing the reader into the scene, and for creating an inclusive ‘we’ that
positions readers and writers together in thinking about science-related
issues. This section discusses these strategies and illustrates them using
Deborah M. Gordon’s ‘Close Encounters’. In this article Gordon, an
entomologist at Stanford, describes her field research on ant colony
behaviors, including several experiments she and her assistants conduct.
   In a situation that includes inherent power differentials and social
distance, ‘It takes some work for the participants to keep the distances
small’ (Myers, ‘Politeness’, p. 45). One way to do this is through humor,
especially humor aimed at oneself, which can ‘serve the effect of
putting the reader at ease and thus establishing rapport with the reader’
(Riley, p. 342). Gordon adopts a casual, friendly tone early in the article
by using some self-deprecating humor. On one page she compares her
research assistants to the insects they study, as their morning routine
always involves ‘some antlike milling around’ (p. 57), and on the next
page she turns this humor on herself: after describing her outfit, which
includes ‘a long-sleeved shirt, a cap with a kind of curtain around its
lower edge, and the largest sunglasses I can find’, she writes, ‘I look
rather like an insect myself’ (p. 58). Ironically, by turning herself into
an insect, she humanizes herself for her readers to overcome any social
distance they might feel between themselves and a Stanford science
professor.
   The writer can also ‘personalize the language through use of the
first person “I”’ (Riley, p. 343). This extends to including the ‘author’s
experiences and opinions’ because this presents him or her as a ‘co-
participant’ with the reader (Riley, p. 344). In the above quotes, Gordon
talks about her thought process as she does research. She also talks
about what drew her to study ants, giving readers a peek into her per-
sonal motivations; she wants to ‘try to figure out how each ant decides
what to do, and how that adds up to the achievements of colonies’ and
explains that ants are interesting because although they ‘are separate
beings that move around freely’, at the same time ‘nothing ants do
makes sense except in the context of the colony. Zoom in, and you see
ants—zoom out, and you see a colony. Ants and colonies are both there
in front of you, all the time’ (p. 58). Cementing the personal touch is
the fact that this passage also invites readers to participate through the
second person ‘you’.
                              Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 107


  Another way a writer can create a sense of mutuality with readers is
by providing a narrative that puts readers into the scene with the writer,
whether through use of the present tense (which tends to create a more
vivid experience than past tense), through metaphors that relate to
readers’ daily lives, or through detailed descriptions. Here is an example
of how Gordon makes her research site visible to readers:

   The twenty-five acres that I have inspected inch by inch for the
   past seventeen summers is part of a 7,000-acre cattle ranch. On
   the human scale, the site looks like a plain of chaparral scrub with
   mountains on either side. On the ant-colony scale, the site looks like
   a bumpy, sandy surface with lots of gray, orange and pink boulders
   and the occasional bush or plant. By the time it gets warm, about
   7:30 in the morning, the ground quietly teems with harvester ants.
   They are called harvester ants because they eat mostly seeds, which
   they store inside their nests. They will happily take termites as well
   when they can find them (p. 60).

This example, in addition to being a fine piece of descriptive prose,
is striking because it shows the readers the landscape they would be
likely to see—the plain and the chaparral—and also the landscape that
Gordon has learned to see through almost two decades of study.7
   Gordon also invites readers to participate virtually in experiments,
for example by going into detail about precisely what the researchers
did with the ants:

   We used paints of several colors, each color corresponding to one task.
   We collected ants as they foraged, patrolled, did nest-maintenance
   work or worked in the midden, or refuse pile. Then we marked them,
   let the paint dry, and released them. The next day we used some of
   the colonies with marked ants to do perturbation experiments and
   left others undisturbed as controls (p. 62).

Because Gordon had earlier described what a perturbation experiment
does, her use of researchers’ language here fits naturally into the descrip-
tion, further suggesting that she talks to readers as respected interlocutors.
   Finally, a positive persona is fostered when the ‘expositor defers to
the reader’s intelligence by exhibiting sensitivity to the complex issues
involved’ (Sipiora, p. 279). In fact, ‘acknowledging the complexity of
the subject matter’ (Riley, p. 347) is one form of what Riley refers to as
‘metaphorical gift giving’ (p. 346). Gordon uses this technique when
108   Communicating Popular Science


she frames her questions about ant behavior in the larger context of
what she struggles to understand, writing that ‘It is difficult to think
about how an ant colony works. Not only is an ant colony’s behavior
complex, woven from zillions of ant acts, but all those minute events
add up to something different from any society we know’ (p. 59). Later
she adds that the ‘most difficult thing to grasp about task allocation is
that it is not a deterministic process even at the individual level. An ant
does not respond the same way every time to the same stimulus; nor do
colonies’ (p. 64). Gordon doesn’t say that the concepts are difficult for
non-entomologists—she says they are difficult, period.


Ethos in Hirsh’s ‘Signs of Life’

Hirsh’s ‘Signs of Life’ illustrates how the different elements of ethos
work together as he demonstrates a blend of authority, friendliness,
and the ability to invite readers to share his appreciation of science,
while also considering what science means when viewed in a broader
context. The article’s opening paragraphs establish Hirsh’s authority, set
his informal tone, and introduce several positive politeness techniques.
He begins with a two-sentence paragraph: ‘It was a four-word e-mail.
“Booyah,” it said. “Sincerely, Freight Train”’ then continues:

  As soon as I read it, I stepped out of my office and hurried down the
  glass-walled corridor. Stealing a glance over Stanford’s red-tiled roofs
  in the day’s first light, I ducked into a room illuminated mainly by
  the cool fluorescence of a window-sized flatscreen. Ever since Hunter
  Fraser and I had been [the project], I’d been able to find him here at
  virtually any hour (p. 79).

His authority is established: Hirsh works at Stanford, he does scientific
research there, and he’s dedicated enough to his work to be there at all
hours. At the same time, Hirsh’s informality signals readers that he will
be writing to them as equals, not as an expert speaking down to the
ignorant. The informality continues in his description of his colleague,
Fraser, and of the conversation that followed the email:

  Over the course of the night, he had inched his way down his seat,
  so that his head was propped against the backrest and his legs were
  stretched out before him, showing me the bottoms of his socks.
  ‘Booyah?’ I asked.
  ‘Booyah,’ he replied, and nodded toward his screen (p. 79).
                            Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 109


Hirsh describes the graphics on Fraser’s screen, then continues:


  To explain why this simple little picture merited ‘Booyah’—a word
  that had somehow worked its way into our collaboration to announce
  a result that was almost too good to be true—I need to back up a bit,
  and begin fairly near the beginning.

  About a billion years ago, a population of single-celled animals, per-
  haps quite similar to the yeast that makes our bread rise, was divided
  into two groups. We don’t know how they were split, but we do
  know that while they were separated, one of the groups accumulated
  so many genetic changes that when the two were reunited, they
  could no longer exchange genetic material. In other words, they had
  become separate species (pp. 79–80).


Here Hirsh balances the authority established in his opening by using
several rapport-enhancing strategies: first-person narration engages
readers in a story, and the informality invites readers to feel that they
are insiders, not outside observers. Hirsh’s use of Fraser’s nickname is
another a shared intimacy he uses throughout the article; so, too, the
dry humor of ‘I need to back up a bit’ followed by ‘About a billion years
ago’ creates an inviting space for nonspecialists. In his reference to
yeast, Hirsh includes readers by comparing the single-celled animals to
something nonscientists know about; the effect is even stronger because
of his use of the first-person plural ‘our’ in ‘the yeast that makes our
bread rise’.
   Hirsh then explains that one of the species that evolved from that
long-ago yeast is still a yeast, and the other has developed into a worm,
a ‘tiny soil nematode’ (p. 80), and then uses another positive politeness
technique, saving face for readers. He writes:

  Despite their obvious differences—one a worm that lives in the dirt,
  the other a yeast you can buy at your supermarket—these two species
  still have a surprising amount in common: they both still carry many
  of the genes that were present in that grandfather yeast a billion
  years ago. (Forgive me if we’re rehashing high school bio here, but
  we’re almost there.) Each of these genes encodes a protein, a func-
  tional molecule that performs a specific job for the organism (p. 80).

He prevents a possible loss of face for readers by suggesting that he is
reviewing basic genetics not because his readers are stupid, but because
110   Communicating Popular Science


he needs to walk through the ‘high school bio’ and appreciates his read-
ers putting up with this digression.
  As he continues, Hirsh also includes readers in the scientific undertak-
ing itself. The paragraph continues, ‘For the past few decades, molecular
biologists have been identifying these interactions [between proteins]
and reporting them to various databases, where you or I can read about
them and play our own, maniacally complex game of connect the
dots, drawing a link between each pair of proteins that interact’ (p. 80).
Although readers are unlikely to poke around in protein interaction
databases, Hirsh chooses to express his belief that readers can, and per-
haps will, join him in exploring this aspect of the natural world.8
  Finally, Hirsh invites readers to consider the nature and limitations of
science, including readers in an exploration of where science fits in their
shared world. In this section of the article, Hirsh tells about how he and
his students were on a dive one morning and watched a fight between
two octopuses. He describes the fight in vivid detail, then talks about
how, later in the classroom, the students seem less engaged than usual.
One student proposes that maybe the mathematics Hirsh had written
would be more interesting if they were in the same colors as the fighting
octopuses had displayed—vivid reds and purples. This exchange gets
Hirsh thinking about the limits of science:

  After a moment, I realize that, in a sense, he’s right. The mathemati-
  cal language that once seemed incomparably powerful—capable of
  distilling the mess of evolution to a few key variables and precise,
  general rules—now seems pathetically deficient, inadequate to the
  task of capturing even the thinnest slice of what matters in nature.
  Today, the red of fury and the violet of dread seem far more useful
  symbols for understanding life in this bay (p. 83).

Ultimately, his speculation leads him to suggest that science is a power-
ful tool, but not one that obviates the need for any other ways of
comprehending the natural world: ‘The Book of Nature may well be
written in the language of mathematics, but it is written in many other
languages as well, and passages inscrutable in one may be legible in
another’ (p. 87), he writes, then continues,

  when we consider the relationship between Darwin’s simple mecha-
  nism and its wildly varied outcomes—when we remember that the
  relationship is not one of reduction or encapsulation, but rather one
  of intelligible creation—the tension between the observations of the
                             Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers 111


  organism and the contemplation of simple scientific models seems
  more or less to dissolve (p. 87).

The tension dissolves not only for evolution—the overt message here—
but also for the relationship between science, especially a rigorously
mathematical aspect of science, and appreciation of the vivid particu-
lars in nature more generally. Hirsh offers a way out of the over-reliance
on mathematics that can lead science to be too reductive by showing
that we can value both mathematics and other, less reductive, ways to
understand the world.
  Overall, Hirsh’s article demonstrates that popular science writing can
be authoritative and, at the same time, in Gieryn’s words, can admit
that ‘nature does not allow but one order of understandings’ (p. 17).
Circling back to ethos, this demonstrates phronesis, which, Aristotle
taught us, ‘recognizes a contingent world, the particularities of practical
existence and the possibility of wisdom—a virtue that involves an inter-
est in matters that transcend immediate circumstances’ (Fisher, p. 354).



Conclusion: Ethos and a New Social Contract

The nature and effect of an author’s textual presence is an incredibly
complex topic—one addressed here at only a very basic level. Even this
basic overview of ethos, however, shows that all three components of
ethos are essential to CUSP model popular science texts. Knowledge is
necessary, but not sufficient, as information without evaluation is sim-
ply public relations. Friendliness promotes CUSP by keeping the focus
on public values, and therefore on science-in-society rather than on sci-
ence in and of itself, and by treating readers as intelligent interlocutors
rather than as passive (at best) audiences.
  As in the chapters on boundary work and expertise (Chapters 5
and 6), the description of rhetorical concepts and applications here is
intended to help readers, writers, and critics become more attuned to
often-overlooked strategies in popular science texts. Greater awareness
may, in turn, help us move closer to a new, CUSP-based social contract
in a few ways. First, writers crafting a persona draw on existing models,
and the positive personas created by writers like Corson, Gordon, and
Hirsh show that it’s possible to have credibility without being conde-
scending. These writers offer models for writers who want to create
prose that respectfully invites readers to engage with scientific issues of
mutual interest.
112   Communicating Popular Science


   An emphasis on how writers present themselves to readers also helps
clarify where the writers’ allegiances lie. All too often, problems in
science popularization arise ‘because of tensions over what ought to
be communicated, and for whose benefit the communication is tak-
ing place’ (Russell, N. J., p. 70). When the audience community is just
insiders, then a strong emphasis on accuracy and credibility of infor-
mation is appropriate. When considering broader audiences, however,
understanding that arete must be complemented by phronesis shifts
the emphasis from technical accuracy to responsible action. Thus, ‘the
preserve of phronesis is insight into what to do when confronted by
conflicting alternatives. Its end is not theoretical knowledge but respon-
sible action to resolve the differences of divergent perspectives that
surface when we address concrete problems’ (Hauser, ‘Aristotle’, p. 12).
Kolbert’s ‘The Curse of Akkad’ is especially strong on this front, showing
how popular science texts can critically address an issue of concern to
scientists and nonscientists alike.
   Finally, a new social contract depends on changes in how science
communicators position themselves. Taylor suggests that if ‘a more
reflexive public persona were enacted in the interpenetrating cultural
discourses of science, then cynicism and anti-intellectualism would be
less likely outcomes of simple disagreement’ (p. 227). Such reflexivity in
public discussions of scientific issues is crucial if the new social contract
is to be sustainable. The publicly oriented ethos that might once have
been a courtesy on the part of those representing science in public dis-
course is now a necessity for future conversations.
8
Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting
Reader Engagement




Chapter 7 talked about how popular science writers position themselves
in texts and about some of the consequences for reader engagement. This
chapter looks at the ways that texts signal readers directly about how to
engage with science-related issues by presenting, or not presenting, cer-
tain kinds of information and arguments. Depending on how texts pose
problems, they may invite readers to be passive recipients of knowledge,
or to be engaged coparticipants in figuring out what the knowledge
means, or (usually) in some middle ground between these poles.
   This chapter uses the concepts of rhetorical orientations to describe
those invitations. It begins by describing the three primary rhetorical
orientations that texts can take—fact-based, value-based, and action-
based. Next, the chapter explains how a text’s use of these orienta-
tions signals where it falls on the PAST (Public Appreciation of Science
and Technology)–CUSP (Critical Understanding of Science in Public)
continuum, then reviews practitioner texts to see what they favor and
why. In the application to popular science texts, it illustrates a few
CUSP model strategies for each orientation, and then, in the integrated
analysis, it shows how all three orientations can work together to invite
critical engagement. Finally, it articulates how an applied understand-
ing of these orientations can help popular science texts promote a new
social contract.


Rhetorical Orientations and the PAST–CUSP Continuum

Rhetorical orientations provide a way to classify prose in terms of what
stance toward the topic the audience is expected to take. The three
orientations discussed from classic times to the present are forensic,
epideictic, and deliberative.

                                  113
114   Communicating Popular Science


• Texts with a forensic orientation ask readers to learn or judge facts.
  Examples of strongly forensic texts include scholarly publications
  (including scientific research articles), court documents, medical
  records, documentaries, informational signs at museums, encyclo-
  pedias, and news reports.
• Texts with an epideictic orientation ask readers to commit themselves
  to a particular value or values. Strongly epideictic texts include obitu-
  aries, values-oriented political speeches, wedding toasts, dedications
  (e.g., of buildings and bridges), graduation speeches, sermons (when
  they seek to install certain values in listeners), encomia, and odes.
• Texts with a deliberative orientation ask readers to make a decision
  about what kind of action should be taken and about who should
  act. Strongly deliberative texts include proposals (including grant
  proposals), action-oriented political speeches, petitions, pledge
  drives, student orientation sessions, ballot initiatives, and sermons
  (when they are intending to change listeners’ behavior).

Most texts include all three, but emphasize one, hence my referring to
them as ‘orientations’. (They are often referred to as ‘genres’—e.g., in
Perelman and Olbrecht-Tyteca’s classic book, The New Rhetoric: A Treatise
on Argumentation—but I avoid the term to avoid potential confusion
with genre theory.) For example, an obituary will include biographical
facts and may suggest actions (such as where to send flowers or make
donations), but will generally focus on praising the deceased person by
talking about the values for which he or she was known.1
  With many texts, the appropriate orientation is inherent in the genre;
a scientific research article is, de facto, a factual claim. In genres such
as popular science writing, however, the orientation differs from text to
text. One way to identify whether a particular text is primarily forensic,
epideictic, or deliberative is by using stasis theory, which involves ask-
ing what kinds of questions a text raises. The four2 categories are fact,
definition, values, and action.

• A text that asks about the existence of things, events, and actions is
  said to be operating at the stasis of fact.
• A text that asks about what kind of thing, event, or action it was is
  said to be operating at the stasis of definition.
• A text that asks about the qualities of the thing or event is said to be
  operating at the stasis of values.
• A text that asks about what should be done and by whom is said to
  be operating at the stasis of action.
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 115


Stasis theory arose in the legal domain, with agreement at each stasis
necessary before a case can move to the next one. Although its applica-
tion to popular science texts is more complex, it is helpful to see the
simple, unidirectional version first, then to elaborate on it.
   For the sake of illustrating how this works, let’s say you and I are at
my house, and I think I see something small run under a chair. I might
comment to you that I saw a small rodent. Moving through the stases,
we might address the following questions.

• Fact: Did I really see something? If we agree that I did see something,
  the next step is to decide what it was. If not, there’s no need for fur-
  ther discussion.
• Definition: Given that I saw something, what did I see? Perhaps you
  think I saw a ball of dust, while I maintain it was a small critter.
  We would remain at the stasis of definition, wrangling over whether
  I saw a dust ball or a rodent, until the issue was resolved. If it was
  dust, the conversation would be over (though maybe you would
  suggest I vacuum). If we agree I saw a rodent, we’d move to the next
  stasis, values.
• Values: Given that I saw a rodent, was it a good rodent (my pet
  mouse) or was it a bad rodent (a wild mouse)? The answer here would
  affect what happens in the next step, action.
• Action: Given that I saw a bad rodent (a wild mouse), what should be
  done and who should do it? Do we both decide or is it my decision
  because it’s my house? Am I the only person who decides or should
  I include other residents in the discussion?

   Orientations and stases together provide a useful framework for
assessing what kind of subject position a text creates for readers, as the
first two stases (fact and definition) suggest a forensic orientation, and
the next two (values and action) suggest epideictic and deliberative ori-
entations, respectively. Figure 8.1 show stases and orientations for the
question of whether I have a mouse in my living room.
   Of course, the conversation is unlikely to be this straightforward.
Although explanations of stasis theory do say the stases ‘build upon
each other from simpler to more complex questions and from seemingly
value-neutral work to value-intensive work’ (Walsh, ‘The Common’,
p. 129), the key here is the word ‘seemingly’. In reality, even the first
stasis is affected by values and existing beliefs. Stases are conveniently
presented in a linear order, but it is more accurate to say that they inter-
act with one another.

  Elaborating on the mouse situation shows how discussions move
around between the stases. In this case resolution in the fourth stasis
(to use live traps) leads to new questions in the first and second stases.
First, are live traps effective (fact)? The answer to that question depends
on our definition of ‘effective’. If we mean, ‘Do they catch mice?’,
the answer is probably ‘yes’, but if we mean, ‘Will they clear out the
entire mouse family that we suspect is lurking behind the baseboards?’,
the answer is probably ‘no’ (Figure 8.2).

Figure 8.2      Orientations, stases, and what to do about the mouse
118   Communicating Popular Science


   As this figure demonstrates, a simple question in one stasis actually
carries with it a myriad of questions from other stases. To focus on one
stasis to the exclusion of others may imply an assumed agreement.
If I say ‘What was that?’ instead of ‘Did you see that?’, I am assuming
you saw something, too. If I say ‘Should we poison it or trap it or borrow
a cat?’, I am assuming that we both saw and identified a mouse (first
and second stases), and that we both agree that mice are not welcome
in the house (third stasis).
   When a text focuses on one stasis, it similarly implies that readers agree
with the implicit resolutions of the other stases. Therefore, one important
goal in talking about science is to address what Kolstø describes as ‘epis-
temological questions’ that address both science-specific and contextual
factors—questions that look not only at scientific ‘evidence and its rel-
evance’ but also at ‘questions for social information, like sources of claims
and evidence, possible interests involved, competence of claim holders,
and level of consensus among scientists’ (p. 305).
   For example, Richard Karban and Mikaela Huntzinger’s handbook, How
to Do Ecology, has the following suggestions for choosing a research topic:
‘The question that you pick should reflect your goals as a biologist…For
example, this might include solving a particular problem in conserva-
tion, such as whether a single large or several small reserves are more
beneficial for amphibian diversity in your region’ (p. 1). The question
of what is considered a problem falls in the second and third stases—
that is it’s a question of definition and of values—and an ecologist’s
answer to that question precedes and underlies a given research project.
Furthermore, values beyond the specific scientific domain come into
play, as conservation is a social, as well as a scientific, concern.
   Karban and Huntzinger also write about different research methods
and how choices made in this area have consequences for what the
researcher learns:

   Much of what you can learn about ecology depends on the ques-
   tions that you ask. Your preconceptions and intuition determine the
   factors that you choose to examine, and these will constrain your
   results. Ecologists take several different approaches to science, and
   these approaches also constrain the kinds of answers that they will
   get. Answers to the questions that you ask then form your view of
   how the natural world works. Deciding on an approach may sound
   like a bunch of philosophical nonsense to waste time, but it can have
   important consequences on everything that follows (p. 16).
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 119


It helps to think of methods as affordances, that is heuristics that help
reveal some aspects of a situation, but at the inevitable expense of
others.3 Putting this in terms of orientations and stases, values (which
are the province of epideictic rhetoric) affect decisions about how to
conduct a study, and those decisions affect findings (which are the
province of forensic rhetoric). And, as shown earlier, even if the deci-
sion about what methods to use is based on which approach is better
for answering a given question, the choice of question itself is based on
particular values.
   Given this understanding of rhetorical orientations, we can evaluate
the level and type of engagement that popular science texts invite by
looking at how many stases they address and in what level of depth.
Three questions about rhetorical orientations in popular science texts
are useful for understanding where texts fall on the PAST–CUSP con-
tinuum: Which rhetorical orientation do they emphasize? Within each
one, how deeply do they engage with the questions each suggests? And
how well do they show the interplay between the forensic, epidictic,
and deliberative aspects of a science-related issue?
   The answers for PAST model texts are epideictic, shallowly, and poorly.
Scholars have noted that popular science texts tend to be ‘overwhelm-
ingly epideictic’ and that ‘their main purpose is to celebrate rather than
validate’ (Fahnestock, ‘Accommodating’, p. 333), and this is certainly
true of PAST model texts. When they engage in forensic prose, they
tend to do so shallowly, presenting facts without much, if any, explana-
tion of the knowledge-making processes involved, while deliberative
aspects focus on applications, especially those that are new, exciting,
or beneficial. Connections between stases are similarly tenuous in PAST
model texts, as they generally present an uncomplicated march through
the stases, from fact to definition to quality to action, as in Figure 8.1,
without considering how answers in one affect questions and answers
in the others.
   In CUSP model texts, where the focus is on science-in-society rather
than on science-in-itself, and the goal is critical engagement, all three
aspects are important. The forensic information involves readers in
the fact-finding aspects of science; the epideictic brings in the relevant
social and scientific values at play in a given situation; and the delibe-
rative completes the picture by addressing questions of what actions
(if any) should be taken and by whom. As important as including the
three orientations is the way that CUSP model texts show interconnec-
tions between them.
120   Communicating Popular Science


Rhetorical Orientations Described in Practitioner Texts

To find out what subject positions popular science writers recommend
creating, I considered two questions:

1. What kind of forensic, epideictic, and deliberative information do
   they think popular science texts should include?
2. Do they talk about how the three kinds of information interact?

Consistent with earlier analyses in earlier chapters, especially Chapters 4
and 5, the answer ranges from texts that conceive of popular science
writing as a praise-oriented epideictic genre to a belief that popular
science texts should give readers the information that lets them take a
knowledgeable and critical stance.
   At the PAST end of the spectrum are the texts that limit their discus-
sion of forensic elements to facts. In the Field Guide, Glick suggests
that ‘what matters…pretty much is, as Joe Friday said, “Just the facts”’
(p. 301). Johnson suggests that science writers serve ‘a noble cause: get-
ting as close as linguistically possible to scientific truth’ (p. 137). This
echoes Folger’s reference to the ‘cold truths from the unflinching, vast
perspective of science’ (‘Foreword’, 2002, p. xiii) and his suggestion
that ‘Sometimes the bare facts of a story speak for themselves’ (the
result, of course, being that the facts make ‘the awe and wonder imme-
diately apparent’) (‘Foreword’, 2009, p. xi). Similarly, Ridley says that a
well-chosen metaphor ‘can reveal the truth’ (p. xii)—truth being what
science writing communicates—while Dawkins laments the existence
of ‘some people who positively prefer ignorance and resent being told
the truth’ (p. xv). These texts imply that the forensic aspect of popular
science texts begins and ends with ‘facts’ and ‘the truth’.
   On the epideictic front, earlier chapters illustrate that scholars such as
Bauer, Fahnestock, and Trench are not far off in thinking that popular
science writers lean toward celebrations of science rather than toward
critical engagement. This tendency shows up in the themes of wonder,
delight, and awe (see Chapter 4), and in some writers’ declarations of
allegiance to science and scientists; consider, for example, Kunzig’s
advice in the Field Guide that readers should be skeptical, but not too
skeptical, and should ‘always remember that 95 percent of scientists—
and since I’m making the number up, I might as well say 99 percent—are
honest, well-meaning, not seriously conflicted people. They’re ordinary
people (most of them) who are doing something extraordinary. They’re
out there beavering away on the edge of knowledge’ (p. 131). Another
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 121


common problem comes from an emphasis on newness; the Field Guide
editors claim one of the ‘common goals’ for all science writers is to ‘find
out what’s new and intriguing, or new and useful, or new and just plain
fun to write about’ (Blum et al., ‘Similarities and Differences’, p. 258),
implying that if science writers cannot find something new, they might
as well not bother writing.
   Likewise, the PAST end of the deliberative spectrum is represented by
science writers who say their prose should improve citizens by making
them more like scientists. For example, Pinker touted the ‘amoral analy-
sis’ of a scientist as ‘a mode of thought that we may have to get used to
if we want to improve human affairs’ (p. xviii), while Greene advocated
applying a ‘scientific mindset’ to issues that ‘that seemingly bear no
direct relation to science’ (p. xvi). Even issues where writers could find
a more engaging deliberative angle, such as funding, tend to be nar-
rowly addressed; for example, in the Field Guide Yam mentions funding
as a way to sniff out potential stories (p. 7), and Cook’s hypothetical
scenario about a presidential address on stem cells refers to policy issues
as part of the ‘B-matter’, the information that ‘will have to be in the
story but that is not part of the news’ (p. 115).
   Underscoring the narrowness is the fact that some practitioners do list
these kinds of issues as important to science stories, but only when writ-
ing for scientists; for example, Powledge says that writing for nonscien-
tists should include basic details and consumer applications, whereas
writing for scientists should discuss ‘the structure of the project and its
findings, who’s participating, as well as any new scientific techniques or
procedures that will be employed’ (Soares, quoted in Powledge, p. 99).
Similarly, Norman’s chapter on writing for trade journals—i.e., writing
for science insiders—raises deliberative issues such as ‘the politics of
agencies that fund research, the growing commercial stake in academic
research, regulations’ (p. 72). These are considered ‘essential ingredients
in the coverage of science for a scientific readership’ (Norman, p. 72),
but are nearly absent from chapters on writing for broader audiences.
   Support for CUSP model use of the three orientations is less common
than support for PAST model approaches, but the writers who pro-
mote it talk about a range of strategies and reasons for doing so. Some
recommend sharing the scientific evidence and reasoning with audi-
ences. For example, Miller describes how the climate change exhibit
at the museum where she works used ‘content from different research
organizations and universities’ so that museum-goers would have ‘the
means…to interpret and understand the data that scientists themselves
gather and evaluate’ (‘Museums’, p. 297). Cook also suggests discussing
122   Communicating Popular Science


data, and says to offer alternative explanations (p. 113), while Cope
takes these questions even further, noting that claims may be ‘believa-
ble’, but ‘based on biased or other questionable data’ (p. 18). He suggests
that writers make sure to include information about the design and
implementation of a study, and to ask scientists questions such as ‘What
cautions should people have in viewing your conclusions?’ (p. 22).
  Cope also urges writers to hedge claims, presenting them with appro-
priate caveats, a strategy with important deliberative, as well as forensic
implications. Revkin, referring to environmental writing, agrees: it is
important, he says, ‘to try to understand—and convey—the tentative
nature of scientific knowledge, to retain at least some shades of gray
in all that black and white’ (‘The Environment’, p. 227). This kind of
advice suggests that it is better to share evidence, admit to doubt, and
convey complexity, even if doing so runs the risk of showing gaps in
what is known, than to imply more confidence than is warranted and
potentially mislead readers, lose their trust, and skew the basis on which
deliberative decisions are made.
  This public service orientation shows up as well in the writers’ opin-
ions about what epideictic and deliberative information to include.
Revkin, for example, points out in his chapter on ‘The Environment’
that an emphasis on novelty can ‘impede or distort environmental cov-
erage’ (p. 224). He adds that while ‘News is almost always something
that happened today…most of the big environmental themes of this
century concern phenomena that are complicated, diffuse, and poorly
understood’ (p. 224), a tacit acknowledgement that deliberative action
must take place, even in the face of scientific uncertainty. On a differ-
ent front, but one that is equally important in an era of post-academic
science, Squires notes that much research on nutrition ‘ends up being
sponsored by the food and dietary supplement industries’, and while this
‘doesn’t necessarily affect the results’, readers still need to know (p. 173).
  The strongest example of a CUSP model orientation in the Field Guide,
one that integrates questions at all levels, comes from an investigative
science journalist who says that popular science texts can, and should,
effect social change. The journalist, Regalado, describes the article he
wrote about Jesse Gelsinger, a young man who died from experimental
gene therapy without having been adequately informed of the risk. As a
result of this story, Regalado says a ‘federal investigation ensued, studies
were halted, and the National Institutes of Health created new rules to
make research safer for patients’ (‘Investigative Reporting’, p. 122). He
concludes that ‘Investigative reporting is worth doing. It can change
how government or industry works, help protect people from harm,
                       Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 123


and improve our society in important ways’ (‘Investigative Reporting’,
p. 125). Here the forensic, epideictic, and deliberative come together in
an example of how popular science texts can promote critical public
engagement.
  Overall, although some practitioners advocate for popular science
writing as ‘sensationalism and gee-whizzery’ (Gregory and Miller,
p. 116), others recognize and promote its role as an important arena of
democratic discourse.


Forensic Orientations in Popular Science Texts

All popular science texts have some forensic prose, if only because they
communicate factual information about science. Texts that stop there,
conveying only cold scientific knowledge, or conveying all knowledge
as if it were cold, are characterized as ‘weakly forensic’—these PAST
model texts offer facts and definitions as if these facts and definitions
are settled. CUSP model texts also include facts and definitions, and also
the support for or reasoning behind them. Instead of presenting facts
as if they are universal, objective truths, these strongly forensic texts
discuss findings, methods, evidence, interpretation, and the rest of the
scientific knowledge-making apparatus.
  Science and math educators Roger T. Cross and Ronald F. Price say
that good science communication happens in ‘a responsible, educative
manner, with evidence provided and discussed’ (p. 776), and argue that
this helps people learn ‘to penetrate the “rhetoric of conclusions”’ typi-
cal of much science popularization and education (p. 783) by showing
the interpretive activities necessary to move from data to results. In the
same way, a strongly forensic text invites readers to engage critically
with the factual aspects of the science. The first step is to distinguish
cold from hot science by differentiating between proof and probability.
The second is to offer readers insights into the areas of agreement,
the reasons for disagreement, the nature of the uncertainty, and other
information that is necessary to not just understand but evaluate the
scientific claims being made.

CUSP 1: Preston Distinguishes Hot From Cold Science
CUSP-oriented texts emphasize that uncertainty is a normal part of
science, that ‘At any given time, the understanding of a phenomenon
is likely to be incomplete, with conflicting explanations and evidence’
( Jenkins, S.H., pp. 5–6). Distinguishing proof from probability means
both giving a sense of how ‘hot’ the science is, but also maintaining an
124   Communicating Popular Science


appropriate level of uncertainty, for example by using hedging devices.
Also known as ‘stance markers’, these include textual features, such as
‘possibility modals (e.g. may, could), adjectives and adverbs expressing
degrees of probability (e.g. likely, perhaps), and “distancing” verbs (e.g.
suggest, seem)’ (Atkinson, p. 103, emphasis original). Richard Preston’s
‘Climbing the Redwoods’ illustrates how a popular science writer can
create a compelling text that comes to very few conclusions about
anything.
  Preston starts confidently, with information about redwoods, such as
their range, their scientific taxonomy and nomenclature, a description
of their ‘fibrous, furrowed bark, flat needles, and small seed-bearing
cones the size of olives’ (p. 313), their life cycle, and so on. Having
presented the known facts—the cold science—about redwoods, Preston
introduces the first of many unknowns:

   No one knows exactly how old the biggest coast redwoods are
   because nobody has ever drilled into one of them to count its annual
   growth rings. Botanists think that the oldest redwoods may be some-
   where between two thousand and three thousand years old. They
   seem to be roughly the age of the Parthenon (p. 315).

Preston conveys the impression of great age without expressing more
certainty than is called for. In addition to saying right out that ‘no one
knows’ how old the trees are, he hedges five times in reporting specula-
tions about their age: ‘Botanists think (1) that the oldest redwoods may
be (2) somewhere (3) between two thousand and three thousand years
old. They seem (4) to be roughly (5) the age of the Parthenon’.
   The same movement between fact and possibility appears throughout
the article, distinguishing between textbook science—established as
fact among scientists in a field—and the frontier science where most
research happens, and where theories are developed, tested, often
rejected, and sometimes adopted. Preston’s careful qualifiers contrast
with Lynn Margulis and Emily Case’s tendency to start with a caveat
and then become more certain when repeating the claim they hedged
initially. In ‘The Germs of Life’, they describe the role that microbes play
in the microcosm of an individual human’s health and in the macro-
cosm of the biosphere as a whole. Hedges are frequent, but moments of
caution are soon followed by unqualified declarations that negate the
effect of that caution. In one of several examples, a statement about
what they ‘think’ and ‘suspect’ is followed by the cocky ‘we expect to
prove’ (p. 125) what happened as long as two billion years ago (p. 124).
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 125


  Hedging works best when the writer does not then reify what
was just presented as uncertain; hedges should stay hedged. Preston’s
do, and his text’s balance of confidence and uncertainty makes it an
example of how to communicate both the known and the unknown
without exaggerating either, acknowledging the complexity of study-
ing an ecosystem, while also showing what researchers have come to
understand about it so far. Whereas unhedged texts ‘leap to results’
(Fahnestock, ‘Rhetoric of Science’, p. 284), Preston encourages readers
to look at scientific claims in terms of degrees of certainty, not a binary
of right-versus-wrong.

CUSP 2: Mann Situates Readers in the Scientific Debate
CUSP texts also provide background knowledge necessary to under-
stand not just the topic but the specific research issues. They include
general facts that explain the disciplinary context, study-specific
facts that explain the question being examined, and insights into the
research methods and disciplinary assumptions underlying a study
(study design, rationale, limitations, techniques, and important differ-
ences of opinion about each of these areas). Finally, they include readers
in interpretive activities, such as how the evidence relates to the results,
how findings fit with existing knowledge, and what possible alternative
explanations are accepted among scientists. In these texts, the scien-
tists’ ‘reasoning processes…are part of the story. The news is not simply
that something occurred. The news is the range of questions, uncer-
tainties, and interpretations the reporter, scientists, and other rational
observers would use to identify and frame this event’ (Rowan, p. 203),
rational observers who include readers of popular science texts. Mann
does all of these things in his description of a dispute among archaeolo-
gists and anthropologists about how many people were living in the
Americas before the Europeans arrived. A short section in the middle of
the article illustrates how he orients readers with basic knowledge about
the field and issue, includes evidence and interpretation, and discusses
research methods.
   Like other sections, ‘Green Prisons’ provides background information
a researcher in the field would know, such as the description, location,
and significance to archaeologists of a particular site, and also the
technical aspects of the research. For example, when Mann explains
why European diseases had a devastating effect on indigenous popula-
tions, he says that ‘Indigenous biochemistry’ could have contributed
to the problem, and gives relevant information about how the human
immune system works: ‘The immune system constantly scans the body
126   Communicating Popular Science


for molecules that it can recognize as foreign—molecules belonging to
an invading virus, for instance. No one’s immune system can identify
all foreign presences. Roughly speaking, an individual’s set of defensive
tools is known as his MHC type’ (p. 35). After a short explanation of
how MHC types work, he explains that ‘Most human groups contain
many MHC types; a strain that slips by one person’s defenses will be
nailed by the defenses of the next’, but Native Americans ‘are charac-
terized by unusually homogenous MHC types’ (p. 35). Mann then ties
this information back to the argument that European diseases had a far
greater effect in the Americas than elsewhere.
   Mann also gives the disciplinary context by explaining the standard
anthropological view of the Amazon and its ability to support human
populations. Citing Betty J. Meggers’ 1971 classic, Amazonia: Man and
Culture in a Counterfeit Paradise, he explains the belief that because of
the poorness of the Amazonian soils, ‘Indian villages were forced to
remain small’ (p. 43), a view Meggers still holds. Referring to a conver-
sation he had with Meggers, he says that ‘any report of “more than a
few hundred people” in permanent settlements, she told me recently,
“makes my alarm bells go off’” (p. 43).
   Having presented the prevailing wisdom in the field, as represented
by an established text and the scholar who wrote it, Mann brings in
another perspective, that of Anna C. Roosevelt. Roosevelt’s 1991 book,
which looks at the same site that Meggers examined, is ‘like the anti-
matter version of Amazonia’ (p. 43). Mann quotes Roosevelt’s claim
that the area contained ‘one of the outstanding indigenous cultural
achievements of the New World’ with ‘possibly well over 100,000
inhabitants’ (p. 43), and explains that Roosevelt’s argument is based
on the idea that


  Rather than damaging the forest, Marajó’s “earth construction”
  and “large, dense populations” had improved it: the most luxuriant
  and diverse growth was on the mounds formerly occupied by the
  Marajóara. “If you listen to Meggers’ theory, these places should have
  been ruined,” Roosevelt says (p. 41, emphasis original).


Here we see an alternative point of view on the Amazon and also some
of the archaeologist’s evidence and the reasoning that connects her
evidence with her conclusion. Were Meggers right, the area could not
have such biological richness; because it does, Roosevelt claims, her
own argument is strong.
                       Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 127


  Mann doesn’t leave the reader with this ‘Meggers says’ versus ‘Roosevelt
says’ exchange, however. Returning to Meggers, he brings in questions
of methodology:

  Roosevelt, Meggers argued, had committed the beginner’s error of
  mistaking a site that had been occupied many times by small, unsta-
  ble groups for a single, long-lasting society. ‘[Archaeological remains]
  build up on areas of half a kilometer or so,’ she told me, ‘because
  [shifting Indian groups] don’t land on exactly the same spot. The
  decorated types of pottery don’t change much over time, so you can
  pick up a bunch of chips and say, ‘Oh, look, it was all one big site!’
  Unless you know what you’re doing, of course’ (pp. 43–4).

Here again, Mann offers a claim backed up by the reasoning for it, not
only Meggers’ argument that Roosevelt’s claim is wrong but also her
explanation of the flaws in Roosevelt’s study that lead to an unsup-
ported claim. Mann’s next quotation from Roosevelt offers a similar,
though implicit, critique of others’ research methods: ‘Meanwhile,
Roosevelt’s team investigated [the site]’ and

  When the traces of human occupation vanished, they kept dig-
  ging. (“You always go a meter past sterile,” Roosevelt says.) A few
  inches below they struck the charcoal-rich dirt that signifies human
  habitation—a culture, Roosevelt said later, that wasn’t supposed to
  be there (p. 44).

The suggestion is that people claiming lack of evidence—Meggers,
perhaps—are operating on inadequate evidence because they didn’t
follow standard archaeological methods.
  Although readers don’t have all the information readers following
the Meggers–Roosevelt debate in Latin American Antiquity would have,
Mann offers enough information for readers to understand some of
the evidence and criteria that the archaeologists use. Nor does Mann
leave the reader with these two perspectives; he complicates the picture
further by citing evidence and reasoning from other sources—including
an anthropological botanist, an archaeologist, a soil geographer, a geog-
rapher, and several more archaeologists (pp. 44–5)—who offer possible
support for Roosevelt’s argument.
  This section began with an obvious idea, that including in-depth
information helps create forensically rich texts. However, as Kolstø
argues, even when ‘comprehension of scientific aspects is needed to
128   Communicating Popular Science


understand the debate among scientists involved, a more urgent issue
is to increase lay decision makers’ information vigilance, their ability
to scrutinize knowledge claims stated’ (Kolstø, p. 306). In light of this
need for critical reading, it is difficult to overemphasize the importance
of including methods, evidence, and interpretation in popular science
texts. Mann’s article shows how popular science writing can, in fact,
include what goes on behind the scenes in science. His contextual
descriptions, combined with his sharing evidence, arguments, and rea-
soning, allows readers to consider the studies from many angles, includ-
ing both social and scientific factors.


Epideictic Orientations in Popular Science Texts

Epideictic writing is ‘intended to strengthen shared beliefs about
the present state of affairs’ (Bizzell and Herzberg, p. 3), usually with the
idea of motivating particular kinds of belief or action. Epideictic prose
in popular science texts ranges from technocratic to democratic; while
popular science texts are often heavily epideictic in a way that over-
emphasizes the exciting and dramatic aspects of science, they can also
promote more democratically significant values.
   Technocratic science writing tends to be skewed toward the sensa-
tional types of epideictic rhetoric, with information selected not for its
scientific merit or interest, but for the excitement or drama it can con-
vey. However, set to different ends than stirring up awe or praising heroic
individuals, epideictic prose can serve important civic functions. At its
best, it can bring areas of tension out into the open for discussion, and
it can help resolve tensions by identifying common values or by helping
facilitate civil debate even where common values are not identified. As
Lois Agnew explains, epideictic rhetoric ‘not only engages with cultural
values that are recognized by the audience but also involves speakers and
audiences in the creative enterprise of exploring new ways of conceiv-
ing those values’ (p. 149). In terms of showing how science works, this
means avoiding the drama impulse and instead showing science a com-
munal effort, one in which differences of opinion are not only normal
but productive. In broader terms, it means valuing scientific knowledge
as important to, but not a replacement for, democratic decision-making.

CUSP 1: Canby Shows Scientists as Real People
in a Shared Undertaking
To move away from quasi-religious views of science and hagiography
of scientists, popular science writing can replace depictions of scientists
                       Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 129


as ‘the heroes of the modern age’ (Broks, ‘Understanding’, p. 36) and
‘remote but superior wizards, above ordinary people’ (Nelkin, Selling,
p. 14) with more accurate depictions of them as real people engaged in
a communal process of discovering and making knowledge about the
natural world. This is not to say that popular science writing should
avoid valorizing people whose actions merit celebration, but rather that
it better serves science by ‘celebrating the deeds of exemplars’ in lan-
guage that will ‘inculcate a common vocabulary of excellence’ (Hauser,
‘Aristotle’, p. 14 and 19). The question is not whether popular science
texts should be celebratory, but what should be celebrated.
   Quammen’s and Canby’s articles, discussed in Chapter 6 for their dif-
ferent representations of local knowledge, offer contrasting visions of
how scientists, and science more generally, are presented. At the PAST
end of the spectrum, Quammen uses epideictic prose that sets the sci-
entist Fay apart from other, lesser humans, as Fay’s superior knowledge
of the forest (described in Chapter 6) is matched by his physical tough-
ness and larger than life personality. Quammen devotes many passages
to how tough Fay is, concluding that ‘Less determined travelers…just
don’t get to the places where Fay goes’ (p. 240) and that Fay is ‘an
untamable man’ (p. 242). He also makes Fay out to be a larger-than-
life character in other ways, ‘a man of humor and generous intellect’
(p. 242) with a ‘gift for command, a level of personal force and psycho-
logical savvy that allows him to push a squad of men forward through
difficult circumstances’ (pp. 240–1). Thus, Fay is the moving force
in this group—or, as Quammen calls it, Fay’s ‘entourage’ (p. 239)—
intellectually and otherwise, a man apart from and above others. Fay’s
singular status also extends beyond the immediate group, as there are
apparently no other scientists working with Fay. The closest Quammen
comes to suggesting interactions with other researchers is a mention
that Fay’s enormous database of information will provide information,
‘should anyone want to know’ (p. 244), and reference to his making
‘recordings of birdsong for later identification by experts’ (p. 241).
   This hagiography and lack of references to scientific conversations
with peers make Fay an exciting character, but do little to promote an
understanding of science; as Charney writes, ‘adopting the perspective
of an individual scientist or inventor sets up inappropriate oppositions,
such as hero-villain or individual-system. The account may accurately
reflect the views and experiences of that individual, but it sidelines
the broader scientific conversation on the problem’ (‘Lone’, p. 237).
Canby’s article includes that broader scientific conversation. Although
he initially describes Blake in heroic terms as ‘audacious, a biologist
130   Communicating Popular Science


who thinks nothing of crossing wild forests clad in sandals and a pair
of shorts’ (p. 2), Canby never implies that Blake is superhuman person-
ally or intellectually; as the comparison in Chapter 6 shows, Blake is
part of team out in the jungle, learning from others, as well as from his
surroundings. Furthermore, Blake’s research is part of a collaborative
venture. Although Canby focuses on Blake, he also mentions Richard
Ruggiero, who ‘runs the elephant fund for the U.S. Fish and Wildlife
Service’ (p. 2) and who has worked with Blake; Amy Vedder, ‘a program
director at the Wildlife Conservation Society’ (p. 3) that funded Blake’s
work; and Billy Karesh, a field veterinarian with whom Blake worked
in tracking, sedating, and putting Global Positioning System collars
