on wild elephants (pp. 9–10); and ‘Andrea Turkalo, a forest-elephant
researcher’ with whom Blake shares data (p. 16).
   Quammen’s tale of Fay as a scientist hero might be compelling, but
Canby shows that the work of even the most heroic scientists involves
a widespread network of other people, including other scientists and
institutions. In fact, Canby’s article includes two passages about Fay.
One explains that Fay did his initial survey ‘As part of a continent-
wide elephant census that began with the conservation efforts, the
Wildlife Conservation Society and the European Economic Community
contracted to estimate the elephant population in the north of the
Republic of Congo’ (p. 6). The second describes how Fay worked with
Amy Vedder and William Weber, ‘directors of the Wildlife Conservation
Society’s Africa program’, to write the proposal for Nouabalé-Ndoki park
(p. 7). Had Quammen included this information about Fay in his arti-
cle, the image of Fay may not have been as glorious, but the image of
science would have been more accurate.

CUSP 2: Corson Shows Conflict and Differences
of Opinion as Normal
Given the communal nature of science, it should be no surprise that
differences of opinion abound, and yet disputes and negotiations are
one of the least publicized—or least accurately publicized—aspects of
science. Kolstø writes that ‘one of the main frustrations mentioned by
lay people trying to understand discussions concerning socioscientific
controversial issues and trying to form an opinion is the perceived
disagreement among scientists and other experts’ (p. 294), and adds
that disagreements are sometimes interpreted ‘in terms of interests, per-
sonal opinions, and incompetence’ (p. 294). That’s not always wrong,
especially when it comes to interests; as Chapter 9 explains, conflicts of
interest are entirely possible in science (think of all the scientific studies
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 131


defending tobacco or today’s energy industry-funded studies refuting
anthropogenic climate change). However, understanding that legiti-
mate science does involve disagreement may ‘diminish the tendency to
interpret diverging statements in terms of interests and incompetence
without further evidence for this to be the case’ (Kolstø, p. 295).
   Corson’s lobster article offers an example of how to show that differ-
ences of opinion are a natural part of science. Despite the testiness of
the interactions between the ecologists and lobstermen on the one hand
and the fisheries scientists on the other, both approaches are ultimately
shown to be useful. Corson explains that a new model emerged from the
combined work of the lobstermen and ecologists on one hand and the
fisheries scientists on the other. He talks about how the model seemed
effective, but then ‘the data the ecologists have collected on lobster lar-
vae and baby lobsters complicate the picture’ (p. 156). After explaining
some contradictions between what the lobstermen–ecologist team and
the fisheries people expected and saw, he says that two ecologists ‘teamed
up with Michael Fogarty, the population modeler at the National Marine
Fisheries Service, to develop a different kind of model—one that would
use the ecologists’ new data to predict future catches’ (p. 156).
   Where some popular science texts might simply stop with an account
of the conflict between the two groups of researchers—an exciting and
dramatic story in its own right—Corson has followed through and
shown that disagreements are not exceptions in science, but essential
to the process by which it advances. His article is explicit about how
scientific knowledge claims are negotiated and how understandings
and models of the natural world will change over time as a result. At
the same time, it shows that people can take reasonable actions based
on the best knowledge to date.

CUSP 3: Kolbert Positions Scientific Knowledge
Within Democratic Processes
Where Corson addresses action and uncertainty at the local level,
Kolbert tackles them at the largest possible scale: the question of what
to do about global climate change. Several of her pieces address this
problem; the one I focus on here, ‘The Catastrophist’, illustrates how
science writing can promote the value of seeing scientific knowledge
as a resource that supports democratic decision-making. Kolbert
presents as a model of civic engagement a climate scientist, James
Hansen, who embodies a particular set of democratic values that can
be embraced by citizens generally and by scientists working to influ-
ence public policy.
132   Communicating Popular Science


   First, Hansen is depicted as both a scientist and an individual with
motivations any citizen can (and, by implication, should) share. He is
not a firebrand or a publicity hound, but someone who became involved
in politics only reluctantly; Kolbert quotes Hansen’s wife, who explains,
‘He started speaking out, not because he thinks he’s good at it, not
because he enjoys it, but because of necessity’ (p. 193). Hansen’s primary
motivations are a concern for the future and a desire not to leave today’s
problems for future generations; he ‘insists that his intent is not to be
provocative but conservative: his only aim is to preserve the world as
we know it’ (p. 189). Kolbert continues: ‘Hansen dotes on his grand-
children…and he claims they are the major reason for his activism’ and
quotes his explanation: ‘I decided that I didn’t want my grandchildren to
say, “Opa understood what was happening, but he didn’t make it clear,”
he explained’ (p. 199). In this respect, he models an engaged citizen act-
ing with unselfish motives to help address a serious environmental crisis.
   The quote Kolbert offers, with Hansen’s comment that his duty is to
‘make it clear’ rather than to force change, also models how scientists
can be involved in politics without being technocrats; he wants politics
to work. Kolbert details Hansen’s political actions, which include testify-
ing before Congress (p. 189), meeting with the vice president and ‘other
high-level Administration officials’ during the Bush administration
(p. 192), writing to ‘world leaders, including Prime Minister Gordon
Brown, of Britain, and Yasuo Fukuda, then the Prime Minister of Japan’
(p. 193), giving public lectures (p. 194), and even becoming involved in
protests and civil disobedience (p. 188).
   In showing these actions—intended to influence, but not replace the
democratic political process—Kolbert offers an example of how epideic-
tic ‘offers instruction on recognizing virtue and thereby on retaining
persuasion as an alternative to authority or force in the public domain’
(Hauser, ‘Aristotle’, p. 16). In this case, the virtue that Hansen embodies
is of engaging in civic discourse not for personal fame or professional
gain but for the public interest.


Deliberative Orientations in Popular Science Texts

The fourth stasis traditionally deals with questions of jurisdiction, and
in current use includes questions about what to do once facts and values
are established. It appears in deliberative rhetoric, which is ‘concerned
with moving people to future action’ (Bizzell and Herzberg, p. 3), and
draws on what we know (forensic) and what we value (epideictic) to
decide what we should do.
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 133


   Deliberative rhetoric ranges from the technocratic to the democratic.
Technocratic deliberative writing says that scientists and other experts
should be the sole, or at least superior, decision-makers when it comes
to issues of policy and science-in-society. This view was illustrated in
Chapter 6, for example, in Gleick’s claim that only nuclear physicists
are qualified to pass judgment on the use of nuclear power. In contrast,
democratic deliberative writing includes citizens as participants in
deciding what science should be funded, how it should be applied, and
what limitations are needed for public and environmental health and
safety. To promote CUSP, science writing can ‘provide the information
and the understanding that is necessary if people are to think critically
about decisions affecting their lives’ and can ‘enhance the public’s abil-
ity to evaluate science policy issues and the individual’s ability to make
rational personal choices’ (Nelkin, ‘Selling’, p. 2–3).

CUSP 1: Franklin Connects Applications to Values
Discussions of applications may serve a celebratory function (Look what
science has done for us!), a condemnatory function (Look what science
has done to us!), or a civic function (Look, what do we want science
to be about?). The third function falls into the deliberative realm and
often comes up in talk about how scientific findings should or should
not be used.
   Applications are one of the most important elements of popular
science writing. In her extensive study of science in the mass media,
LaFollette finds that Americans ‘assign greatest importance to research
that, if successful, will be useful (and hence used)’ (p. 178). However,
while the question of applications is important, it should not be the
sole focus, as it is when popular science texts ‘emphasize the potential
applications and outcomes of scientific results, rather than the process
by which they were developed’ (Gregory and Miller, p. 116). Such a
focus fails to acknowledge that the research underpinning the applica-
tions is itself rooted in certain theories and assumptions, and that even
when the facts of a case are clear, application is never a value-neutral
enterprise. Popular science texts that foster CUSP therefore ground
applications in their scientific and social contexts.
   The interaction of values and action is nicely illustrated in Franklin’s
‘The Most Important Fish in the Sea’ as he connects actions to values,
for example in blaming the fishing industry for harvesting far too
many menhaden and, by extension, questioning the value system that
favors efficiency over sustainability. This commentary begins on the
first page when Franklin compares bluefish feeding habits to human
134   Communicating Popular Science


fishing methods. The bluefish ‘tear at the menhaden like piranhas in
a killing frenzy, gorging themselves, some killing even when they are
too full to eat, some vomiting so they can kill and eat again’ (p. 80).
The image is unappealing at best, but two factors mitigate the appar-
ent waste. First, other sea creatures, such as weakfish and crabs, eat the
scraps that the bluefish drop. And, second, the ‘school of menhaden
survives and swims on, its losses dwarfed in plenitude’ (p. 80). Franklin
contrasts this with ‘a greater danger than bluefish’, the two factory
ships mentioned above that end up having ‘trapped the entire school’
(pp. 80–1).
   This implied critique of human greed is made explicit later in the arti-
cle through quotations from a former spotter pilot, Watters, who tells
how fishing practices have changed since the 1940s, when he started
flying. Early on, Watters says, company fishermen ‘only fished the big
schools’ and would ‘stop when the schools broke up into small pods’,
a practice he says had been abandoned by the 1980s, when ‘We caught
everything we saw. The companies wanted to catch everything but the
wiggle’ (p. 85). Franklin adds: ‘More than half a century after he first
took to the air as a spotter pilot, [Watters] fumes that “the industry
destroyed their own fishery, and they’re still at it”’ (p. 87).
   Had Franklin simply focused on the conflict of Watters against the
fishing industry, his article would still have informed readers about the
decline in menhaden populations and the role that overfishing has
played in that decline. By going beyond this dramatic element Franklin
not only meets that communication goal but also creates a richly nuanced
text that invites readers to consider many dimensions of the menhaden
problem, from the scientific evidence available to the social forces at play.

CUSP 2: Franklin Discusses Policy and Jurisdiction
Talking about policy follows from talking about applications and val-
ues, as it raises questions about the roles of expert decision-making, of
public input, and of government oversight. Although not all scientific
issues have policy implications, most that are discussed in public do,
and science writing serves a deliberative function when it assumes that
popular science texts should address those aspects.
   In PAST-oriented texts, even those ‘about controversial theories that
bear on social policy, the press projects an image of science as an eso-
teric activity, a separate culture, a profession apart from and above other
human endeavors’ (Nelkin, ‘Selling’, p. 32). In contrast, CUSP-oriented
texts acknowledge that ‘in collective decision making there are several
social domains impinging upon the decision making: religion, ethics,
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 135


politics, military issues, science, and others’ and addresses those that are
relevant in a given situation (Kolstø, p. 462).
  Franklin ties the values described earlier to policy questions. After
describing (and providing evidence of) the dangers of overfishing,
Franklin offers a few perspectives on the need for better regulation.
He writes:

   Since market forces are unlikely to curtail the menhaden fishery,
   governments may have to take action. Price thinks the fishing sea-
   son for menhaden should be closed each December 1, ‘because after
   that is when the age zeros migrate down the coast.’ No matter what
   is done, most researchers agree the menhaden must be viewed not as
   a specific problem about a single species of disappearing fish but as
   a much larger ecological threat (p. 88).

Franklin also shows how questions of jurisdiction become important to
socio-scientific issues:

   The large oceanic schools of menhaden are often too scarce to chase
   profitably, so the fishing industry has moved into estuaries and bays,
   particularly the Chesapeake. Maryland has banned purse seining
   in its portion of the Chesapeake. Virginia has not. Omega Protein,
   headquartered in Houston and the largest U.S. menhaden fishing
   firm, has almost unlimited access to state waters, including the
   mouth and southern half of the Chesapeake (p. 86).

It is easy to see interstate and international aspects of environmental
issues—a reason that ecological subjects are especially useful for illustrat-
ing CUSP. However, as scientific research and its consequences become
more global in scope, the strategies represented here apply also to other
areas of science, even those traditionally contained within national
boundaries. The difference between a PAST and a CUSP approach to
science communication is the difference between ‘empowering people
to know and empowering them to act’ (Eden, p. 185, emphasis original).
That action includes being able to connect values to action, including
via political participation.


Rhetorical Orientations in Nijhuis’ ‘Taking Wilderness
in Hand’

Michelle Nijhuis’ article, ‘Taking Wilderness in Hand’, shows how
forensic, epideictic, and deliberative aspects of an issue interconnect.
136   Communicating Popular Science


The issue, a new and controversial conservation strategy called ‘assisted
migration’, is described using the example of Torreya taxifolia, an endan-
gered tree growing in the American southeast. The article explains that
although species have always shifted from place to place as a result of
habitat change, fragmentation of habitats has left many species unable
to do so. Advocates of assisted migration want to, and sometimes do,
help species move to new, and potentially more suitable, locations by
transporting them there directly. Although Nijhuis provides background
information on the species and on what researchers do and don’t know
about stressors causing its decline, the article centers on the deliberative
issue of whether humans should try to manually establish T. taxifolia in
a new habitat—to ‘assist’ its migration—and, in exploring the question,
it shows how the debate also involves epideictic and forensic questions.
   Forensic questions include the obvious—Why is T. taxifolia dying?
Will moving it actually help?—but also the less obvious questions, such
as how we define ‘assisted migration’. As Nijhuis shows, answers to that
question depend, in turn, on epideictic, third-stasis questions about
what we consider natural. In fact, the debate about assisted migration
can be framed in terms of a difference in perspectives on this question.
Those skeptical about assisted migration, Nijhuis explains, tend to be
traditional ecologists whose point of view she describes as the ‘long-
standing conservation credo’ that provided there is

   enough space, money, and knowledge, we can protect natural places
   and, in many cases, restore them by stitching them back together.
   But while we’re welcome to restore, redesign is frowned upon; that
   sort of tinkering crosses an invisible line between humans and
   capital-N Nature, and risks making things much worse. We’ve good
   reason to distrust ourselves, after all. Until the 1950s, we thought
   planting kudzu was a good idea (p. 185).

Ecologists taking a cautious stance toward assisted migration include
Mark Schwartz, a professor at the University of California, Davis, who
has ‘studied the Florida torreya at the Apalachicola Bluffs preserve since
the late 1980s, and remains one of the scant handful of scientists with
in-depth knowledge of the species’ (p. 186) and David Printiss, director
of a Nature Conservancy preserve near Torreya State Park in Florida.
Both Schwartz and Printiss are concerned about the fate of T. taxifolia,
but both worry about the potential for assisted migration to go bad. It is
true that T. taxifolia ‘is unlikely to become the next kudzu, but the next
species on the poleward wagon might very well prove a nasty invasive’
                        Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 137


(p. 187). Another concern Printiss raises is that while planting trees
further north may help the species in one way, it may actually backfire
if one result is to ‘take attention and funding away from the work in
the preserve, and make an already bad situation even worse’ (p. 185), a
concern that shows the interplay of political and scientific factors.
   However, the pro-migration side offers another set of perspectives.
Proponents include Connie Barlow, a naturalist whose ‘background
in ecology and evolutionary biology have immersed her in the long
time-scales of evolution’ (p. 186), and the paleoecologist Paul Martin,
someone else whose disciplinary specialty emphasizes long spans of
time. Other advocates of assisted migration are horticulturalists, such
as Lee Barnes, whose doctoral dissertation was on endangered Florida
species, including the Florida torreya, and Jack Johnston, who sums up
a view shared by many others in the field: ‘“People have been moving
plants around for a long time,” he says. “This idea that we should be ter-
ritorial about our plants, well, that’s just kind of a provincial attitude”’
(p. 188). Nijhuis also reports that people in the subfield of restoration
ecology also tend to approve of assisted migration, seeing it as ‘simply
another way to impersonate the process of dispersal’ (p. 191). These dif-
ferent perspectives on natural versus assisted migration do more than
illustrate the way that values are embedded in definitions; they also
show the fundamental dilemma at the heart of many environmental
issues: if action is risky, but nonaction is also risky, what criteria do we
use for judging what to do or not do?
   In framing the question in these terms, Nijhuis’ article demonstrates
how a popular science text can acknowledge the complexity of socio-
scientific issues. She also shows how texts can resist a reductive, deficit-
style tendency to focus on drama and conflict. Such an approach would
be easy to take, as there is plenty of emotion on both sides of the debate:
ecologist Camille Parmesan reports that when she first mentioned the
idea of assisted migration at a conservation conference ‘people were
horrified—just horrified’ (p. 183), and Nijhuis characterizes the reac-
tion of conservationists in general as one of ‘almost visceral hostility’
(p. 187). However, she chooses to focus not on the horror and hostility,
but on ways that people from different camps are working on creating
constructive conversations with each other. Two sections of the article
illustrate both the ways these conversations are happening and how
popular science texts can spotlight inclusive, civil discussions about
contentious issues.
   In the first example, Nijhuis describes how three researchers with
three different opinions about assisted migration coauthored and
138   Communicating Popular Science


published a paper that, in her words, ‘modestly proposes a “frame-
work for debate” on assisted migration’ (p. 189). The second example
expands the scope of participation even further, wedding the epideictic
and deliberative in the process: ‘Last fall’, Nijhuis writes, in order ‘to
initiate a broader discussion, the three scientists organized a meeting in
Davis, California, with other researchers, land managers, environmental
groups, and even an environmental ethicist’ (pp. 189–190). In the sec-
ond example, Nijhuis not only shows civil debate as a productive goal,
she also manages to ‘avoid arbitrary, sharp delineations of expertise,
and instead include a process to examine knowledge claims critically’
(Burgman et al., p. 83) via an inclusive conversation. Here, and through-
out the article, Nijhuis demonstrates that while scientific knowledge
and expertise is crucial for such complex environmental decisions,
at the same time ‘scientists need to recognize that the most valuable
contributions are not proof and truth, but good judgment achieved in
an intellectually honest arena of discourse’ (Allen et al., p. 484). ‘Taking
Wilderness in Hand’ offers one example of what an intellectually honest
arena of discourse looks like.


Conclusion: Rhetorical Orientations and a New
Social Contract

Rhetorician Michael Zerbe, writing of the tendency of the media and
of sciences not to communicate about the ‘complexities’ that make up
any scientific endeavor, describes these complexities as ‘multifarious
and many in nature, ranging from design to methodology to the inter-
pretation and application of results’ (p. 14). When popular science texts
emphasize only the application of results, or the results themselves,
they present an artificially narrow picture of how scientists have come
to certain findings and offer little insight into how certain or uncertain
those findings are. In contrast, texts that delve more into the forensic
elements of science, such as design methodology and interpretation,
and also connect the forensic elements with epideictic and deliberative
elements, invite readers to engage with science-related issues not as
spectators but as members of civil society.
  By including a mixture of forensic, epideictic, and deliberative ele-
ments, popular science texts can offer accurate and engaging pictures
of how science works and of how it fits into democratic decisions about
science-related issues. Thus, the strategies detailed in this chapter pro-
duce texts that connect knowledge to action in ways that make sense in
civil society. Epideictic, often seen as mere bombast or shallow appeals
                         Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement 139


to emotion, is actually a key component. Knitting the other two modes
together, it is used ‘to demonstrate...a continuity between past, present,
and future events’ (Agnew, p. 149).
   In addition to offering models of civic discourse, such texts are also
beneficial for science as they do not set science up to fail. Popular sci-
ence texts promote CUSP when they are realistic about how science
works, and this means including the contingencies that come with
applying uncertain scientific knowledge to real-world problems. In doing
so, they avoid the binary of right/wrong that sets up an ‘expectation
that scientists reach certainty, a determinate and exclusive answer to
their research questions’ (Charney, ‘Lone’, p. 218) that can lead to disil-
lusionment when scientists fail to produce a clear answer. For example,
communications scholar Matthew Nisbet writes about how uncertainty
has been used by climate change deniers; for example, one strategist,
Frank Luntz, has ‘recommended emphasizing repeatedly that the “sci-
entific debate remains open”’ and that ‘further research is needed before
government action is taken’ (p. 22). Nisbet refers to the ensuing lack of
action as ‘paralysis by analysis’ (p. 56), while Paroske calls this the ‘epis-
temological filibuster’ (quoted in Condit et al., p. 395). What Nisbet
warns against, however, is not an accurate depiction of scientific uncer-
tainty, but a manipulation of public perceptions of that uncertainty.
That manipulation is enabled, in part, by overly confident depictions of
scientific knowledge; in an all-or-nothing positivist model, any crack in
the edifice brings down the whole structure. In a more nuanced model,
gaps in knowledge are not fatal, as readers look for probabilities, not
absolute proof, and weigh the evidence presented.
   Finally, CUSP model popular science texts can help experts better
understand their own work in relation to its broader social context,
including the tacit values that may underpin that work. Whatever the
outcome—a more engaged citizenry or more reflective professional
practice—what is at stake is, to quote Jeanne Fahnestock, ‘the machin-
ery and quality of social decision making in an expert-dominated age’
(‘Accommodating’, p. 346).
9
Technocracy and Democracy:
Talking about Risk




Chapter 8 talked about the importance of shifting popular science
writing from an epideictic orientation that invites readers to admire
science to a deliberative orientation that invites thoughtful engage-
ment. Nowhere is such deliberation more crucial—and nowhere are the
booster and critical approaches more at odds—than in discussions of
risk. The opposition between these approaches is reflected in the way
that risk discourse tends to divide between technocratic and democratic
views of how risk should be managed.
   This chapter addresses how popular science texts can frame science-
related risks in terms that encourage CUSP (Critical Understanding of
Science in Public) even—or especially—when the scientific aspects are
still uncertain.1 As in earlier chapters, I start by describing where differ-
ent positions on risk communication fall on the PAST (Public Apprecia-
tion of Science and Technology)–CUSP continuum and talk about
practitioner points of view before turning to analysis of popular science
texts and then concluding.


Risk and the PAST–CUSP Continuum

Risk situations are complicated, but the elements of a risk situation can
be divided into two general sets: probability factors and contextual fac-
tors. Underlying both sets of factors is the question of what philosophy
of risk is being used, an issue I return to after describing issues relating
to probability and context.

Probability Factors
Probability factors used in expert risk assessments (ERAs) include like-
lihood, severity, and scope. Likelihood is the chance that something
                                    140
                           Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   141


will happen; it is what we mean when we ask ‘What are the odds?’ The
odds of my car having a wheel fall off are very low, while the odds of
it developing a leak in the air-conditioning (AC) system are moderately
high (at least for my current car). Severity is generally discussed in terms
of how bad the situation is likely to be, but also refers to how bad it
could be; for example, if a wheel does fall off my car, I will probably
(given my normal driving habits) be able to pull the car onto the side
of the road, but if it happened in the wrong place and time, it could
cause a fatal accident. A problem in the AC system will almost certainly
mean some hot summer drives, but nothing worse. And scope is about
how extensive the effects are likely to be or could be, including things
like the number of people affected and the duration of the effect, from
the temporary to the irreversible. If a wheel falls off my car and causes
me to crash, this could hurt or kill other people, whereas if my AC stops
working, the only people suffering will be those who ride in my car in
the summer.
   Each of these factors is subject to uncertainties that limit the accuracy
of predictions. Limiting factors include how much is known about the
possible risk, the complexity and interactivity of the system, and the
uniqueness of the situation. These are illustrated by comparing two
modern technologies: a car engine and a nuclear power plant.
   ‘Amount of knowledge’ refers to how much is known about the par-
ticular kind of risk. In the case of a car’s engine, the system is very well
understood; there is a great deal of information about how car engines
work and data about how they break. For nuclear plants, however, there
are relatively little data about what happens when they malfunction.
While the fact that we don’t have more information is good in many
ways—after all, more data would have come from more accidents—at
the same time, it does limit how much we know.
   How much we know is often tied to complexity and interactivity.
Complexity refers to how complicated a system is—the more compli-
cated, the more difficult accurate prediction becomes—while interac-
tivity refers to how tightly or loosely coupled the system is. The more
interactive, or tightly coupled a system is, the more effect a failure in
one part has on other parts and on the whole; the more loosely coupled,
the less likely a failure in one part will lead to an overall failure. With
a car, the complexity level is not so high that the engine’s function
cannot be modeled accurately, and cars are loosely coupled systems,
meaning that a failure in the braking mechanism (for example) does not
cause the steering to fail as well, and vice versa. Nuclear power plants
are different: the reactors are ‘such inherently complex, tightly coupled
142   Communicating Popular Science


systems that, in rare, emergency situations, cascading interactions will
unfold very rapidly in such a way that human operators will be unable
to predict and master them’ (Gusterson, ‘The Lessons’).
  Finally, uniqueness refers to how applicable general models are in a
given situation. Automobile engines and the conditions under which
they are used tend to be uniform enough that what we learn from one
car’s problems can be generalized to others. In a column on Fukushima
in the Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists, Hugh Gusterson, an anthropolo-
gist who studies how people learn (or don’t learn) from mistakes,
describes four ‘grave nuclear reactor accidents’, including Fukushima,
and notes that

   Each accident was unique, and each was supposed to be impossible.
   Nuclear engineers have learned from each accident how to improve
   reactor design so as to diminish the likelihood of that particular
   accident repeating itself but, as Donald Rumsfeld famously reminded
   us, there are always ‘unknown unknowns,’ and so each accident has
   been succeeded by another, unwinding in a way that was not fore-
   seen (‘The Lessons’).

The conclusion I reach about automobiles is that a car’s engine is an
advanced technology, especially in newer cars with computerized sys-
tems, but if I maintain it well, it is reasonable to take its mechanical
safety for granted. In addition, even if my car engine dies, I will be
able to steer the car to the side of the road and bring it to a stop (albeit
with some effort if I have power steering and brakes). I also have con-
fidence that my mechanic will be able to diagnose and fix whatever
went wrong. With nuclear power, Gusterson suggests that ‘the lesson of
Fukushima is not that we now know what we need to know to design
the perfectly safe reactor, but that the perfectly safe reactor is always just
around the corner. It is technoscientific hubris to think otherwise’ (‘The
Lessons’). Given the difficulty of predicting risk in situations with little
data, high complexity, a tightly coupled system, and lessons that do not
necessarily transfer well from one situation to the next, this assessment
seems reasonable to me.

Contextual Factors
The second set of factors to consider in a risk situation are contextual;
I address three: fairness, voluntariness, and locus of control.2 Fairness
has to do with whether the benefits and risks are distributed equitably.
The most equitable distribution happens when only the people bearing
                           Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   143


the risk are those who stand to benefit. When friends and I go hiking in
remote areas, for example, we risk being injured when far from medical
help, but choose to accept the risk because of the pleasure we get from
being in the wilderness. An inequitable distribution of risk happens
when the people benefiting do not bear the cost, or experience the
consequences, if anything goes wrong. Innumerable incidents come
to mind, most having to do with corporate profit and public risk: BP’s
Deepwater Horizon deadly explosion and subsequent oil spill is one of
the more dramatic examples from recent years.
   Voluntariness and locus of control are related. Voluntariness refers
to whether a risk is chosen or imposed.3 Voluntariness needs to be
understood in terms of what people can reasonably expect. Therefore,
an underwater welder who works on an oil rig engages voluntarily in
a risky occupation, perhaps because it is also lucrative. However, if the
rig’s owners fail to maintain it properly and therefore increase the level
of danger, that additional risk is imposed. Locus of control has to do with
who makes risk-related decisions, from decisions affecting likelihood to
how problems will be handled if something goes wrong. In the above
scenario, the locus of control for diving-related safety is with the welder,
while the locus of control for rig safety in general is with the rig’s main-
tenance crew and, ultimately, with the people who set maintenance and
safety priorities.

Philosophies of Risk
The last consideration, and one of the most important for many envi-
ronmental issues, is the philosophy of risk that people apply in a given
the situation. Risk philosophies are couched in terms of what kind of
mistake people would rather make, a Type 1 error or a Type 2 error.
   In a Type 1 error, we assume there is a problem and later learn there
isn’t one, or we assume the problem is bigger than it later turns out to
be; in simple terms, we over-react. In a Type 2 error, we under-react; we
assume everything is fine and later learn that it is not, or we assume
things are better than they later turn out to be. Statistical methods are
designed to avoid Type 1 errors, but at the cost of being more likely
to make Type 2 errors. In scientific terms, it is better to reject false-
positives, even at the cost of accepting false-negatives, than the other
way around. The bias is to under-react to evidence in order to maintain
high standards for accepting it. Applied to risk situations, this means
it is better to accept some extra risk of harm in order to avoid acting
unnecessarily. The opposite position, to prefer over-reaction rather
than under-reaction, is called the ‘Precautionary Principle’. According
144   Communicating Popular Science


to the precautionary principle, it is better to accept some false-positives
(to act as if evidence of a problem is true) in order to maintain high
safety standards. In everyday terms, the precautionary principle is often
expressed as being ‘better safe than sorry’.
  The difference between Type 1 and Type 2 errors is nicely illustrated
using the previous example of my car. The car was making a strange
noise, but I decided it was unlikely to be anything severe and that I did
not need to address it on a day when I had other things planned. This
turned out to be a drastic under-reaction, Type 2 error as one of the
wheels came off at 65 miles per hour. I now tend to make Type 1 errors
with cars, taking them to the mechanic at the first sign of trouble.
  The car example demonstrates that which kind of error is better
depends on what is at stake. I now prefer to make Type 1 errors with
my car; people, in general, prefer to make Type 1 errors with fire alarms,
that is to act as if the alarm is real, even if we later learn it was a false
alarm. In contrast, criminal law in the USA is biased in favor of Type 2
errors, meaning it is set up so that we may find we have let guilty peo-
ple go free, but we are less likely to find we have convicted innocent
people. Even with science-related issues there is no inherently correct
bias; in scientific publications, it is important to avoid Type 1 errors
in order ‘to maximize truth’ (Myhr and Traavik, p. 234), but in public
health situations it makes more sense to avoid Type 2 errors in order to
minimize harm.

The PAST–CUSP Continuum
Any or all of the probability and contextual factors may be involved in
a given risk situation, and how a text treats them signals where they fall
on the PAST–CUSP spectrum.
   PAST model texts are characterized by a technocratic focus on the
probability factors and by a tendency to take ERAs as given, without
delving into the assumptions behind the calculations. In keeping with
perspective on risk, they view nonspecialists through a deficit lens,
seeing any differences from a purely ERA-based risk assessment as a
problem.
   Social psychologists George Gaskell, Sally Stares, and Nicole Kronberger
offer a perfect example of the PAST model view of risk. They focus on
probabilities, explaining that ‘expert risk assessment’ is a process of
developing ‘probabilistic models that estimate the likelihood of nega-
tive outcomes and the potential impacts of these outcomes’ (pp. 65–6).
In and of itself that is simply a description of what an ERA is. However,
they also say that only scientific experts can truly understand and
                            Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   145


evaluate the risks associated with a given line of research, a given appli-
cation, a given technology, and so on, when they write that ERAs are
trustworthy because they are ‘underpinned by the scientific method’
and offer ‘an almost universal currency, transcending place and time’
(pp. 65–6). As Chapters 2 and 4 discussed, such blanket confidence in
the ‘scientific method’, while justified for cold science, obscures the
contingent and shifting nature of hot science.
   Seeing ERAs in such idealized terms not only diverts attention from
the uncertainty and interpretation underlying statements of probabil-
ity, it also positions contextual factors as external, illegitimate, or even
superficial. In this vein, even though Gaskell et al. do acknowledge
that sometimes ‘the potential risks are beyond the frontiers of current
scientific knowledge’, they add that ‘even when the scientists know
that they don’t know, they still know better than anyone else’ (p. 67).
They also describe ERAs as the only legitimate way to understand risks:
of the various ways that people approach risk, they say that only those
approaches that are based on science ‘can be expected to base judg-
ments primarily on expected consequences, on utilitarian grounds’
(p. 66). Other factors are labeled as ‘different, external issues’ (p. 66) and
are dismissed as irrational. They characterize nonspecialist responses to
risk as being ‘intuitive’ and say that ‘intuitive logics lead to a concept
of risk that goes beyond sound science…For the intuitive public, the
essence of perceptions of risk are not cold, calculating, cognitive deci-
sions but rather fears, hopes, pleasure and anger’ (p. 67). Because this
defines a ‘knowledgeable public’ as one that ‘will agree with experts,
who do not succumb to biases as the public does’ (Bauer et al., ‘What
Can’, p. 83), it creates a circular reasoning in which the public is only
deemed well informed enough to speak against ERAs if it agrees with
the ERAs.
   Although they are more blunt than some writers, Gaskell et al. are
not alone in these perceptions of risk, expertise, and contextual factors.
This point of view is one that says ‘that “science determines risk” while
“the population perceives risk”’ (Abraham and Sheppard, p. 150), and
that ‘protests are purely a problem of information: if only the public
knew what the experts know, they would see the issues the same way’
(Abraham and Shepard, p. 150). Like other expressions of a deficit
model, this view of risk has two problems: it is undemocratic and, as
communication scholars have found, it doesn’t work.4
   In contrast to the deficit model belief that people are not interested
in or able to understand scientific issues, a democratic view respects
that Americans ‘believe they have a right to be involved—experts alone
146   Communicating Popular Science


should not choose what benefits should be sought and what risks the
research might acceptably pose’ (LaFollette, p. 177).
  As Lee Wilkins explains in her ‘Primer on Risk’,

   People can and do weigh risks against benefits. Communities can
   decide to assume certain risks (for example, agreeing to become the
   site of a prison because jobs will be created). However, such decisions
   seldom mirror numeric risk assessments; they are far more likely to
   reflect many of the qualities of lay rationality. These decisions are
   much more the result of political and social negotiation than an
   automatic acceptance of a numeric risk assessment would suggest
   (p. 168).

And people do learn about risk when they feel doing so is important. In
fact, ‘empirical studies have explored the knowledgeability and resource-
fulness of particular publics when encountering science-related issues
within the contexts of everyday life’ (Irwin, ‘Risk’, p. 204). For exam-
ple, Abraham and Sheppard describe AIDS activists and their ‘dynamic
educative process of citizenship in which the lay person would develop
expertise through political involvement’ (p. 150). In another example,
‘research has shown that in communities with water-quality problems,
even people with limited education can quickly come to understand
highly complex technical information’ (Brossard and Lewenstein, p. 13).
Because they recognize that democratic risk communication works
within ‘the cultural framework of the audience, not within the concerns
of industrial technologists’ (Russell, N. J., p. 75), CUSP model texts offer
greater depth and breadth than PAST model texts.
   Greater depth means looking into the probability factors and address-
ing possible causes of uncertainty in discussions of likelihood, severity,
and scope. As noted earlier, ERAs regarding these factors, while useful,
are not inherently sound simply because they are offered by specialists
in a given domain. In fact, one of the potential complications comes
from domain-based assumptions and interpretive biases. Others arise
owing to complexity and/or interactivity in a system, to uncertainty in
the data being used, and in the inevitable differences between a general
model and a particular case.
   CUSP-oriented examinations of risk understand that what lies behind
a probabilistic risk assessment is just as important as the figures them-
selves. Because of complicating factors, an ERA requires making some
assumptions, but this also means that changing the assumptions
may change the risk assessment (Wilkins, p. 165). Furthermore, ‘any
                            Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   147


assessment will still not take into account all the possibilities for failure
or success. The result: something that looks firmly grounded in empiri-
cal evidence is in reality a single point on a continuum of probabilities’
(Wilkins, p. 165). To understand even the probabilistic aspects of a risk,
it is crucial to understand the assumptions that were made in coming
up with the figures, and to ask whether and how a particular situation
differs from those on which models are being based.
   CUSP model texts also examine facets of a risk situation that a tech-
nocratic approach considers external. While few texts have room to
cover all potential angles, a CUSP model approach includes those that
are important for the deliberative process. In doing so, they use what
Hornig calls an ‘expanded vocabulary of risk’ (quoted in Gregory and
Miller, p. 189). For example, where a PAST model text focuses on the
question of how safe something is, a CUSP model text also considers
who has control, what interests they serve, whether they can be trusted
to have the public interest as their primary loyalty, and so on. Related
to these questions about the locus of control, CUSP model texts also
acknowledge the philosophies of risk that different stakeholders apply.
   Overall, CUSP model texts take a critical view of things that PAST
model texts tend to take for granted; unlike their public relations-
oriented counterparts, CUSP model texts ask about the limitations of
numeric risk assessments, emphasizing interpretation, and considering
questions of power, control, and other social factors that will affect who
experiences the risk and how.


How Practitioners Talk About Risk

Few of the practitioner texts I examine specifically address issues of
risk beyond occasional glancing references,5 so their positions must
be inferred from how they talk about science more generally. Most of
the relevant inferences are already present in earlier chapters; in this
chapter, I review these positions briefly and show how each relates to
writing about risk-related situations, and also address the few texts that
do openly talk about risk.

PAST Model Views of Risk Communication
PAST model texts are, as earlier chapters have discussed, based on a
view of science that emphasizes its strengths and a view of readers that
emphasizes their inability to learn or unwillingness to be guided by sci-
ence. Given the belief that science is always the solution to problems
(even when prior scientific research has helped cause the problems), it
148   Communicating Popular Science


is unsurprising that some practitioner texts view risk communication in
terms of how get an irresponsible or indifferent public to follow the lead
of those who know better: scientists.
   PAST model practitioner texts imply a technocratic view of risk when
they marginalize nonscientist perspectives, as when Folger praises writ-
ers ‘who remind us that there is nothing ordinary about our world and
that there is perhaps no better means for uncovering the unexpected
than science. Our daily lives contain dangers and delights that most of
us overlook’ (‘Foreword’, 2007, p. xi). In saying that science, not every-
day experience, is the best means for recognizing problems, statements
like this and others covered in Chapters 4 and 5 imply that scientists are
the rightful guardians against risk.6
   A more complex example of PAST model approaches to risk comes
from Revkin’s chapter of the Field Guide.7 Revkin’s reference to popular
science texts in which ‘the only questions are about science’ (p. 226) was
mentioned in Chapter 5 as an example of demarcation. The full passage
refers to knowing sources’ motivations, and cites examples of bias in
both industry and environmental groups. Revkin quotes an industry
PhD who is a climate change skeptic, then says ‘Such a voice can have
a legitimate place in a story focused on policy questions, but is perhaps
best avoided in a story where the only questions are about science. The
same would go for a biologist working for the World Wildlife Fund’ (p.
226). This statement is problematic for a few reasons. One, as noted, is
the idea that there is such a thing as a story that is only about science,
that science—especially on environmental topics—can be reduced to a
simple and easily-demarcated arena.
   Another concern is that Revkin appears to reduce risk questions to
ERAs. In the same section of his chapter, Revkin contrasts ‘the potent
heat generated by emotional content and the sometimes less compel-
ling light of solid science and statistics’ (p. 226), and the ‘emotional
power of the grief emanating from a mother who lost a child to leuke-
mia’ with ‘the cold statistical reality of epidemiology, which might in
that instance never be able to determine if contamination caused the
cancer’ (p. 227). These representations of ‘solid science and statistics’
and ‘the cold statistical reality of epidemiology’ suggest a view of risk as
something that should be understood mainly, if not only, in the quan-
titative terms of ERAs; they also say that it is more important to avoid
Type 1 errors than to err on the side of public health.
   The third concern has to do with contextual and philosophical differ-
ences between the industry and nongovernmental organization (NGO)
sources. In equating the two, Revkin elides differences in motivation
                           Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   149


that, if not relevant scientifically, are important to consider in risk
situations. Industry motives, as Ziman argues in his critique of post-
academic science, are necessarily centered on private profit. NGOs,
while not necessarily embodiments of nobility, at least have the public
interest as their raison d’être. The differences in motivations guide not
only their communication agendas, with NGOs expected by members,
as well as by law, to disclose more information than corporations, but
also their research emphases. This is not a condemnation of scientists
working in any context, but an acknowledgement that scientists’
research agendas are shaped by the needs and interests of the entity
for which they work; overlooking this aspect marks the text as at least
partly Traditional–Idealist in its view of science.
   The most overtly PAST-oriented statement comes from Pinker’s
‘Introduction’ to The Best American Science and Nature Writing 2004. In
describing his selection criteria for the anthology, Pinker writes that he
rejected one piece because it ‘denounced a research program that suc-
ceeded in modifying corn to synthesize pharmaceuticals cheaply despite
its promise of vast enhancements to human health and a demonstrably
trivial risk to the environment’ (p. xix). In and of itself this statement
simply identifies Pinker as a proponent of genetically-modified plants.
However, he continues:

  Halos are awarded only to whistleblowers in ecology or climate sci-
  ence who warn us about the wages of our technological lifestyle. In
  Europe, left-leaning greens call for a Precautionary Principle in which
  applications of science should be banned or restricted if there is some
  chance they will have harmful effects, even in the absence of scien-
  tific evidence that they do. If the policy, aptly satirized as ‘Never do
  anything for the first time,’ had been applied in the past, it would
  have ruled out every new technology from fire to fertilizer to malaria
  control to oral contraception (p. xix).

Aside from the fact that climate scientists would probably be surprised
to learn they have been awarded halos, this passage is problematic for
its caricature of the precautionary principle and its suggestion that, as
the cliché goes, absence of evidence is evidence of absence. That is, in
advocating aversion to Type 1 errors, Pinker’s statement exemplifies the
extreme PAST model position of ignoring (or even belittling) concern
about Type 2 errors. This statement, while extreme, represents a posi-
tion held by many other scientists and science writers, a position based
in the belief ‘that decisions about what risks are socially acceptable are
150   Communicating Popular Science


scientific rather than political judgments’ (Nelkin, Selling, p. 59) and
that they therefore require technocratic solutions.

CUSP Model Views of Risk Communication
In contrast with the technocratic view expressed by Pinker (and sug-
gested, to greater or less degrees, by other practitioners), CUSP model
texts discuss risk-related issues in terms of how to empower readers. This
is consistent with the CUSP model characteristics examined in previous
chapters; understanding the contingent nature of scientific knowl-
edge leads to a questioning stance toward ERAs, for example, while
recognizing the socially embedded nature of scientific issues leads to a
contextual view of science-related risks. Two authors in the Field Guide
exemplify a CUSP model approach to risk.
   Cope, co-author of News & Numbers: A Guide to Reporting Statistical
Claims and Controversies in Health and Other Fields, begins his chapter
on ‘Understanding and Using Statistics’ with this scenario: ‘A doctor
reports a “promising” new treatment. Is the claim believable, or is it
based on biased or other questionable data?’ (p. 18). From there, Cope
goes on to talk about various statistical questions writers should ask.
Many of these, including three of his ‘principles of scientific analysis’,
bear directly on the need to question ERAs: there will always be uncer-
tainty (p. 19); the more data there are, the more sound an observation
based on that data will be (p. 20); and writers should be aware that
data can be interpreted differently (p. 21). Cope particularly stresses
the study size, asking ‘Is a claim being made on the basis of only lim-
ited observations?’, and ‘What are the numbers? Was the study large
enough (did it have enough patients or experiments or whatever) to
reach believable conclusions? Are the results statistically significant?’
(p. 19, emphasis original). Through these and other tips, Cope’s chapter
provides popular science writers with strategies for understanding and,
when necessary, critiquing statistical claims, including ERAs.
   Cope offers excellent tips for dealing with statistical aspects of a risk,
but the chapter of the Field Guide that most embodies a CUSP model
perspective is Cristine Russell’s ‘Risk Reporting’, which raises all of
the factors mentioned above, both probability-related and contextual.
Although she addresses numeric aspects of risk, she also says bluntly
that a ‘risk story is not just about numbers’ (p. 254) but includes issues
such as fairness and locus of control (p. 254). ‘In general,’ she writes, the
audience reading a risk story ‘tends to be more accepting of significant
voluntary risks or natural hazards and less accepting of novel, uncer-
tain, man-made risks imposed by others, such as radon or pollution’
                           Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   151


(p. 254). In describing how to write about risk she calls for an approach
that combines accurate reporting on scientific knowledge and the proc-
ess by which it is created—‘to write more about the self-correcting,
evolutionary process of scientific research by taking readers or listen-
ers inside the laboratory or clinical setting’—while also addressing the
question of ‘whether prudent public health policy requires action by
government, industry, or individuals before the scientific answers are
in’ (p. 252). Finally, Russell says to discuss contextual factors, such as
fairness, arguing that sometimes ‘potential beneficiaries and at-risk
groups are different. Workers and residents living around a chemical
plant may face higher exposure risks, while the consumers who buy the
company’s products receive the benefits’ (p. 255). Russell’s recommen-
dations complement Cope’s; together, the two exemplify a CUSP model
approach to writing about risk.


Risk in Popular Science Texts

The CUSP model approach to risk—critically examining probability
factors and including contextual factors—uses and builds on strategies
described in previous chapters. The connections can be understood in
terms of four ‘dimensions’ that John C. Besley, a science and health com-
munication researcher, says people use in assessing risk-related decisions:
interpersonal, informational, procedural, and distributive. The first two
dimensions correspond to strategies in Chapters 7 and 8: the interper-
sonal dimension has to do with the trustworthiness of the writer, while
the informational has to do with ‘the degree to which participants in
decision making are given access to the best available information’ and
‘whether information is provided to explain the relationship between
decisions and evidence’ (Besley, pp. 71–2). Because those chapters already
covered strategies for earning trust and for critically communicating
forensic information, I focus here on the procedural and distributive
dimensions. These dimensions, while also related to strategies in earlier
chapters, have added layers of complexity when elements of risk are
introduced and therefore warrant separate treatment here.

CUSP 1: Goodell Discusses Fairness
Jeff Goodell looks at the distributive dimension—i.e., at who benefits
and who bears the risk—in his article about nuclear power, ‘The Fire
Next Time’. Fairness has both epideictic and deliberative elements, as it
includes how values are expressed in action, or the unexamined values
underlying actions. Although risk is often posed as a purely scientific
152   Communicating Popular Science


issue, it is, in fact, a construct that tends to reflect the interests of those
describing it. Nowhere is this more apparent than with the risks posted
by nuclear power plants which, as was noted earlier, exemplify a com-
plex, interactive system about which there is much uncertainty. Goodell
raises these points, but the facet of his article that I want to emphasize is
its discussion of fairness. Where other writers reduce debates of nuclear
power to a binary of rational scientists versus an irrational public,
Goodell foregrounds the contextual issues at the heart of that debate
when he explains the regulatory structure that assigns profits from
nuclear power plants to the companies that own them, while placing
the risk—both financial and environmental—on the public.
   Goodell describes how companies benefit financially from two public
subsidies. First, federal funds help pay to build plants; private investors
‘won’t finance nuclear reactors without direct federal assistance’ partly
because plants are expensive to build and partly because ‘nukes are the
only energy investment that can be rendered worthless in a matter of
hours’ (p. 127). Second, nuclear plants are underwritten by the federal
government; companies do not pay the full price if something goes
wrong ‘because Congress passed the Price-Anderson Act in 1957, limit-
ing the liability of nuclear plant operators in case of disaster’ (p. 127).
In other words, the public helps pay to build plants and the public pays
when accidents happen.
   Adding potential injury to actual insult is the physical risk to the
public. Goodell cites examples of plants continuing to operate despite
the potential for harm to citizens living nearby. These include Vermont
Yankee, which was ‘uprated’ (allowed to continue running past its
safety date) despite the fact that the Nuclear Regulatory Commission’s
‘own advisory committee on reactor safety was vehemently opposed’
(p. 130), and Indian Point, which is 38 miles upriver and upwind from
New York City, and continues operation even after it has been ‘found to
be at higher risk because of new information about earthquake zones’
(p. 131). Another earthquake-vulnerable operation, the Diablo Canyon
plant, ‘sits near one of the most active seismic zones in the world’ (p. 131).
Although it ‘is supposedly engineered to withstand a 7.5 earthquake’,
Goodell says there’s ‘one problem: Two nearby faults are capable of pro-
ducing quakes of 7.7 or higher’ (p. 131). In each of these cases, energy
companies profit from keeping the plants running while millions of
people bear the risk of living in the danger zone.
   Although it is inappropriate to malign an industry by assuming its
claims are biased, it is equally inaccurate to assume those interests
don’t play a part when there is evidence that they do. This is especially
                            Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   153


important when the ‘potential beneficiaries and at-risk groups are dif-
ferent’ (Russell, C., p. 255) as they are in the situation Goodell describes,
and as they often are in situations involving other environmental risks.
In such positions, Wynne correctly notes, ‘so-called public risk concerns
are always also public concerns about their social relations of depend-
ency—and rationally so’ (p. 22, emphasis original).

CUSP 2: Scott Examines the Locus of Control
The procedural dimension of risk situations raises deliberative ques-
tions about who makes risk-related decisions and how those decisions
are made. Scott’s article, ‘Pesticides Indicted in Bee Deaths’, raises issues
regarding locus of control as it discusses the mass death of honey bees,
a phenomenon referred to as ‘colony collapse disorder’, and the actions
taken or not taken by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to
regulate chemicals contributing to those deaths.
   Scott builds her discussion of colony collapse disorder on a solid
forensic foundation, explaining what is known, what is suspected, and
what is unclear. Although nobody knows precisely what causes colony
collapse disorder, it is generally agreed that a group of chemical pesti-
cides called chloronicotinoids are part of the problem. Scott provides
the evidence from beekeepers, academic scientists, and even the chemi-
cal manufacturer. One beekeeper describes imidacloprid as having a
‘pathological effect…interference with basic functions’ on his bees, and
says ‘They get lost, they get disoriented. They fall to the ground. They
get paralyzed and their wings stick out’ (Scott, p. 163). Eric Mussen, an
entomologist at University of California, Davis, agrees that the chemi-
cals are a threat to bees. He cites studies showing that toxic levels of
imidacloprid can persist on trees for over a year, and says that even
when bees don’t die, he thinks ‘any of the pesticides the bees bring back
to the beehive is hurting the bees’ (quoted in Scott, p. 165). ‘It’s likely,
he says, that exposure to even low doses acts like a one-two punch: It
can weaken the bees until a parasite or pathogen moves in to finish
them off’ (Scott, p. 165). Even Bayer CropScience, the manufacturer of
the two main chloronicotinoids on the market (imidacloprid and clo-
thianidin), has reported finding imidacloprid ‘in the nectar and pollen
of flowering trees and shrubs at concentrations high enough to kill a
honeybee in minutes’ (Scott, p. 162). The consensus is clear: the pesti-
cides, while not the only cause of bees’ deaths, do play a significant role.
   Where questions arise is over exactly what the mechanism of harm is
and how much the chemicals are contributing to colony collapse disor-
der. Scott quotes Mussen who says, for example, that ‘ongoing research
154   Communicating Popular Science


into chronic exposure to insecticides will be crucial’ (p. 165) to discover
precisely what is going on. Bayer CropScience, meanwhile, says that
the chloronicotinoids’ toxic effects on bees are not evidence that the
chemicals are contributing to colony collapse disorder; Scott quotes the
company’s spokesperson who says that ‘We do a lot of research on our
products and we feel like we have a very good body of evidence to sug-
gest that pesticides, including insecticides, are not the cause of colony
collapse disorder’ (p. 163). In a case like this, the job of judging the
evidence and deciding what to do falls to the EPA, and the key question
is not whether the chemical causes harm, as it clearly does, but what
criteria does the EPA apply? Do they take the industry-friendly position
that the product is safe until, and unless, it has been proved to be caus-
ing colony collapse disorder? That is, do they avoid Type 1 errors? Or
do they follow the precautionary principle and say that in uncertain
situations the burden of proof falls on those claiming something is safe?
The answer—and this is point of Scott’s article—is unclear, but EPA crit-
ics say the agency is catering to the industry rather acting to protect the
environment, and evidence suggests there is some merit to this view.
   Scott’s article includes a quick overview of the EPA’s positions since it
approved imidacloprid in the mid-1990s. Since then, according to plant
biologist Neil Carman, ‘It’s been found to be more persistent in the
environment than was reported by Bayer’ (quoted in Scott, p. 164). In
2003, the agency concluded that a similar pesticide, clothianidin, was
dangerous to honeybees and ‘suggested that chronic exposure could
lead to effects on the larvae and reproductive effects on the queen’
(Scott, p. 165). Despite this, and despite the fact that they had enough
concern to ask Bayer for more studies, the agency ‘nevertheless author-
ized the chemical for market’ (p. 165). This apparent contradiction
raises the question of how the agency made its decisions; Scott quotes
an environmental lawyer who points out that ‘If the EPA had sufficient
concern about harm to bees that they would insist on other studies,
it seemed unwise to approve it anyway and ask for research after the
fact’ (p. 165). It would be unwise if they were following the precaution-
ary principle, especially as ‘The EPA’s job is to make a decision about
whether a chemical is safe or not’ (p. 165), and they appeared to have
decided that it was not.
   The same puzzle continues in the present, as the EPA describes imi-
dacloprid as ‘“very highly toxic” to honeybees and other beneficial
insects’, but the only action it has taken is to add a label to the product
asking farmers ‘not to spray it on a plant when bees were foraging in the
neighborhood’ (p. 164). That the EPA has not banned a product they
                            Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   155


acknowledge to be dangerous is part of the problem Scott presents; a
bigger problem, at least for a government agency in a democratic soci-
ety, is the fact that their reasons are unclear. Scott explains:

   In a statement, the EPA says that before banning a pesticide, it ‘must
   find that an “imminent hazard” exists. The federal courts have ruled
   that to make this finding, EPA must conclude, among other things,
   that there is a substantial likelihood that imminent, serious harm
   will be experienced from use of the pesticide.’ The EPA did not clarify
   what is meant by ‘imminent hazard’ and why the death of honey-
   bees does not qualify (p. 166).

Although the EPA has started new studies of chloronicotinoids, skeptics
worry about bias and lack of transparency in the process; key informa-
tion, Scott explains, is not always released, even when it is the result of
agency research. She quotes environmental attorney Aaron Colangelo
on the problem: ‘“The EPA,” he explains, “often keeps its test results con-
fidential for proprietary reasons at a company’s request”’ (Scott, p. 166).
This is the crux of the problem because deliberation requires informa-
tion. As Rachel Carson noted in 1962, also writing about the chemical
pesticide industry, ‘It is the public that is being asked to assume the risks
that the insect controllers calculate. The public must decide whether it
wishes to continue on the present road, and it can do so only when in
full possession of the facts’ (p. 13). As Scott’s article makes clear, at least
in the case of chloronicotinoids, nobody outside of the EPA has all the
facts. By raising this issue, the text exemplifies the CUSP model per-
spective that, faced with no way to determine clear causality, questions
about locus of control and the decision-making process are not only
pertinent but central to discussions of risk.


Risk in Trivedi’s ‘The Wipeout Gene’

Trivedi’s article about genetically modified mosquitoes, ‘The Wipeout
Gene’, includes a number of risk factors. It describes two research
teams, one based at a company called Oxitec in the UK and the other
at the University of California at Irvine, studying how to genetically
modify disease-carrying mosquitoes in order to cause their popula-
tions to collapse. In contrasting these projects, Trivedi asks the kind
of risk-related questions that are growing more and more common as
biotechnology takes off: Who decides what risks are acceptable? How
are risks and benefits being distributed? How should we proceed with
156   Communicating Popular Science


technologies that have the potential for great benefit, but for which the
risks are unclear? Trivedi doesn’t say. What he does instead is provide
background information about the potential benefits and risks of the
research, and explore how two different groups approach the field trial
stage of their research.
   As Trivedi describes them, the potential benefits of the research are
significant. The mosquitoes being genetically modified carry dengue
fever, a disease whose effects range from flu-like symptoms at the mild
end to the severe hemorrhagic form that kills more people globally
than ‘other viral hemorrhagic fevers—including Ebola and Marburg—
combined’ (Trivedi, p. 58). Each year, Trivedi reports, approximately
100 million people are infected with dengue, and global health officials
describe it as ‘one of the world’s most aggressive reemerging infections’
(p. 58). Anything that reduces the prevalence of the disease could be a
great boon to public health, especially in poor areas of the world where
dengue takes its greatest toll.
   At the same time, Trivedi brings up concerns about the nature of
the research, noting that ‘opponents of the plan warn of unintended
consequences—even if mosquitoes are the intended victims’ (p. 54)
and quoting a research scientist with Greenpeace who points out that
releasing genetically-modified mosquitoes ‘is extremely risky. There’s
no such thing as 100 percent sterility, so there are going to be some
fertile females that will be released, and we don’t know the implications
of that’ (p. 63). Other concerns have to with the ethics of wiping out
a species and with the way that previous plans failed as other species
moved into the vacant ecological niche. One solution, a ‘gene drive
system’ that ‘promotes the spread of dengue resistance genes through
a wild mosquito population’ (Trivedi, p. 64) is even more controversial
than current, more self-limiting modifications.
   Echoing this scientific uncertainty is a lack of legal clarity; Trivedi
says that

  No international laws or agencies exist to police trials of new trans-
  genic organisms. For the most part, scientists and biotech companies
  can do what they want—even performing uncontrolled releases of
  test organisms in developing countries, neither warning the residents
  that their backyards are about to become a de facto biocolonialist
  field laboratory nor gaining their consent (p. 54).

This lack of legal oversight means that ‘Developing countries have long
made a convenient location for First World field trials’ (Trivedi, p. 59),
                           Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   157


and one of the two research teams he describes, Oxitec, appears to be
using them in precisely that way.
   Oxitec was founded in 2002 by a former Oxford research scientist,
Luke Alphey, who had developed ‘a technique for generating fruit flies
harboring genes that selectively killed females’ and who founded the
company ‘to capitalize on the technology’ (Trivedi, p. 56). As part of
its research, it began field tests a few years ago; Trivedi writes, ‘In 2009
and 2010 his organization took advantage of the minimal regulations
in the Caribbean’s Grand Cayman island to release millions of geneti-
cally modified mosquitoes into the wild’ (pp. 54–5), and it has since
begun trials in Malaysia and Brazil. Critics say that Oxitec ‘is prey-
ing on countries with minimal bureaucracy and regulations’, and the
Malaysian trials raised objections from more then twenty nonprofits
(p. 63). Trivedi cites the concern voiced by Gurmit Singh, chair of the
Center for Environment, Technology and Development in Malaysia,
that ‘Even with a newly minted National Biosafety Board that monitors
modified organisms and 2009 Malaysian Biosafety Act regulations…
Malaysia lacks the experience to monitor the experiment’ (p. 63).
   Alphey defends his ‘gung-ho approach’, Trivedi says, by explaining
that his company ‘leaves outreach largely to the governments because
they understand the cultural sensitivities’ (p. 62); in Grand Cayman,
this outreach consisted of a five-minute news item and ‘a pamphlet that
described the mosquitoes as sterile, avoiding any mention of genetic
modification’; Trivedi adds that ‘There were no public meetings or
opportunities for residents to voice concerns’ (p. 62).
   Trivedi does not comment directly on Oxitec’s approach, but his article
contrasts it with that of another research team, led by Anthony James at
the University of California, Irvine. Like Oxitec, James’ team has estab-
lished a field site, but with a few differences. First, instead of releasing
mosquitoes directly into the wild, they have created a contained set of
habitats where they can study the modified insects in situ without letting
them loose. Even more significantly, they have done so in cooperation
with local people. Knowing the history of abuse in genetics research, and
wanting to avoid any ethical transgressions, James took several steps to
involve the community from the start of the project.
   The first step was actually his choice of location; unlike the Oxitec
team, James chose not only a place a ‘where dengue was a significant
public health issue and control methods were failing’ but also one ‘in
a country with a stringent, sophisticated regulatory structure capable
of assessing the risks and benefits of a genetically modified, dengue-
fighting mosquito’ (Trivedi, p. 60). He chose, in other words, to conduct
158   Communicating Popular Science


research in a place where the local people would know their safety was
protected by more than the researchers’ ethics. At the site itself, James’
team worked within the local governance structure at all stages, from
buying land ‘through the traditional communal land-ownership pro-
gram in the area’ (Trivedi, p. 61) to ensuring the ongoing involvement
of community members, for example by having the field site manager
attend weekly town hall meetings to answer questions.
  The contrast of Alphey’s and James’ approaches to field trials illus-
trates how popular science writing can foster a critical understanding
of scientific risks. Specifically, it shows why, in a world where science
does not stay in the laboratory, requiring certainty is impossible, but
does not mean that no safeguards can be expected. Instead, it means a
return to deliberation, to allowing civil society to decide what risks it
will and will not take.


Conclusion

Considered through the lens of Kunzig’s statement that money and
conflicts of interest and power are not the ‘real story’ about science
(p. 127), and Revkin’s belief that there are stories in which ‘the only
questions are about science’ (p. 226), the articles examined in this
chapter may seem to stray from science writing proper. The techno-
cratic view common in CUSP model texts creates an ‘epistemology
asymmetry’ in which ‘Science has “facts”, the public has “opinions”’
(Broks, ‘Understanding’, p. 129). However, science produces uncertain
knowledge, especially when the area of research is new or complicated.
Even when knowledge is certain, the public’s opinions are supposed to
be exactly what a democracy is based on; we make risk decisions based
on what we value as much as on what we know. Goodell’s, Scott’s, and
Trivedi’s articles acknowledge this and serve the public interest by bring-
ing some of the contextual factors related to environmental risks into
the open and by establishing a place in science-related conversations for
Hornig’s ‘expanded vocabulary of risk’ mentioned earlier.
   The strategies they use also move us closer to a new social contract.
Holding researchers accountable for the risks their work creates or
allows, and holding industries accountable for the risks their products
pose, will help us escape from the Ravetz paradox in which scientists
take credit for positive developments and evade responsibility when
things go wrong. Byerly and Pielke argue that ‘Under democratic
accountability, science is consciously guided by society’s goals rather
than scientific serendipity. Good science is necessary but not sufficient;
                           Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk   159


association with a societal goal is required’ (p. 1532). Couched in terms
of the social contract, socially robust research can only happen in a con-
text where public oversight is given not lip service, as in a technocratic
approach, but the power to balance scientistic optimism with socially
informed caution. Such a context, in turn, can only happen when pub-
lic conversations about science move away from a PAST model approach
in which science boosters set the terms of the debate and into the realm
of the science critics.
   Various scholars have pointed out that ‘the application of scientific
findings before they have been fully explored erases the boundary
between the laboratory and society’ (Knorr-Cetina, p. 5). In techno-
scientific risk situations like those described in this chapter, we see
the ultimate breakdown of demarcation between science and the civil
sphere, and the strongest possible argument for a new social contract
that will foster CUSP: given the nature of science and technology today,
we all live in the laboratory.
This page intentionally left blank
Part III
Final Words
This page intentionally left blank
10
Conclusion: Toward a New
Social Contract




In the final chapter of Understanding Popular Science, Peter Broks proposes
that we move the locus of discussion from science per se to science-
in-society. Communicating Popular Science can be seen, in many ways,
as an extended proof-of-concept for Broks’ proposal, an attempt to
articulate what a shift to his ‘third generation model’ (p. 143) of popular
science—i.e., CUSP (Critical Understanding of Science in Public)—looks
like in action. It has also sought to integrate a perspective often left out
of critiques of popular science writing, that of popular science writers
themselves.
   In the process, I hope that it has accomplished three things. First,
I hope it has demonstrated the need for CUSP and the ways that popu-
lar science writing can meet that need. Second, I hope that it has placed
rhetoric of science in dialogue with other Science and Technology
Studies (STS) disciplines in productive and mutually supportive ways.
Third, and most ambitiously, I hope it has helped in some small way
to shift the sometimes antagonistic debates about science studies—the
so-called science wars—into a more productive alignment. I address
each of these in turn.


The Need for CUSP and the Role of Popular
Science Writing

To demonstrate the need for CUSP, I would like to turn to an issue I have
not discussed previously: pseudoscience. While I think it is clear why
