CUSP matters from a democratic point of view, this final example might
help illustrate why even boosters will find that CUSP is, ultimately, a
more productive approach to science popularization than the PAST
(Public Appreciation of Science and Technology) model.

                                    163
164   Communicating Popular Science


   Pseudoscience is a common topic in boosters’ discussions of public
scientific literacy; the general theme is that if only people knew more
about science they would be more likely to recognize and reject pseudo-
science and bad science. This theme appeared in some of the more
extreme deficit model positions in Chapters 4 and 7, but also shows
in more reasoned statements; in one example, science communicators
Richard Hayes and Daniel Grossman worry that many people ‘accept
pseudo-scientific claims’, which they describe as ‘assertions presented
in scientific language but lacking evidence and plausibility’ (p. ix). The
problem is that all too many popular science texts fit this description,
presenting findings without providing evidence or explaining how the
evidence supports the conclusions. In fact, research shows that ‘report-
ing scientific findings with little evidence of their merit has become a
frequent practice’ (Rowan, p. 205). How, then, are readers to distinguish
reports of valid scientific research from scientific-sounding hype?
   Hayes and Grossman are part of a growing chorus calling for the
public to be more critical of pseudoscience and bad science, but for
people to be more critical of these, they need to be more critical of sci-
ence in general. If people reject pseudoscience not because they have a
generally critical stance toward information presented as scientific but,
instead, simply because they believe statements by established scientists
about what does and doesn’t count as the real thing, then the point
has been missed entirely. As Rowan argues, ‘Readers need evidence if
they are to make their own judgments, and they need writers to teach
them how to evaluate the evidence. When they are not given evidence,
audiences have no guidance on what to think if conflicting findings
are reported 6 months from now’ (p. 206). A CUSP approach to science
communication includes the evidence and the reasons for interpreting
it in particular ways, and readers are invited to think for themselves
about whether the claims are credible.
   To illustrate why this is so important, I want to shift briefly away from
ecology to a topic very much in the news these days: the purported
link between vaccinations and autism. The study that made the now-
debunked connection was published in The Lancet, a prestigious British
medical journal, and Andrew Wakefield, the lead author on the paper,
was, at the time, a doctor and medical researcher working at the Royal
Free & University College Medical School (now the University College
London Medical School) in London. A PAST model text reporting on
the study by Wakefield et al. would not distinguish between their paper
and others published in The Lancet. A CUSP model text, however, would
ask critical questions: What was the sample size in the study? Was the
                                Conclusion: Toward a New Social Contract   165


methodology sound? Were there confirming studies? How preliminary
or how substantial were the findings? Did the authors have conflicts
of interest? The fact that Wakefield’s claims were later found to be
fraudulent and his medical license revoked is beside the point. Readers
learning of the study in 1998 could not have known this would happen;
what they could have done, and some did, was ask critical questions
about the study. Even before Wakefield’s conflict of interest became
known, or even had he not had a conflict of interest, this kind of critical
stance could have shown problems in his study, or at least encouraged
writers and readers to wait for confirming studies.
   Physician Atul Gawande writes that an article on vaccines and autism
in The Best American Science Writing 2006 ‘sounds a serious alarm for
scientists about the depth of public mistrust in our authority and expla-
nations’ (p. ix), and yet Wakefield’s work may not have been trusted in
the first place were it not sanctioned by that authority. Furthermore, as
Doubleday argues, ‘The dissemination of popularized versions of science
that cannot be evaluated by publics encourages a credulity, which opens
the door to public acceptance of “new mysticisms clothed in apparently
scientific jargon”’ (p. 29). Wakefield’s work has been denounced, but its
proponents continue to clothe it in scientific jargon, drawing around it
the mantle of a trusted discourse to continue, promoting the misinfor-
mation campaign he began. CUSP, not blind faith in pronouncements
of medical and scientific experts, is needed to strip away that mantle.
   In the interest of promoting a real criticism of science, one that does
strip away the mantle protecting unethical and ethical science alike,
I have argued that we need to stop chasing the elusive grail of pure sci-
ence and technocratic certainty, and accept realities about how science
works and about the contingent nature of the knowledge it produces.
Popular science writing can help effect this societal shift from the tra-
ditional social contract to a new one that focuses on science-in-society
and values socially robust knowledge. As Chapter 2 explained, popular
science texts have a powerful discursive role in helping shape public
conversations about science. As Devitt points out, ‘to alter the situation,
the genre must be altered, and to alter the genre, the situation must be
altered’ (p. 23). To shift the conversation in the ways described above,
popular science writers need to do the following.

• Adopt a Realist–Skeptical construct of science and look at how it
  really works, including an honest appraisal of its cultural embed-
  dedness and its material interests. Understanding the context gives
  nonspecialists a position from which they can ask critical questions.
166   Communicating Popular Science


• Respect readers and their place in a deliberative democracy by engag-
  ing with them as intelligent, engaged interlocutors whose interest in
  science, while different from that of specialists, is no less important
  or valid.
• Present science as one voice—an important one, but not the only
  one—in science-related deliberations. This includes having realistic
  expectations of what science can and cannot do.


As noted, these stances not only value the ‘public’ part of public interest
writing, they also are good for science itself; that is, a Realist–Skeptical
construct of science removes it from the pedestal onto which scientism
places it, and, in the process, creates a more realistic perception among
readers of how science works and what it offers.
   Some scholars are skeptical of whether popular science writing can
meet these goals. Priest, for example, observes that science writers, like
scientists, often make ‘a naïve set of assumptions that ignores the fact
that policy decisions involve priorities, values and ethical considera-
tions that lie entirely outside (narrowly defined) science’ (p. 226). Such
skepticism is reasonable, given that, in the words of another rhetorician
of science, many science writers do ‘see themselves as in charge of fill-
ing the gap between science and the public’, and therefore ‘share some
of the misconceptions about popular science’ that lead to PAST model
communication (Charney, ‘Introduction’, p. 5).
   However, this book shows that while many popular science writers
identify themselves as boosters, others argue that scientific knowledge
should be approached with an open-minded skepticism that sees sci-
ence not just in terms of knowledge but also in terms of knowledge-
making and knowledge-using contexts. Analyses of popular science
texts show that science can, in fact, be presented in a way that does
justice to the complexities of the issues while also making them acces-
sible to nonspecialist readers. In the process, CUSP model texts do not
take scientific claims as sacred truth, but as what they are—claims—and
assess them using various factors. Specifically:


• because a CUSP approach acknowledges that scientific knowledge
  varies in how certain it is, CUSP model texts consider factors such
  as the degree of consensus in the scientific community, the stability
  of the claim over time and how ‘hot’ or ‘cold’ the science is, and
  the degree to which it has been subjected to critical scrutiny by the
  relevant scientific community
                               Conclusion: Toward a New Social Contract   167


• because a CUSP approach acknowledges that science is not an iso-
  lated endeavor, but part of the society that creates and supports it,
  CUSP model texts consider issues such as funding sources and their
  potential to affect research, as well as social factors and disciplinary
  biases that might come into play
• because this approach acknowledges that understandings of nature
  are filtered through technologies, and that these technologies are
  human inventions, CUSP model texts ask questions about the effects
  of the tools being used, including cognitive tools (such as theories or
  measurement systems)
• because a CUSP approach acknowledges that scientific claims can
  have consequences far beyond the laboratory or the field, CUSP
  model texts consider how scientific knowledge is used, or likely to
  be used, in other social arenas—political, medical, technological,
  mercantile, and so on; they follow David Miller’s argument that we
  should ‘regard ideas as tools with which social groups may seek to
  achieve their purposes in particular situations’ (quoted in Taylor,
  p. 18) and look as critically at those purposes as we do at the ideas
  themselves.

  In this book I have attempted to elucidate strategies that CUSP-model
texts use to meet these goals, and to develop an analytic framework1
and vocabulary for recognizing and talking about them. Overall, I have
argued that popular science writing can help foster CUSP because it
influences what people know about science and what they know about
science-in-society; how people view science and how they think science
should be viewed; how people view themselves in relation to science,
including what kinds of involvement they consider appropriate; how
people think about science’s responsibilities toward society; and how
people think about society’s responsibilities toward science.
  The specific strategies enumerated in Chapters 5–9 can work together
in texts to encourage the development of a new social contract. Michael
Gibbons, whose 1999 Nature article titled ‘Science’s new social contract
with society’ I have drawn on throughout this book, argues that con-
textualization of science must involve scientists themselves and that the
requisite changes in scientific culture cannot be effected by bureaucratic
change or by communications specialists. He says specifically that ‘con-
textualization is not a public relations exercise’ and that is also cannot
be accomplished ‘by asking journalists to develop popular accounts
of the significance of research’ (C84). While I agree that the culture
of science can only change from within, I think that popular science
168   Communicating Popular Science


writing can play a role as it gives researchers new ways to understand
their own and their colleagues’ work in a social context. Furthermore,
Gibbons also says that socially robust knowledge ‘cannot arise at one
specific site, or out of the views of one scientific discipline or group of
highly respected researchers’, but, instead, ‘must emerge from bringing
together the many different “knowledge dimensions” involved’ (C83).
The latter is precisely the role that CUSP model popular science texts
can and do play in forging new discursive pathways for making socially
robust meanings out of scientific knowledge.


Engaging Larger Conversations

A secondary goal for this book has been to integrate insights from soci-
ology, philosophy, history, and rhetoric of science in order to develop
an analytic vocabulary for furthering CUSP via science popularization.
In the process, I have tried not to make pronouncements on findings
in those fields, but to show ways that rhetoric can complement existing
lines of research. In that spirit, I think that STS scholarship might take
a few things from this book.
   First, the book offers an enriched understanding of how power imbal-
ances between different arenas of civil society are created or reduced
via discourse. Calsamiglia describes popular science writing in terms of
what discourse analysts would call ‘the canonical situation of asymme-
try between interlocutors, the latter typical of the relationship between
those who know and those who do not’ (p. 142). In looking at one such
situation, perhaps my work here will have use for scholars considering
other such asymmetrical rhetorical situations.
   In addition, I have tried to provide readers with insights into how
rhetoric of science can complement work done in other STS fields, espe-
cially communication studies. Where communication scholars look for
macroscale trends, rhetoricians and writing studies scholars can focus
on microscale strategies, as I have done here. So, where scholars like
Nisbet study ‘the measurement of media trends and…the observation of
any influence’ (p. 43), my focus is on the finer-grained details of specific
textual strategies and how they contribute to creating the definitions
and attitudes that Nisbet tracks.
   Finally, I hope this book has shown how Broks’ CUSP model of sci-
ence popularization opens up the possibility of science communication
that promotes conversations between different areas of society and that
allows different knowledge systems—episteme and doxa—to be in com-
munication with each other.
                                Conclusion: Toward a New Social Contract   169


Escape From the Science–Society Dualism

In Chapter 2, I noted that rhetoric is, above all, pragmatic. This is espe-
cially so given my choice to use Richards’ definition of rhetoric as ‘the
study of miscommunication and its remedies’ (Richards and Constable,
p. 3), and my goal of using rhetorical theory to understand and help
solve the communication problem of deficit model science populariza-
tion. It is pragmatic, too, in that pragmatism ‘offers a method of moving
beyond dualism, recognizing that a third principle can lead thinkers out
of the traps of ideological narrowness, meaningless debate, and fatalism’
(Roskelly and Ronald, p. 25).
   Moving from PAST to CUSP takes us out of the public-versus-science
division that even the PEST model upholds and into a new area, the
public space in which science happens and in which people, includ-
ing scientists, have many roles. It does this because, in the CUSP view,
nonscientist involvement matters to the extent that the science pertains
to the public, and scientific involvement matters to the extent that it
is relevant to the problem. Focusing on CUSP—an area where we see
not deficits, but shared problem solving—lets us get beyond measuring
what various actors lack (e.g., nonscientists who don’t understand sci-
ence, scientists whose understanding of risk is too narrowly focused on
quantitative risk assessment, and so on) and instead ask what each party
can bring to the discussion.
   By applying the CUSP model to popular science writing, this book has
attempted to demonstrate its robustness and its practical value as a third
point of view, one that can serve to break us out of the science-society
dualism, to help us to understand that all discussions about science are
about science-in-society, and to address them in that light.

1 Popular Science Writing: Problems and Potential
1. Some people refer to this as ‘PUS’, or Public Understanding of Science.
   I use PAST instead because I don’t think the ‘understanding’ part of ‘PUS’ is
   accurate, making the acronym misleading. ‘Public Appreciation’ more closely
   represents the deficit model’s goals.
2. Contrary to popular belief that learning in one domain applies in all others,
   people exercise expert rationality only in knowledge domains with which
   they are familiar. For example, media scholars Lee Wilkins and Phillip
   Patterson describe how in unfamiliar circumstances even scientists and engi-
   neers perceive risk through the lay rationality lens. The belief that scientists
   think differently than other people outside their areas of specialization is
   known as the fallacy of critical thinking, which science education professor
   Jonathan Osborne describes as ‘the fallacious assumption that mere contact
   with science will imbue a sense of critical rationality by some unseen proc-
   ess of osmosis’ (p. 175). He cites studies showing specifically that scientists
   do not apply scientific reasoning in other contexts, and also notes that this
   lack of transfer of knowledge or skills from one domain to another is com-
   mon: ‘The notion that science develops generalizable, transferable skills is
   also an assumption questioned by the body of research which suggests that
   people’s use of knowledge and reasoning is situated within a context’ and that
   ‘detached knowledge is of little use to individuals until it has been reworked
   into a form which is understood by the user’ (p. 175). Less tactfully, historian
   of science I. Bernard Cohen suggests that the failure of the critical thinking
   fallacy ‘may easily be demonstrated by examining carefully the lives of scien-
   tists outside the laboratory’ (quoted in Gregory and Miller, p. 65).



2 Theoretical and Analytical Framework
1. This chapter is not intended to offer a detailed review of the scholarly lit-
   eratures on the nature of science, but ‘to derive from their work a model of
   science’ (Ziman, p. 10) that represents current understandings in Science and
   Technology Studies (STS) of what science is and how it works.
2. Granted, some science scholars do appear to deny the role of material reality.
   For example, rhetorician Alan Gross caused a flurry of scandal in the 1990s
   by claiming, in The Rhetoric of Science (1990), that it’s not possible to have
   science ‘without remainder’ (p. 33), that is that there is no essential core of
   science that cannot be explained rhetorically. Some critics read this to mean
   that Gross and, by extension, rhetoricians of science were taking an extreme
   relativist stance in which all facets of reality are created by human under-
   standing. Whether these interpretations of Gross’s 1990 work are correct is
   less important than the fact that radical relativism has no serious defenders

                                       171
172   Notes


   in STS. Even Gross moderated back from his earlier stance in Starring the Text:
   The Place of Rhetoric in Science Studies, his 2006 reworking of The Rhetoric of
   Science.
3. ‘Genre’ has meant different things at different times and when used by differ-
   ent communities of scholars. For example, in traditional literary criticism, the
   concept of genre was used to create taxonomies of texts based on their formal
   characteristics. In this conception, a genre was a particular kind of text, and
   texts could be sorted into genre categories, such as ‘the sonnet’ or ‘the short
   story’. In contrast, rhetoric considers genres in terms of the relationship
   between utterances or texts and the contexts in which they happen. This view
   can be found in Aristotle’s division of speeches 2500 years ago into foren-
   sic, epideictic, and deliberative types. While this tripartite division remains
   useful—in fact, it is the focus of Chapter 8, ‘Rhetorical Orientation: Inviting
   Reader Engagement’—genre theory has grown both broader and richer
   since Aristotle’s initial conceptualization and has come to mean something
   a lot more complicated and interesting than it used to mean. Specifically,
   rhetorical genre theory looks at genres in terms of how they are constitutive
   of the situations in which they are used.
4. These models were ‘emergent’ (Atkinson, p. 60); that is, rather than specifying
   them in advance and seeking each in my study texts, I developed the models
   iteratively over time as I drew meaning from the texts and analyzed and
   adjusted those meanings as new data suggested new interpretations.
5. Although a piece’s being anthologized does not guarantee its being read, the
   pieces in these collections generally come from high-circulation publications,
   and their inclusion in these popular collections further increases their audi-
   ences. This variety of publications, from newspapers to literary journals, also
   means the collections provide a useful cross-section of popular science writ-
   ing, from very short articles to long essays, and include texts written by both
   insiders (scientists writing about their own work) and outsiders (those writing
   about work that is not their own).
6. I am not calling these research areas ‘ecological’ simply out of whimsy. The
   list is a sampling of the areas of research done by the 125 faculty members in
   the University of California Davis’s Graduate Group in Ecology.
7. This selection method resembles that used by various rhetorical theorists
   and historians [such as Bazerman, Condit et al., Connors, Journet, Myers
   (Writing), and Prior] as they work with a large body of material.

1. This chapter is indebted to Dwight Atkinson’s rhetorical and linguistic analy-
   sis in Scientific Discourse in Sociohistorical Context: The Philosophical Transactions
   of the Royal Society of London, 1675–1975. Atkinson provides a detailed look

   the backbone of this chapter. Parts of this trajectory are also summarized by
   historian Bernadette Bensaude-Vincent in ‘A Genealogy of the Increasing Gap
   Between Science and the Public’. Bensaude-Vincent describes how the gap
   between science and the public has broadened over the last few centuries and,
   in the process, has changed qualitatively.

5 Boundary Work: Presenting Science in Context
1. Of course, demarcation is real. As Taylor points out, ‘we can tell where sci-
   ence ends and society begins, and vice versa. We do it every day. Few of us,
   for example, expect, less yet desire, the next Nobel Prize in physics to be
   awarded to an avant-garde performance artist’ (p. 15, emphasis original).
   However, he also cautions that overestimating the strength of demarcation
   can be misleading, as ‘the distinctions between the spheres are more a matter
   of relative emphasis on particular pragmatic rules or strategies and not a mat-
   ter of conceptual distinctions’ (p. 125). Given that the tendency for popular
   science texts to overplay the importance of the differences, my task is to show
   why the similarities should be given more emphasis than they are.
2. See, for example, Friedman and Richter, ‘Relationship between Conflicts of
   Interest and Research Results’ in the Journal of General Internal Medicine; Gennaro

   and Tomatis, ‘Business Bias: How Epidemiologic Studies May Underestimate or

   Industry and Conflicting Interests in Cancer Research’ in the American Journal

   and Sismondo, ‘Pharmaceutical Company Funding and Its Consequences:

   DSM-III: The Influence of a Charter Document in Psychiatry’, McCarthy looks
   at ‘how this 500-page classification system of psychiatric disorders shapes real-
   ity’ for a psychiatrist as it ‘shapes what she knows about mental illness and
   how she communicates that knowledge’, including her ‘diagnostic work: her
   information gathering, her analyses, and her writing’ (p. 359). In ‘Cultural
   Studies and Critical Science’ Herndl explains that because medicine is prone
   to a kind of reduction and reification (p. 75), ‘dominant medical practice seeks

   they claim epistemic exceptionalism for science in also stating that ‘Science

6 Expertise: Broadening the Scope of Participation
1. I realize that in some areas of science, for example high energy physics, there’s
   no such thing as lay expertise. Even there, though, members of the nonscien-
   tific public may have informed opinions about issues affecting the scientific
   practices. As Ihde argued at the public hearing about the Shoreham nuclear
   power plant, one does not have to be a nuclear physicist to have an opinion
   about living next to a reactor.
2. This differs from Collins and Evans’ definition, which says that all contribu-
   tory experts, by definition, are also interactive experts (p. 37). I disagree; there
   is plenty of evidence to show that a scientist, for example, may be very good
   at explaining his or her research to peers, but not to others.
3. Other writers in the Field Guide who say to look for a range of perspectives,
   then limit that range to scientists only, include Hotz (p. 60), Hall (who catego-
   rizes bioethicists as ‘nonexperts’ on page 201), and Chang (p. 211). McFarling
   talks about climate change discussions as having a substantial political
   dimension, but suggests that writers be wary of all experts except scientists
   working for the government (pp. 246–7).
4. Although most of the articles I examine in ‘Part II: Applications’ exemplify
   CUSP model strategies, analyzing Canby’s article by itself does not show the
   value of including local expertise as well as a contrast with Quammen’s article
   does. The contrast is especially interesting given how similar the two pieces
   are: each author writes about traveling with a scientific expedition through
   the Nouabalé-Ndoki National Park in the Republic of Congo, and each expe-
   dition included an ecologist leader, another scientist or two, and a group of
   the local people called the Bayaka.
5. Eliding indigenous expertise is a bizarre oversight, especially from an author
   who travels extensively and writes, in other pieces, about many kinds of
   knowledge. Erasing local knowledge is also counterproductive to the goals
   of the CUSP movement because it shrinks the frame a CUSP model seeks to
   expand, reifying the boundary between scientists and other people instead of
   showing that many kinds of knowledge have value and can work together.


7 Ethos: Establishing Relationships with Readers
1. For a fascinating discussion of how expertise has taken the spotlight,
   see Carolyn Miller’s ‘Expertise and Agency: Transformations of Ethos in Human-
   Computer Interaction’. Miller notes that ethos sometimes ‘allies itself strongly
176   Notes


   with logos’ and in other cases ‘with pathos’ (p. 199), and describes today’s tech-
   nical ethos in terms of ‘the transformation of ethos into logos’ (p. 203) such
   that ‘in a technical discourse, a discourse that banks on logos, expertise stands in
   for ethos’ (p. 205, emphasis original). Furthermore, ‘Rational ethos…focuses our
   attention on the ‘knowledge-base’ that authorizes claims’ (p. 205).
2. Both ‘ethos’ and ‘persona’ have been used in rhetorical scholarship for hun-
   dreds of years, and ‘persona’ is sometimes offered as a Latin equivalent for
   the older Greek term. However, as Roger D. Cherry points out, the two terms
   come from different traditions and ‘therefore provide different (but com-
   plementary) perspectives on self-representation’ (p. 385). Cherry describes
   persona as ‘an intentional “mask” a writer adopts in the written text’ (p. 393).
   He further describes persona in terms of how writers benefit from ‘fulfilling
   or creating a certain role (or roles) in the discourse community in which they
   are operating’ (p. 399). Thus, persona has to do with how the writer adapts
   his or her self-presentation to suit a particular audience.
3. Again, this is fine with cold science—a writer does not need to evaluate the
   claim that a falling body in a vacuum accelerates at 9.8 meters per second
   squared—but not with hot science.
4. Gregory and Miller note that scholars unfamiliar with media studies (no doubt
   including some rhetoricians) ‘often end up attributing characteristics to science-
   in-the-media that are simply characteristics of the media’ (p. 105). This is a good
   point. However, my goal is not to say how popular science writing is or isn’t simi-
   lar to other kinds of writing, but, instead, to say what I see happening in the genre.
   Whether it is happening because of media industry pressure is another issue.
5. I look only at how writers create ethos in texts, without considering factors
   outside the text. Some of the reader’s impression of the writer does come from
   factors outside the text, such as the writer’s reputation or the reader’s trust in
   the publication where the text appears. These ‘atechnic’ (‘non-artistic’ because
   not in the text itself) factors, while important, are outside of the writer’s con-
   trol (at least in any given text) and therefore not part of what I address. In
   other words, although these factors do matter, if the writer does not allude to
   them in the text, they do not contribute to the ethos as presented to readers.
6. On the practical level, she also notes the connection between growing public
   concern about an issue ‘and a concomitant shift in government research
   funds’ for studying that issue (p. 52), a recognition of the material depend-
   ence of science on its funders.
7. Interestingly, this technique mirrors early science writing when writers
   were concerned with allowing readers to be virtual witnesses and would
   offer ‘detail…piled on detail so that the reader may re-experience the event’
   (Harmon and Gross, p. 19).
8. Curious if such databases require paid access, I looked up a few using the
   search phrase ‘database protein interaction’. I found two dozen; of the ten I
   checked, nine were open access.



8 Rhetorical Orientations: Inviting Reader Engagement
1. Although one prose orientation is often stronger than the others in a given
   genre, most texts include a mixture of all three; a grant proposal, for example,
                                                                         Notes   177


   may present evidence intended to persuade readers of some fact while it also
   invokes values such as those named in the grant agency’s mission statement.
   Obviously, some genres offer more flexibility than others in this regard—a
   forensic wedding toast would be odd indeed—but the important point is
   that rhetorical orientations, while often associated with particular genres, are
   not strictly tied to those genres, nor are most genres limited in the rhetorical
   orientations they can use in pursuing particular purposes.
2. Some scholars (including Fahnestock and Secor; and Grant-Davie) add
   another stasis, ‘Cause’, between the second and third stases. I use four instead
   of five because, for the purposes of analyzing popular science texts, there is
   not need to separate out causes; in this context, they can be subsumed under
   the first stasis.
3. A good example of affordances comes from the way that development of
   standardized measurements in the late eighteenth and early nineteenth cen-
   turies actually narrowed the domain of science. As Atkinson explains, the rise
   of quantitative methods, including the creation of ‘standardized measurement
   systems’ (p. 155), altered what people looked at in science by encouraging
   practitioners to focus on problems that were amenable to these methods: ‘The
   outcome was as much a natural world refashioned to fit the new methods of
   science—partly in the sense that phenomena unable to be so investigated were
   de facto ruled out of court—as science refashioned to better fit nature’ (p. 156).


9 Technocracy and Democracy: Talking about Risk
1. The subject of risk communication in popular science texts could itself fill a
   book; this chapter can only address, in broad strokes, the major ways that risk
   communication issues converge with the framework developed in this book
   and suggest further avenues for exploration.
2. Another factor is familiarity; people tend to be more tolerant of risks to which
   they are accustomed. As this factor is not within a writer’s purview, I don’t
   address it.
3. For more nuance, see Adams, ‘Not 100% Sure? The “Public” Understanding of
   Risk’. His diagram on ‘Risk acceptability and risk amplification: what kills you
   matters’ breaks down risks into voluntary, indifferent, and imposed, where
   indifferent refers to natural phenomena, such as earthquakes. Imposed breaks
   down further into ‘benign’, ‘profit motivated’ (such as genetically-modified
   organisms), and ‘malicious’ (such as terrorism) (p. 96).
4. See, for example, Irwin’s chapter on ‘Risk, Science, and Public Communication’
   in the Handbook of Public Communication of Science and Technology.
5. For example, many writers make a nod to the negative consequences of
   scientific endeavors, but then dive back into positives. These include Gleick
   (‘Introduction’), Kolata, Ferris (‘Foreword’ 2001), and Dyson. The editors’
   introduction to the Field Guide says that good popular science writing ‘dis-
   cusses safeguarding the public from the risks of the new knowledge and talks
   about the cost of Big Science and who has to pay for it’ (Blum et al., ‘Editor’s
   Note’, p. viii) but, as in the other chapters mentioned here, they do not dwell
   on this point, or return to it in detail later.
6. Such claims belie the fact that harm is often brought to official attention by
   the people experiencing it. Sociologist Phil Brown has written extensively on
178   Notes


   how communities act on their own behalf; see, for example, his 1992 and
   1997 articles ‘Popular Epidemiology and Toxic Waste Contamination: Lay
   and Professional Ways of Knowing’ and ‘Popular Epidemiology Revisited’,
   and his 1997 book with Edwin J. Mikkelson, No Safe Place: Toxic Waste,
   Leukemia, and Community Action.
7. Revkin’s work actually raised a conundrum for me. In a piece published five
   years later, ‘Tackling the climate communication challenge’, Revkin takes a
   much more CUSP-oriented stance in which he talks about how those causing
   the problems are insulated from the consequences of their actions. For exam-
   ple, he notes that ‘populations generating the most heat-trapping emissions
   are mostly separated in space and time from the communities or ecosystems
   most exposed to potentially heightened risks of flooding, drought, and other
   climate-related hazards’ (Revkin, ‘Tackling’, p. 143) and that the wealth that
   comes from bad practices ‘can insulate emitters from some climate-related
   risks’ (Revkin, ‘Tackling’, p. 143). The problem was that I knew this, and oth-
   ers texts show more nuanced or CUSP-oriented positions than those in the
   Field Guide. To only present the authors in light of what was in the Field Guide
   seemed unfair to them, as it does not do justice to the complexity of their
   thought. At the same time, reading all the reflective pieces by the authors in
   the Field Guide would have taken me a way from what I was trying to deter-
   mine—not what each of these authors thinks in general, but how popular
   science writing is presented in a teaching text.


10 Conclusion: Toward a New Social Contract
1. The framework developed in this book enables and suggests further research.
   For example, it could be applied in reception studies (looking at how read-
   ers understand and interpret popular science texts) and could be adapted for
   looking at science writing programs; according to Peter Weingart, science
   writing is still being affected by deficit model thinking, with institutional fac-
   tors, such as training for journalists, encouraging writers to choose the roles
   of science translators and boosters rather than investigators or skeptics. The
   framework could be used to examine the programs’ curricula and the model
   texts they use in order to understand their theoretical commitments. It could
   also be used for more focused research on practitioner perspectives, includ-
   ing empirical research such as interviews with and surveys of popular science
   writers.
